# Evolution Picture Book / Sheet14 (copied from Google Sheets, 2026-11-27)
R1: Species | Nervous System | Digestive System | Respiratory System | Circulatory System | Reproductive System | Excretory System
R2: 1. Earliest Cephalopods | (H) Basic molluscan nerve ring and paired ganglia controlling body functions. (I) Enlarged brain and giant nerve fibers for fast signal conduction, coordinating agile swimming and tentacle movement; image-forming eyes for predation (advanced among molluscs). | (H) Complete gut with mouth, esophagus, and radula from molluscan ancestors for scraping food. (I) Powerful chitinous beak and jaws to tear prey (predatory adaptation); a crop and stomach with digestive caecum for extra cellular digestion. Possibly an ink sac evolved for predator evasion (unique to cephalopods). | (H) Pair of gills in mantle cavity like other aquatic molluscs for gas exchange. (I) Muscular mantle pumping water over gills, enabling active respiration and jet propulsion. Developed siphuncle in shell for buoyancy control, freeing mantle for strong jet-driven breathing/outflow. | (H) Dorsal heart and blood vessels from common molluscan design. (I) Only mollusk lineage with a closed circulatory system (blood fully contained in vessels) and multiple hearts (two gill hearts plus one systemic) to support high activity​en.wikipedia.org. High-pressure blood flow improved oxygen delivery for predation. | (H) Separate sexes with external fertilization ancestral to molluscs (spawning eggs and sperm into water). (I) Evolution of internal fertilization via spermatophores (packages of sperm) to ensure fertilization in open water; larger yolky eggs laid in protective gelatinous clutches, reducing drift and predation. | (H) Nephridia (“kidneys”) filtering blood and releasing ammonia into mantle cavity water (typical aquatic waste removal). (I) Enhanced excretion to handle high-protein diet: efficient ammonia diffusion at gills. No major new organs, but likely higher renal output to eliminate metabolic wastes from active lifestyle.
R3: 2. Euthycarcinoids | (H) Arthropod ventral nerve cord with segmented ganglia and simple brain (control of appendages and senses). (I) Well-developed compound eyes and antennae for both aquatic and terrestrial sensing (facilitating amphibious foraging); improved coordination for walking on land in short bouts (integrating leg movements and balance). | (H) Straight alimentary canal with foregut (lined in chitin) and hindgut as in primitive arthropods. (I) Mandibulate mouthparts (jaws derived from appendages) for biting/chewing microbial mats or detritus; digestive ceca to aid in breaking down tough plant detritus, supporting early land herbivory/omnivory. | (H) Aquatic gills on appendages or along body (like crustacean gills) for underwater breathing. (I) Ability to breathe air briefly: possibly retained water around gills under a closeable cover or kept gills moist, enabling gas exchange in air (analogous to modern isopods). First arthropods to make short terrestrial forays. | (H) Dorsal tubular heart and open hemolymph circulation (like ancestral arthropods) with hemocyanin to carry oxygen. (I) Perhaps slightly higher hemocyanin levels or heart rate to supply oxygen during excursions on land (supporting muscle activity out of water), but overall no closed vessels (still an open system). | (H) External fertilization in water (eggs and sperm released in moist environments, as ancestral aquatic arthropods). (I) Transition toward land reproduction: eggs laid in damp mud or water’s edge to stay moist; tougher egg coatings to resist desiccation compared to fully aquatic eggs, foreshadowing terrestrial life cycle needs. | (H) Coxal glands (primitive excretory organs at leg bases) filtering hemolymph and excreting ammonia, like ancestral arthropods. (I) Partial shift to uric acid or less toxic waste when on land to avoid ammonia buildup (akin to later terrestrial arthropods). Still largely ammonia when in water, but some capacity for water retention during land travel.
R4: 3. Jawless Fish | (H) Dorsal hollow nerve cord and three-part brain from chordate ancestors (forebrain, midbrain, hindbrain). (I) Enclosed brain within a cartilaginous skull for protection; developed sense organs feeding into brain (image-forming paired eyes, inner ear balancing with 2 semicircular canals, olfactory bulbs) for active navigation and survival. | (H) Filter-feeding pharynx with gill slits from proto-chordates. (I) Muscular pharynx pump to draw in water/food, using gills now exclusively for respiration and mouth for feeding. Appearance of a true liver and pancreatic tissue (digestive glands) for improved nutrient processing in the vertebrate lineage. | (H) Multiple gill slits along the throat for gas exchange (as in ancestral chordates). (I) Gills repurposed solely for respiration (no longer for feeding) with gill filaments and rich blood supply; a muscular gill pouch system (in lampreys) or gill pouches pumping water, greatly improving oxygen uptake for a more active lifestyle than filter feeders. | (H) Basic circulation with ventral heart tube pumping blood forward (proto-heart). (I) True two-chambered heart (atrium and ventricle) evolving to push blood to gills then body; blood cells with hemoglobin appear, vastly increasing oxygen transport. This closed-loop cardiovascular innovation supports higher activity than in non-vertebrate chordates. | (H) External spawning in water – eggs and sperm released into the environment, as with amphibian-like reproduction inherited from ancestors. (I) More yolk in eggs to nourish developing larvae (e.g., lamprey eggs develop into filter-feeding larvae), and a clear metamorphosis from larval stage to adult (an extended developmental strategy unique to vertebrates). | (H) Diffusion of ammonia across gills and skin (sufficient for small ancestors). (I) Primitive kidneys (pronephros/mesonephros) with segmented nephrons that filter blood and remove waste into the cloaca. These vertebrate kidneys allow regulation of salt and water, an advance over the simple diffusion and flame cells of invertebrate precursors.
R5: 4. Bony Fish | (H) Vertebrate CNS with brain regions (cerebrum, cerebellum, etc.) similar to earlier fish. (I) Enlarged optic tectum for acute vision, and cerebellum for precise motor control of fins. Enhanced integration of sensory input (sight, lateral line, smell) supports complex behaviors like schooling and predator evasion in diverse aquatic niches. | (H) Complete digestive tract with stomach, intestine, liver, pancreas as in basal jawed fish. (I) Moveable jaws with calcified teeth for grabbing and chewing prey (first time jaws used for true biting). Many bony fish evolved a protrusible jaw for suction feeding (allowing them to slurp prey). Also, pyloric caeca (blind sacs) off the intestine increase nutrient absorption. | (H) Gill-based respiration with gill arches and filaments. (I) Bony operculum covering the gills, which pumps water over gill surfaces, allowing breathing even when stationary (major efficiency gain over sharks). Some developed a swim bladder that doubles as a lung in low-oxygen water, an air-filled organ homologous to lungs, allowing gulping air to survive oxygen-poor environments. | (H) Single-loop circulation (heart → gills → body) from ancestral fish. (I) Stronger two-chambered heart with an S-shaped ventricle for more force; addition of elastic bulbus arteriosus to smooth blood flow. Erythrocytes with hemoglobin are standard. Some bony fish later evolved accessory hearts in tail for venous return (e.g., in eels – a minor circulatory innovation). | (H) External fertilization by ancestral pattern (spawning): females release eggs and males shed sperm over them in water. (I) Complex reproductive strategies: many bony fish produce thousands of eggs with minimal yolk (broadcast spawning), while others evolved demersal sticky eggs or even mouth-brooding and nest guarding (parental care as a new behavior). Some lineages even developed internal fertilization and live birth (e.g., guppies), showing early viviparity. | (H) Kidneys and gills jointly manage waste (ammonia diffuses out at gills). (I) Advanced osmoregulation: marine bony fish drink seawater and excrete excess salt through gills; freshwater bony fish produce very dilute urine to expel water. Nitrogen waste is mostly ammonia (highly toxic but easily flushed in water) – an efficient strategy for aquatic life, with urea production in a few cases when needed (e.g., lungfish during drought).
R6: 5. Scorpions | (H) Arthropod nervous system (brain plus ventral nerve cord with segmental ganglia) inherited from chelicerate ancestors. (I) Fused ganglia forming a larger nerve mass in the cephalothorax for better integration of sensory input from multiple eyes and touch receptors. Specialized circuits control the pedipalps (pincers) and tail stinger with precise aim and timing during hunting/defense. | (H) Arthropod gut with foregut (esophagus, sucking stomach) and hindgut. (I) External digestion: scorpions secrete digestive enzymes onto prey, liquefying tissues to slurp up (pre-oral digestion adaptation). The muscular pharynx acts as a pump to suck in prey fluid. A large hepatopancreas (digestive gland) produces enzymes to break down the meal and also stores nutrients, an innovation supporting their feast-and-fast lifestyle. | (H) Book gills in aquatic ancestors (like horseshoe crabs). (I) Book lungs – internal stacked lamellae in abdominal sacs for air breathing, evolved from book gills to function in air. Four pairs of book lungs connect to outside via slit-like spiracles on the underside, enabling scorpions to live in oxygen-poor burrows. This was a key terrestrial adaptation, allowing gas exchange while conserving water. | (H) Open circulatory system with tubular heart and hemolymph, as in all arthropods. (I) Long dorsal heart with multiple ostia running through the abdomen, pushing hemolymph into sinuses including fine vessels in book lungs for gas exchange. Hemocyanin pigment carries oxygen. The system is efficient enough for a predatory but low-metabolic lifestyle, and scorpions can survive low oxygen by slowing circulation – an ancestral trait leveraged for terrestrial life. | (H) Internal fertilization via spermatophore (male deposits sperm packet on ground, then positions female to take it up) – present in early arachnids. (I) Viviparity (live birth): embryos develop inside the female, nourished by a “uterine milk” secretion. Young are born alive and crawl onto the mother’s back. This innovation (rare among invertebrates) gives the young a higher survival rate. Extended maternal care (carrying young until first molt) is also a new behavioral strategy improving offspring success. | (H) Malpighian tubules (excretory organs emptying into hindgut) from arachnid ancestors, removing wastes from hemolymph. (I) Very efficient water conservation: scorpions convert nitrogenous waste to insoluble compounds like guanine and uric acid, excreting a dry powdery feces with minimal water loss. This adaptation, along with a waxy cuticle, allows survival in deserts. They also have a rectal gland to reabsorb water, maximizing retention.
R7: Species | Endocrine System | Immune System | Integumentary System | Skeletal System | Muscular System | Sensory System
R8: 1. Earliest Cephalopods | (H) Neuroendocrine cells in brain controlling growth, development, and reproduction (as in other mollusks). (I) Specialized hormone systems for rapid body pattern change (e.g. chromatophore hormones for camouflage) and reproductive timing (optic gland secretion triggers reproductive maturation and death in modern octopus). These indicate early cephalopods already had complex hormonal control for short, intense life cycles. | (H) Innate immune responses with wandering amebocytes (blood cells) that phagocytose invaders, typical of invertebrates. (I) Possibly more robust wound-healing and skin lesion repair, given active predatory lifestyle (e.g. clot-forming cells to seal injuries). Cephalopods also have antimicrobial peptides in their skin mucus as a first defense. | (H) Soft epidermis covered by a protective shell-secreting mantle (inherited from molluscan forebears). (I) Chambered external shell providing physical protection and buoyancy control; shell was made of aragonite and partitioned by septa, an innovation to lighten body weight. The mantle could rapidly change skin texture and color via primitive chromatophores even in early forms (for camouflage and signaling – novel among mollusks). | (H) External calcium carbonate shell (from snail-like ancestors) as exoskeleton. (I) Siphuncle-bearing shell: a tube running through shell chambers to pump fluid/gas, allowing buoyancy regulation. Internal cartilaginous supports for brain (braincase) evolved to protect the large brain – a precursor to internal skeleton elements. Tentacles function as muscular hydrostats (no bones, but stiffened by muscular pressure), providing flexible “skeleton” for manipulation. | (H) Muscular foot inherited from mollusks (in cephalopods this became the arms and funnel). (I) Radial and circular mantle muscles enabling jet propulsion (a cephalopod innovation – the first jet-propelled locomotion in evolution). Extrinsic eye muscles to move their sophisticated eyes. Arm muscles with suckers for grasping prey, an all-new locomotive and manipulatory system distinct from the creeping foot of ancestors. | (H) Chemosensory cells and simple light sensors from ancestral mollusks. (I) Highly developed camera-type eyes (in Nautilus, a pinhole eye; in others, lens eyes rivaling vertebrates) for keen vision. Statocysts (balance organs) provide equilibrium in 3D space, critical for jet swimming. Acute chemoreceptors in arms to “taste” water and find food or mates. Though color-blind, they had polarization-sensitive vision and complex skin-light sensing, novel sensory capabilities for their time. They essentially pioneered many sensor innovations among invertebrates.
R9: 2. Euthycarcinoids | (H) Molting hormone (ecdysone) system to regulate exoskeleton shedding, as in all arthropods, and juvenile hormones controlling development. (I) Possibly timing of terrestrial forays linked to endocrine signals (e.g. hormonal response to drying habitats might trigger these arthropods to migrate or lay eggs). Overall, endocrine system remains basic and inherited, with no known special glands unique to them. | (H) Exoskeleton as first defense barrier; hemocyte-mediated wound healing and phagocytosis of pathogens (arthropod innate immunity). (I) Cuticular antibiotics: like many arthropods, they likely had waxes or phenolic compounds in cuticle that inhibit microbial growth (handy for partial terrestrial life). Any exposure to new terrestrial microbes might have driven slightly enhanced innate responses, but no evidence of adaptive immunity (only jawed vertebrates have that). | (H) Hard chitinous cuticle covering body and appendages, providing protection and preventing desiccation (a trait from arthropod ancestors). (I) More heavily sclerotized (hardened) tergites and limb surfaces to support body weight in gravity. Possibly a waxy layer on the cuticle to reduce water loss during excursions on land – an early step toward a fully water-resistant integument. The body segments were fairly uniform (minimal regional differentiation), but the terminal segment bore a distinctive paddle-like tail spine in some, perhaps aiding swimming – an innovation within the exoskeleton. | (H) Segmented exoskeleton with jointed appendages from early arthropods. (I) Limb specialization for walking on muddy land: sturdy, likely uniramous legs with stronger internal leg muscles and joints capable of lifting the body. Their endoskeletal elements were limited to small tendons/apodemes inside exoskeleton. No backbone, but the exoskeleton provided anchor points for muscles. They may represent a stem-lineage leading to arthropods with biramous limbs, showing experimental limb forms (paddle-like vs. walking limbs) as an evolutionary test of skeletal layouts. | (H) Segmental arthropod musculature (bands of striated muscle attaching to exoskeleton for each segment and joint). (I) Robust leg muscles to push against substrate for short bursts on land (needing more force than swimming). Perhaps differentiation of muscle fiber types: endurance muscles for sustained walking vs. quick twitch for swimming escapes. These creatures likely moved between water and land, so their muscular system had to accommodate both rowing in water and clambering over uneven terrestrial surfaces – a novel versatility at the time. | (H) Compound eyes for wide-field vision and motion detection, antennae for smell/touch – both inherited arthropod features. (I) Improved aerial vision calibration: eyes adapted to function both submerged and in air (focus may have been adjustable). Mechanosensory bristles on legs to detect vibrations on land (alerting to predators or prey out of water). They also left trackways, suggesting they could navigate exposed surfaces – implying their sensory systems could handle light and chemical cues in both environments. This dual-environment sensing was a new challenge that euthycarcinoids met with moderate success (precursor to insect land sensory mastery).
R10: 3. Jawless Fish | (H) Endocrine organs like thyroid (from endostyle of proto-chordates) and pituitary gland controlling homeostasis and growth. (I) Pituitary-hypothalamus axis well-developed, regulating complex life cycle (e.g., lamprey metamorphosis from larva to adult). Primitive adrenal-like cells (interrenal tissue) start producing corticosteroids for stress response, an early vertebrate endocrine innovation for adapting to variable environments (freshwater to ocean in some lampreys). | (H) Innate immune cells (phagocytes, inflammation) present as in all multicellular animals. (I) VLR-based adaptive immunity – jawless fish like lampreys evolved unique Variable Lymphocyte Receptors (LRR-based) that function analogously to antibodies. This is an alternative adaptive immune strategy, indicating the first appearance of specific immune memory in vertebrates. It’s a novel system (separate from the immunoglobulin-based immunity of jawed vertebrates) that provides targeted defense against pathogens, showing that even these early fish could “learn” to fight infections. | (H) Smooth scaleless skin (in modern lampreys/hagfish) or bony-plated skin (ostracoderms) inherited from early vertebrates, with mucous secretion to reduce drag and resist infection. (I) Dermal armor in many jawless fish: large bony plates and scales in the skin for protection, an innovation providing defense against predators. These bone scales (as in ostracoderms) were heavy but effective, shaping a form of “external skeleton.” Additionally, lampreys have keratin “teeth” in the mouth – a unique integumentary structure for latching onto prey. | (H) Notochord as primary axial support (from chordate ancestry), with rudimentary cartilage skull protecting brain. (I) Cranial cartilage and gill supports: the first skull (cranium) and gill arch cartilages form a head skeleton. Bony head shields and plate-like exoskeleton in ostracoderms, which acted as a hard protective skeleton outside the body. Tiny vertebral elements (arcualia) began to form around the notochord in tail regions – the origin of a vertebral column. These innovations stiffened the body for more powerful swimming and protected vital organs, setting the stage for later fully-formed internal skeletons. | (H) Segmental myomeres (muscle blocks) along the body, a feature from cephalochordate ancestors allowing lateral undulation. (I) W-shaped myomeres attached to flexible notochord, providing stronger, more controlled swimming motions than simpler V-shaped muscles. Development of specialized muscles for sucking (e.g., circular muscle in lamprey mouth to create suction on prey). In armored forms, muscles also had to attach to internal sites beneath dermal armor, leading to early tendons. These jawless fish could burrow (lamprey larvae) or swim powerfully – muscle adaptations for both behaviors show an increase in muscular versatility. | (H) Lateral line system along the body to detect water movements and vibrations (a primitive sense shared with ancestral fish). (I) Image-forming eyes with lens and retina – a big leap from light-sensitive spots in proto-chordates. Two semi-circular canals in inner ear for balance (improving 3D orientation). Single median nostril in lampreys for smell (not yet dual nostrils like jawed fish). Also, electroreception may have been present (some modern lampreys can detect weak electric fields). These sensory advances made jawless fish keen detectors of their environment, able to find prey and avoid predators in murky waters.
R11: 4. Bony Fish | (H) Endocrine glands (pituitary, thyroid, interrenal, gonads) producing hormones for metabolism, growth, and reproduction as in all vertebrates. (I) Refinement of endocrine control for osmoregulation (hormones like prolactin and cortisol manage salt balance in differing salinity environments). Also, sex hormones regulate complex spawning behaviors (timing migrations, coloration changes during mating seasons). Some bony fish later evolved endocrine-driven sex change (sequential hermaphroditism), a novel strategy in teleost fishes. | (H) Fully functional innate and adaptive immune system from early gnathostomes. (I) Immunoglobulin-based adaptive immunity appears – jawed fish were first to use antibodies and T-cell receptors via thymus and spleen. This is a major innovation, giving them robust defense and immunological memory. Additionally, bony fish possess skin mucus loaded with antibacterial substances, and some have lymphoid tissue in gills and gut, showing diversification of immune organs (e.g., kidney as a hematopoietic organ). | (H) Overlapping dermal scales covering the body, as inherited from ancestral osteichthyans (early scales were bony with enamel-like surface). (I) Lightweight, flexible cycloid or ctenoid scales (in advanced bony fish) that allow agility while still offering protection. A slimy mucus coat covers the scales, reducing drag and infection. Many bony fish also evolved fin rays (made of keratin-like protein) supporting their fins – an integumentary innovation that enables fine control of fin shape for maneuvering. Pigment cells in skin allow rapid color change for camouflage or signaling, more pronounced than in earlier fish. | (H) Internal bony endoskeleton (ossified bones) supporting body and fins, building on the cartilage template of ancestors. (I) Ossification – strong calcified bone throughout the skeleton (cranium, jaws, vertebrae, fin bones) giving better muscle attachment and protection. Development of specialized skeletal elements: jaw bones (premaxilla, maxilla, dentary) that allowed a formidable bite, and fin rays (dermal bony spines with webbing) for expanded fin surfaces. The shoulder girdle attached to skull (in most fish) to support pectoral fins, and symmetrical tail (homocercal tail in later teleosts) supported by modified tail bones to improve swimming efficiency – all new skeletal refinements. | (H) Robust segmented axial muscles for swimming (left-right contraction waves for forward thrust). (I) Muscles controlling new jaw mechanics (e.g., opercular and pharyngeal muscles to suck in prey). Extrinsic eye muscles for rapid eye movements (tracking prey). Red muscle (aerobic, for cruising) and white muscle (anaerobic, for burst speed) differentiation is pronounced – an adaptation for sustained vs. burst swimming. In many ray-finned fish, fine muscles at fin bases allow sophisticated fin control (for braking, hovering, etc.), which is a marked contrast to the limited fin motion in earlier fish. | (H) Acute vision with color detection (in shallow-water species) as in other fish, and lateral line for water currents detection. (I) Evolution of a swim bladder with hearing function: in some, the swim bladder connects to the inner ear (Weberian apparatus in minnows) to amplify sound, greatly improving hearing sensitivity. Chemoreception is keen – fish use smell and taste to find mates/food (e.g., salmon homing by olfactory cues). Some developed electroreception (e.g., knifefish) or electric organs for communication, showing sensory innovation. Overall, bony fish refined vision (many have UV or polarized light sensitivity) and have specialized lateral line organs, making them superbly tuned to aquatic environments.
R12: 5. Scorpions | (H) Arthropod hormone systems for molting (ecdysteroids) and development (juvenile hormone) as inherited from chelicerate ancestors. (I) Extended gestation in viviparous scorpions required new hormonal controls: e.g., hormones to maintain pregnancy and trigger birth. Molting cycles slow down in scorpions (they have fewer molts, reaching maturity over years), likely regulated by neuroendocrine changes – an adaptation to their long lifespan relative to smaller arthropods. | (H) Chitinous exoskeleton as a physical immune barrier, and innate immune cells (hemocytes) that encapsulate parasites or seal wounds. (I) Potent venom with antimicrobial components – scorpion venom not only subdues prey but can have antibacterial peptides, offering an immune advantage by sterilizing prey or bite wounds. Also, cuticular wax and low surface moisture make it hard for pathogens to thrive. No adaptive immune system (unique to vertebrates), but scorpions are remarkably infection-resistant, indicating highly effective innate defenses. | (H) Tough exoskeleton with segmented plates (sclerites) from ancestral arthropods, providing defense and reducing water loss. (I) Waxy epicuticle that makes the exoskeleton nearly waterproof – crucial for desert survival. Thickened armor on the cephalothorax and claws for defense; the tail’s integument is stretchable yet strong to accommodate the bulging telson (venom gland). Unique pectines (comb-like sensory appendages on the belly) are also part of the integumentary system – covered in chemo- and mechanosensors for exploring the environment. Scorpions even fluoresce under UV light due to chemicals in their cuticle – an interesting innovation whose function is still debated. | (H) Jointed exoskeleton forming body framework (no internal bones, inherited from arthropod design). (I) Specialized skeletal elements: enlarged pedipalp pincers with heavy calcification for strength, and a spined telson (stinger) at the tail’s end housing venom glands. The tail’s last segments are elongated as a lever arm for the stinger – a unique skeletal adaptation among arachnids. Internally, scorpions evolved strong cartilaginous tendon analogues (apodemes) within the exoskeleton to anchor powerful muscles (e.g., for claw closure). Their segmentation is also more consolidated (broad cephalothorax) than in primitive arthropods, an innovation improving stability on land. | (H) Striated muscles attached to exoskeleton for locomotion and feeding, as in other arthropods. (I) Huge pincer muscles in the pedipalps to grab and crush prey; a multi-segmented tail muscle system to curl and strike with the stinger rapidly. The book lung ventilation is passive, but abdominal muscles can help pump hemolymph. Leg muscles are adapted for slow, powerful movements with the ability to hold a raised stance (important for ambushing prey). Scorpions can also compress their body to hide in crevices, indicating flexible membranous joints between armor segments – a muscular and skeletal adaptation for a burrowing lifestyle. | (H) Multiple simple eyes on head detecting light and movement (inherited from early arachnids). (I) Pectines – unique comb-like sensory organs on the underside that detect chemical trails and texture, guiding scorpions to mates or prey. Vibration sensors: leg hair sensilla and slit sensilla in the exoskeleton allow exquisite detection of ground vibrations or air movement (they can sense a prey’s footsteps). While scorpion vision is poor (they rely more on touch and vibration), their other senses are highly tuned; e.g., they can detect minute chemical cues in the soil with pectines, an innovation among terrestrial arthropods replacing the antennae function (since chelicerates lack antennae). They have a rudimentary hearing ability for low frequencies (via vibrations), but largely navigate the nocturnal world through tactile and chemical senses.
R13: 6. “Plants Take Root” | (H) No nerves; cellular signaling via hormones and electrical potentials inherited from algal ancestors. (I) Expanded cell-to-cell communication: e.g., action potentials in plant cells (especially root hairs) to coordinate responses (similar to a primitive nerve signal). Early land plants evolved gene networks for environmental sensing (light, gravity) without neurons – effectively an innovation to perform “nervous” functions through slow chemical and electrical signaling in tissues. | (H) Photosynthetic “digestive” strategy – chloroplasts (from algal endosymbiosis) in cells produce sugars; nutrients absorbed across surfaces. (I) True roots developing at the plant base, an organ specifically for water and mineral uptake. Roots featured root hairs (single-cell extensions) to greatly increase absorptive surface area in soil. Also, symbiosis with mycorrhizal fungi was established, an innovation allowing nutrient exchange: fungi provided minerals (phosphate, etc.) to the plant, and the plant gave sugars – enhancing “digestion” of soil resources beyond the plant’s own enzymatic capabilities. | (H) Gas exchange by diffusion through surfaces (ancestral aquatic algae absorbed CO₂ from water). (I) Stomata – adjustable pores on stems and leaves to regulate gas exchange and water loss, present even in early Devonian plants. A cuticle (waxy layer) on epidermis prevented uncontrolled evaporation, so plants could take in CO₂ without desiccating. Internal air spaces evolved in tissues, facilitating gas diffusion to all cells. These innovations allowed efficient respiration (oxygen release and CO₂ uptake) in a terrestrial atmosphere while conserving water – a balance aquatic ancestors didn’t need to achieve. | (H) Simple diffusion and cytoplasmic streaming in ancestor plants to move substances. (I) Vascular tissue: xylem for water/mineral transport upward from roots, and phloem for distributing sugars from photosynthetic parts. Early vascular plants evolved lignified tracheids in xylem (rigid, tube-like cells) that could carry water under tension from soil to aerial organs. Phloem sieve cells carried organic nutrients. This circulatory analogue in plants was a breakthrough enabling height and complexity – resources could be moved efficiently throughout the organism, unlike in non-vascular mosses. | (H) Reproduction by spores and swimming sperm (a trait of bryophyte ancestors requiring water). (I) Retention of embryo on parent plant (hence “Embryophyte”) – the fertilized egg (zygote) grew into a multicellular embryo while attached to and nourished by the mother plant, a crucial land plant innovation. True roots allowed plants to anchor and support larger sporophytes that produce many spores. These early rooted plants were still tied to water for fertilization (sperm swam to egg), but spores could disperse through the air to new locations. Some later Devonian plants evolved heterospory (different spore sizes for sexes), presaging seeds. | (H) No specialized excretory organs; plants typically sequester or recycle metabolic waste. (I) Cuticle and stomata indirectly manage “excretion” of water vapor and oxygen (byproducts of photosynthesis). Toxic byproducts (e.g., oxygen free radicals) were mitigated by antioxidants – an innovation given high light exposure on land. Additionally, plants began to store waste in vacuoles or dead tissues (e.g. depositing heavy metals or calcium oxalate crystals in leaves) which would later be shed. The loss of leaves or shedding of bark in more derived plants is an excretory strategy to remove wastes – these mechanisms had their origins in the first rooted plants as simple waste compartmentalization in certain cells.
R14: 7. “Woody Stems” (Early Trees) | (H) No nervous system; hormonal signaling continued as primary coordination method. (I) Height-related signaling: larger size prompted evolution of new chemical signals to coordinate growth between roots and an expanding shoot system. For example, auxin produced at the shoot tip travels down and influences the vascular cambium (secondary growth) – a hormonal integration to ensure balanced wood production and root support. Also, detection of gravity (gravitropism) became more critical: woody plants use statolith cells in roots to guide downward growth, an enhanced sensory-hormonal response due to increased size. | (H) Same autotrophic “digestion” via roots and photosynthesis. (I) Secondary growth (wood formation) allowed the development of thick trunks, necessitating deeper roots to uptake sufficient nutrients and water. Early trees like Archaeopteris (a Late Devonian woody plant) had extensive root networks to mine minerals and a vascular cambium to continually supply the growing crown. Leaves (or needles) in woody plants increased in number and area, requiring an efficient transport of nutrients from roots – the coordination between a broad root base and broad leaf area is an innovation of woody architecture, effectively maximizing “feeding” from soil and sun. | (H) Stomata and cuticle from earlier land plants. (I) Woody bark with lenticels – as stems thickened into trunks, gas exchange for inner tissues was enabled by lenticels (small porous breaks in bark) which allow oxygen in for living cells and CO₂ out. Additionally, many early trees evolved leaf stomatal control attuned to height: leaves in the canopy might have smaller, sunken stomata to limit water loss in drying winds. Wood itself contains hollow xylem which also stores some air; in some cases this helps with buoyancy of water transport. These adaptations together ensured that even at larger size, respiration at the cellular level in all tissues was maintained. | (H) Vascular system with xylem/phloem. (I) Wood (secondary xylem) – concentric rings of lignified xylem added each year from a vascular cambium. Wood gave strong support and contained vessels/tracheids for water conduction to great heights. The vascular cambium and cork cambium were new meristems: one producing wood and inner bark, the other producing outer bark, respectively. This allowed the plant to grow thicker indefinitely. The woody stem also served as storage for water and nutrients, helping trees survive seasons. Essentially, the evolution of wood and bark created an internal circulation and support system unparalleled in earlier herbaceous plants, enabling the first forest ecosystems. | (H) Spores and free-swimming sperm (in early tree-like plants like ferns or progymnosperms). (I) Seeds (in seed ferns and early gymnosperms) – by the late Devonian/Carboniferous, woody plants evolved seeds, though the category “woody stems” may precede true seeds. If considering just wood: some woody plants like Archaeopteris were still spore-bearing. But shortly after, seeds evolved, providing embryos with a nutrition package and protective coat, liberating reproduction from water. This meant fertilization via pollen (air-borne or carried) instead of swimming sperm, a revolutionary reproductive strategy for land. Woody seed plants could colonize drier habitats because the embryo was safe inside a seed until germination conditions were right. | (H) No dedicated excretory organ; wastes often stored in plant tissues. (I) Heartwood formation – older xylem in the center of a woody trunk becomes clogged with wastes (tannins, resins) and turns into heartwood, a durable core that also sequesters metabolites the plant doesn’t need. Leaves of deciduous woody plants became an excretory route: trees withdraw nutrients and then drop the leaves, effectively excreting accumulated waste products (and getting rid of excess minerals). Also, thick bark often stores waste compounds (resins, gums) which can be periodically shed or are inert, thus preventing toxin buildup in living cells.
R15: 8. Cartilaginous Fish | (H) Gnathostome brain with developed cerebellum and optic tectum, similar to bony fish. (I) Emphasis on sensory brain regions: extremely large olfactory bulbs in sharks for smell (a key innovation for tracking prey over long distances). Their lateral line and electroreception systems are wired into the central nervous system, giving them a “sixth sense” integration. The cerebellum in fast-swimming sharks is enlarged for balance and coordination in three dimensions. Overall, their nervous system became tuned for predatory efficiency in open water. | (H) Jaws and complete gut as in other jawed fish. (I) Spiral valve intestine in sharks – a corkscrew-shaped fold inside a short intestine that increases surface area for nutrient absorption (compensating for a shorter gut). Also, continuous tooth replacement (teeth arranged in conveyor-belt rows) is a feeding innovation ensuring they always have sharp teeth for prey capture. Their stomach is very stretchy and acidic, allowing them to consume large meals infrequently. Some large sharks (and rays) evolved plankton-filtering mechanisms (gill rakers), showing digestive flexibility despite the typical image of them as macropredators. | (H) Gills with multiple gill slits for water exit, inherited from early fish. (I) Refined gill ventilation: most have muscular pumping of water via mouth and spiracle, but many must swim to ram ventilate. Sharks have no operculum, but a spiracle behind each eye (reduced first gill opening) that draws water in, especially useful when mouth is occupied or when resting on seafloor (e.g., rays breathe through spiracles). The blood-gill interface is efficient, and some active sharks have extremely high gill surface area to support their metabolism. No swim bladder; instead, many sharks can ventilate both while moving and by gulping water, and some can even buccal pump (nurse sharks), showing a range of respiratory strategies. | (H) Single-loop circulatory plan (heart → gills → body). (I) Modifications for osmoregulation: sharks retain urea in their blood in high concentrations, making their blood iso-osmotic with seawater (an innovation in blood chemistry). The heart remains two-chambered, but blood from the gut can bypass the liver in a special shunt (the arterial cone with valves) to maintain blood pressure. Their capillary networks in certain muscles (rete mirabile) allow countercurrent heat exchange in some sharks (e.g., makos), an innovation enabling regional endothermy (warm muscles). Overall, while the basic circulation is inherited, chemical adjustments (urea, trimethylamine oxide in blood) are innovative to their niche. | (H) Internal fertilization traces back to some early placoderms, adopted by cartilaginous fish (males have claspers to introduce sperm into females). (I) Live birth (viviparity) in many species – e.g., some sharks and rays have uterine adaptations to support embryos (yolk-sac placenta in requiem sharks, uterine milk in stingrays). Even oviparous sharks lay tough leathery egg cases (“mermaid’s purses”) that protect the embryo from desiccation and predation – an innovation over simple fish eggs. Extended gestation and fewer, well-developed offspring (K-selection strategy) is a major reproductive shift from spawning huge numbers of eggs as most bony fish do. | (H) Kidneys that filter blood and gills that excrete ammonia, as in other fish. (I) Urea retention – rather than excreting all nitrogenous waste, sharks convert ammonia to urea and keep high levels in blood for osmotic balance. Excess salts from seawater are excreted via a specialized rectal gland (salt gland) – a unique organ of cartilaginous fish. Thus, the excretory system innovated to serve osmoregulation: kidneys also reabsorb urea actively. When necessary, they still excrete some ammonia through gills (especially if in low-salinity water). This dual approach (retain urea, expel salt) lets them live in marine environments without dehydrating.
R16: 9. Lobe-Finned Fish | (H) Brain and nerves similar to other bony fish (forebrain controlling smell, etc.). (I) Neural circuits preadapted for weight-bearing: these fish occasionally use their lobed fins to “walk” under water, and in lungfish, to push through mud. The nervous system controlling fins had to coordinate a front-back limb-like gait (a rudimentary walking rhythm), an innovation from the usual fin-stroke pattern. Additionally, lungfish have an increased olfactory region to detect airborne scents when they gulp air at the surface – showing their nervous system’s adaptability to both water and air cues. | (H) Carnivorous digestive system with jaws, teeth, stomach, intestines akin to other fish. (I) Some lobe-fins developed unique dentition: labyrinthodont teeth (infolded enamel surfaces) providing strength for crushing prey – a trait seen in early sarcopterygians and later early tetrapods. Lungfish have grinding tooth plates to eat hard foods like snails and plants. A spiral valve is present in the intestines of coelacanths (like in sharks), an ancient feature retained to improve nutrient absorption. These modifications hint at a more diverse diet and tougher plant material processing, anticipating partial herbivory or omnivory in land environments. | (H) Gills for aquatic respiration, as with any fish. (I) Lungs in lungfish (and a primitive lung/swim-bladder in coelacanths). Lungfish have a paired lung connected to the gut, allowing them to breathe air in oxygen-poor waters. They can aestivate in mud by breathing air when water dries up – a significant respiratory innovation. Coelacanths have a vestigial lung (fat-filled) but rely on gills; still, the presence of a lung structure shows the lineage’s propensity for air-breathing. The dual respiratory modes in lungfish (gills in water, lungs in air) represent a pivotal evolutionary step toward terrestrial respiration. | (H) Fish circulatory layout with a two-chamber heart and single circuit. (I) Partial double circulation: Lungfish hearts have a partially divided atrium and ventricle, plus a spiral valve in the outflow tract to separate oxygenated blood going to body from deoxygenated blood going to gills/lungs. This is an innovation approaching the 3-chambered heart of amphibians. Blood can bypass the lungs or gills as needed (e.g., when breathing air, more blood is shunted to systemic circulation). This flexibility in circulation and the beginnings of a pulmonary circuit were crucial adaptations for surviving in variable aquatic environments (and foreshadow the tetrapod circulatory plan). | (H) External fertilization in water (spawning), as in most fish. (I) Internal fertilization and viviparity appeared in coelacanths – modern coelacanths give live birth, indicating that some ancient lobe-fins may have evolved this (fossil evidence of this is scant, but the capacity is there). Lungfish still lay eggs in nests, but exhibit parental care: male African lungfish guard the nest and even have vascularized structures on their pelvic fins to oxygenate the water for the young – a novel reproductive behavior. These innovations show a trend towards fewer, better-provisioned offspring, aligning with more challenging freshwater environments. | (H) Kidneys that excrete ammonia when in water. (I) Estivation metabolism (in lungfish): when lungfish burrow in mud during drought, they switch to producing mostly urea (less toxic than ammonia) and store it in tissues to avoid poisoning themselves – a temporary shift in excretory strategy to survive without water. Coelacanths, interestingly, retain urea like sharks, making themselves osmotic with seawater. So among lobe-fins, we see an innovation of ureotelism in two different contexts: one as a permanent marine strategy (coelacanth) and one as a seasonal survival strategy (lungfish). Both indicate advanced kidney regulation of nitrogen waste depending on environment.
R17: 10. Trees | (H) No neurons; uses hormones for internal communication. (I) Canopy-level signaling: large trees evolved complex hormonal and perhaps electrical signaling over long distances (from root to crown). For instance, when under attack (herbivory), some trees send electrical signals and jasmonate hormones from the leaves under attack to other parts, “alerting” them to ramp up defenses – a quasi-nervous communication across a big organism. They also coordinate leaf shedding and growth in response to daylength via phytochrome signals interpreted across the whole plant. This scale of integrated response is an innovation in tall, long-lived plants. | (H) Root absorption and leaf photosynthesis from earlier vascular plants. (I) Deep, extensive root systems with perhaps root grafting between trees of the same species (roots fusing, sharing resources) – an innovation in forest trees to stabilize and mutually support. Also, the development of a central trunk and branching architecture ensures efficient transport of nutrients: this is supported by “source-sink” hormonal signals that direct nutrients from high-production areas (leaves) to growth areas (buds, fruits, roots). Many trees also form mycorrhizal networks linking multiple trees (sometimes called the “wood-wide web”), enabling communal nutrient sharing – effectively an innovation turning a forest into a cooperative digesting network that can redistribute resources. | (H) Stomata, cuticle, lenticels as in woody plants. (I) Massive transpiration engine: tall trees pull enormous volumes of water from soil to leaves (transpiration stream), which also brings CO₂ in and oxygen out. The cohesion-tension mechanism in xylem is pushed to its limits in tall trees, and they evolved bordered pits in xylem cells to prevent air bubbles (emboli) from breaking the water column – a micro-structural innovation ensuring steady gas exchange. Additionally, many trees regulate stomatal opening in different microclimates (sun vs shade leaves) to optimize gas exchange. Some tree species (conifers) evolved needle-like leaves with recessed stomata to continue gas exchange in extremely dry or cold environments without losing too much water – allowing them to inhabit climates that broadleaf ancestors could not. | (H) Vascular system with wood and bark from earlier woody plants. (I) Large-diameter xylem vessels (in angiosperm trees) for very efficient water transport, and phloem companion cells for active transport of sugars – these appeared as plants diversified into true trees. The ability to grow a hundred feet tall and a broad canopy is supported by a refined vascular cambium that can produce reaction wood (compression or tension wood) to physically support branches at different angles. In addition, structural adaptations like buttress roots in tropical trees or taproots in deep soil are skeletal innovations to support massive size. Essentially, trees evolved the most elaborate vascular “circulatory” and support systems in the plant kingdom, including wood anatomy that resists bending and torsion from wind. | (H) Seed-based reproduction (in gymnosperm and angiosperm trees) from earlier seed plant innovations. (I) Pollen and seed dispersal strategies at large scale: tall trees use height to their advantage by spreading pollen and seeds over long distances (wind dispersal in conifers, or via animals in fruit-bearing angiosperm trees). Many evolved seasonal reproduction cycles (flowering/seed set annually or mast years) timed with environmental cues – a higher order integration of reproduction with climate and ecosystem. Additionally, trees often have long juvenile periods and very long lifespans, requiring innovations in meristem maintenance (some can produce seeds even after centuries, showing incredible somatic longevity). All this ensures that reproduction in trees, though slower, has wide reach and high success in variable environments. | (H) Same plant strategy of sequestering wastes in tissues. (I) Deciduous habit in many trees: by shedding leaves annually, trees discard loads of metabolic wastes that have accumulated, effectively a dump-and-regenerate strategy for excretion. Some evergreens sequester resins and complex waste in heartwood or bark, then periodically shed bark (e.g., eucalyptus peeling bark releases wastes). The production of secondary metabolites (tannins, alkaloids) in wood and leaves – often considered defenses – also serves to tie up nitrogenous or other wastes in inert forms. So, trees innovated in using their large size and tissue turnover to manage wastes over decades, something short-lived plants face less.
R18: Species | Nervous System | Digestive System | Respiratory System | Circulatory System | Reproductive System | Excretory System
R19: 11. Tiktaalik | (H) Fish-like brain structure (optic lobe, olfactory bulb, cerebellum) and lateral line nerves. (I) Neural control for a movable neck – first fish to lift its head independently of body, requiring new muscle control and brainstem integration. Also, expanded motor neurons to limbs: its pectoral fins (proto-limbs) had to bear weight and push, so Tiktaalik’s nervous system was adapting to coordinate limb-like movements on substrate. This foreshadows the neural requirements for walking in tetrapods, an innovation beyond purely aquatic locomotor patterns. | (H) Predatory diet with jaws and sharp teeth, swallowing prey whole like a fish. (I) Flat, crocodile-like skull with a strong jaw allowed snapping up prey at water’s edge. Tiktaalik likely could gulp air and food: it had a robust ribcage suggesting muscles to gulp down air or food against gravity (unlike fish which rely on water buoyancy). It may have had a stronger tongue or throat musculature beginning to form, to help manipulate food in shallows (where suction feeding is less effective) – a minor but vital digestive adaptation as it started feeding in very shallow water/land interface. | (H) Gills for aquatic breathing, like any fish (likely covered by gill flaps). (I) Lungs present: evidence suggests Tiktaalik had lungs (as indicated by rib structure and its place among lunged fish) and spiracles on top of its head that could function as breathing holes. This means it could raise its head to gulp air, an adaptation to oxygen-poor swamp water. The ribs were stout, forming a partial ribcage to prevent the lungs from collapsing out of water – an innovation towards terrestrial breathing. Thus Tiktaalik could breathe both in water (with gills) and air (with lungs), a dual capability critical in the transition to land. | (H) Fish-type circulation (two-chambered heart, blood flows from heart to gills to body). (I) With functional lungs, a rudimentary pulmonary circulation was evolving. Likely a partial partitioning of the atrium (as seen in lungfish) to direct some blood to lungs and some to gills. Its blood oxygenation could occur in either gills or lungs, requiring flexibility in circulation. The robust physiology to handle both oxygen sources is innovative. Additionally, a stronger vascularization of limbs would be needed (to supply muscle now occasionally working against gravity), so Tiktaalik’s circulatory system was beginning to accommodate weight-bearing limb muscles by delivering more blood there when on land. | (H) External fertilization in water – eggs and sperm released in aquatic environment, like amphibian eggs. (I) Tiktaalik likely still reproduced in water, but as a transitional form it may have sought shallow, plant-choked water to lay eggs, reducing predation (a behavioral shift). The eggs might have larger yolk than typical fish to support development in potentially warmer, variable shallows. While not a full amniote, Tiktaalik’s life history was trending toward the amphibian strategy of seasonal breeding in specific shallows. It did not yet have any amniotic features (no hard egg or internal fertilization), so reproduction was largely inherited, with slight ecological adjustments. | (H) Kidneys and gills excreting ammonia (the standard fish mechanism). (I) In shallow water or mud, Tiktaalik might sometimes experience higher ammonia levels; it possibly could excrete some waste through skin when in air (ammonia can diffuse out through moist skin surfaces). If stranded in low water, it may have reduced ammonia production temporarily. Some speculate semi-terrestrial fish can switch to more urea production to avoid ammonia toxicity out of water – Tiktaalik may have had a nascent ability to detoxify ammonia to urea when air-breathing for extended periods. This would be an analog to lungfish estivation physiology, a forward-thinking excretory tweak in an evolving tetrapod.
R20: 12. Earliest Tetrapods | (H) Tetrapod brain derived from fish (with regions for vision, smell, etc.). (I) Improved motor control for limbs – spinal cord and brain circuitry evolved to generate walking gaits on land. Enlarged cerebellum to coordinate four-limbed locomotion and balance on uneven surfaces. The lateral line system is still present in aquatic juveniles but is lost in adults that live on land, indicating neural plasticity (the nervous system retools as the animal metamorphoses). Additionally, cranial nerves adapted: the hyomandibular nerve now serving hearing (via stapes) rather than gill support, a repurposing of neural function tied to the new auditory sense. | (H) Carnivorous diet, gulping prey as inherited from fish (many early tetrapods ate fish or invertebrates in water). (I) Muscular tongue development – to feed on land, a sticky tongue or at least a mobile tongue was necessary to manipulate food (since suction feeding doesn’t work in air). Early tetrapods developed a fleshy tongue attached to the floor of mouth (from ancestral gill arch musculature) to help swallow prey on land. Their teeth were labyrinthine (folded enamel) like their lobe-finned ancestors, good for gripping slippery prey. Also, salivary glands likely began to appear to lubricate food (keeping mouth and food moist for swallowing in air, an innovation absent in fish). | (H) Lungs in adults (inherited from lungfish-like ancestors) and gills in larvae – amphibian ancestors had both respiratory modes. (I) Cutaneous respiration – the skin became a respiratory organ, exchanging O₂/CO₂ when moist​pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. This is a novel strategy that supplements relatively inefficient lungs. Also, earliest tetrapods retained internal gills (Acanthostega had fish-like internal gills), but as tetrapods diversified, adults lost gills entirely and relied on lungs & skin. A buccal pump mechanism (throat pumping) evolved to inflate lungs since ribs were still not fully developed to ventilate. These innovations allowed survival both in water and on land, albeit clumsily – needing moist habitats due to cutaneous breathing. | (H) Heart with partial separation (from lungfish/tetrapod ancestor) sending blood to lungs and body. (I) Three-chambered heart fully established – two atria and one ventricle with a partial septum, so oxygen-rich and poor blood are better separated. This supported higher activity on land by improving oxygen delivery. Double circulation (pulmonary vs systemic) was now obligatory: blood goes heart → lungs → heart → body. Additionally, blood pressure increased to overcome gravity and push blood up into the head and through limbs. Early tetrapods likely developed valves and lymphatic systems to maintain circulation in a terrestrial posture. | (H) Eggs without shells laid in water, as per amphibian reproductive mode (inherited from fish). (I) Gelatinous egg clutches in moist environments – earliest tetrapods (early amphibians) began laying eggs in very shallow water or wet land, sometimes wrapping eggs in vegetation to keep them moist. Some evidence suggests they experimented with laying eggs on land in very humid areas. They also had an aquatic larval stage with metamorphosis into a land adult, a distinct innovation of amphibians. Parental investment increased slightly: guarding of egg masses or choosing nursery pools (a behavior not seen in fish) started to appear, protecting offspring until metamorphosis. | (H) Kidneys excreting ammonia in water (larval stage) as fish do. (I) Urea excretion – adult tetrapods (amphibians) shifted to primarily excreting urea through the kidneys, which requires less water than ammonia. They evolved urinary bladders to store urine (a new organ to accumulate dilute urine and reabsorb water from it, especially in terrestrial life). Skin and lungs also became routes of excretion for CO₂ and some ions. Overall, earliest tetrapods had a flexible excretory strategy: ammonia release in water, urea on land – bridging the gap until the more water-conserving amniote system took over.
R21: 13. Insects (wings & size) | (H) Segmented ventral nerve cord and fused brain (proto-cerebrum, etc.) as in arthropod ancestors. (I) Flight circuitry – insects evolved specialized motor neuron clusters and central pattern generators to control wing flapping at high frequencies, a neural innovation for powered flight. Giant Carboniferous insects had to coordinate much larger bodies, so their nervous system likely had larger diameter nerves for faster conduction to far extremities. Compound eyes became more complex, feeding massive visual data to the brain for flight navigation. Some insects also evolved sophisticated social behaviors (later on), hinting at neural plasticity, but the initial big leap was the neural integration required for flight and aerial acrobatics. | (H) Typical arthropod digestive tract (foregut with crop, midgut for digestion, hindgut for water absorption). (I) Extracellular enzyme digestion and symbiosis for herbivory: as insects grew in size and diversified, many (like giant Paleozoic cockroaches) harbored gut microbes to help digest cellulose – an adaptation to consuming high-fiber plant matter. The evolution of a compartmentalized gut (crop for storage, gizzard-like proventriculus for grinding, midgut caeca for enzyme secretion) allowed efficient processing of food to fuel high metabolism (especially in flyers). Some flying insects developed sugar-rich diets (nectar feeding in early forms) requiring long proboscises and the ability to digest liquid diets quickly – new feeding strategies tied to specialized mouthparts. | (H) Tracheal system: network of internal air tubes delivering O₂ directly to tissues, present in primitive hexapods. (I) Spiracles with valves – insects evolved muscles to open/close spiracles, preventing water loss while ensuring gas exchange as needed, crucial for living in dry air. The tracheal system scaled up in giant insects: high atmospheric O₂ (≈30%) in the Carboniferous allowed tracheae to supply even huge bodies, enabling dragonflies with 70 cm wingspans. Additionally, flying insects evolved air sacs and synchronized abdominal movements to pump air, enhancing oxygen flow during flight. The ability to ventilate tracheae (instead of relying purely on diffusion) was an innovation supporting sustained powered flight. | (H) Open circulatory system with heart (dorsal vessel) pumping hemolymph, as in arthropods. (I) Although blood does not carry O₂ in insects, circulation was refined for other roles: rapid distribution of nutrients and heat. In large insects, the dorsal heart gained multiple chambers and auxillary pumps (e.g., in wing base) to ensure even hemolymph flow to far extremities like wings. Some insects evolved antifreeze proteins and thermal regulation behaviors (e.g., shivering flight muscles to warm up) – not circulatory organs per se, but physiological innovations to support an active lifestyle. The open system remained, but high metabolic insects showed increased hemolymph circulation rates and better pressure control during flight (an adaptation to avoid pooling of blood in the abdomen when upside down, for example). | (H) Internal fertilization (spermatophores or copulation) producing eggs, inherited from early land arthropods. (I) Amniotic-like egg: insect eggs have a hard or leathery chorion shell that resists desiccation, often with specialized micropyles for sperm entry and that protect the developing embryo on land. The evolution of metamorphosis is also key: many winged insects (especially later, in Permian) evolved complete metamorphosis – eggs hatch into larvae specialized for feeding, then a pupal stage, then adult – a strategy to exploit different niches and reduce competition between life stages. Even primitive winged forms (hemimetabolous) had distinct nymph stages often aquatic (e.g., dragonfly nymphs), showcasing an innovation of life cycle complexity that allowed exploitation of both water (young) and air (adult). Wings themselves are an enormous reproductive boon – facilitating dispersal to new habitats to lay eggs. | (H) Malpighian tubules emptying into hindgut, excreting nitrogenous waste – an arthropod trait. (I) Uric acid production: insects predominantly excrete nitrogen as solid uric acid or related compounds, a crucial water-saving innovation for terrestrial life. They can absorb water from feces in the rectum, producing nearly dry pellets – key for survival in air. In huge insects of the Carboniferous, water was plentiful (swampy environments), but the uricotelic system still allowed them to maintain ion balance efficiently. Additionally, some insects evolved the ability to “reflex bleed” defensive fluids (not true excretion, but a use of excretory pathways for defense by ejecting noxious blood when threatened). Overall, the insect excretory system became superbly frugal with water, which along with the waxy cuticle, set the stage for their conquest of land.
R22: 14. Early Crabs & Shrimps | (H) Arthropod CNS with brain and ventral nerve cord controlling segmented body and many appendages. (I) Fusion of ganglia in thorax to coordinate complex limb movements (e.g., the rapid beating of swimmerets in shrimp or the sideways walk of crabs). In crabs, the antennae and eyestalks feed sensory data to a relatively advanced brain for an arthropod. Some crabs show primitive learning (maze navigation) indicating a neural upgrade. The crustacean nervous system also evolved stomatogastric ganglia (for rhythmic gut movements) that are finely tuned – not new, but well-developed. Unique behavior like polarization vision in mantis shrimp is beyond early forms, but early decapods did refine their equilibrium sensing via statocysts to aid complex locomotion – a neural-sensory integration improvement. | (H) Typical crustacean digestive tract with two-chambered stomach and digestive gland. (I) Gastric mill – many decapods have a set of chitinous “teeth” in the cardiac stomach that grind food, an innovation for processing hard food (e.g., mollusk shells). The hepatopancreas (digestive gland) is large and multi-lobed, efficiently secreting enzymes and absorbing nutrients; its role is so extensive that it’s akin to a liver-pancreas combo, refined in these crustaceans. Filter-feeding shrimps evolved setae (bristles) on limbs to sieve food, requiring modifications in the gut to handle fine particles. Also, storage excretion (accumulating calcium in gut ceca between molts) is notable – prior to molting, they reabsorb calcium from old shell via the digestive system, an innovative recycling mechanism linked to digestion. | (H) Aquatic gills on appendages or inside branchial chambers, as in ancestral crustaceans. (I) Branchial chamber and scaphognathite: decapods like crabs have gills enclosed in a chamber under the carapace, with a specialized leaf-like appendage (the scaphognathite) that pumps water actively over the gills. This efficient ventilator allows crabs to breathe even when stationary or partly out of water. Terrestrial crabs later modified gills or developed pseudolungs, but early forms perhaps had rudiments of this (e.g., isopod-like pleopodal lungs in some). The key innovation here is active ventilation and protecting gills from drying – the sealed branchial cavity of crabs retains moisture, enabling some to venture onto land briefly. Hemocyanin in blood binds O₂ in these crustaceans, and some evolved very high affinity hemocyanins for low-oxygen environments, reflecting respiratory protein innovation. | (H) Dorsal heart and open hemolymph circulation, like all arthropods. (I) Decapods often have a well-developed network of arteries from the heart to vital organs (brain, muscles), an improvement over a simple open wash. This quasi-closed network in larger crustaceans ensures adequate blood flow to brain and limbs during rapid movement. Blue copper-based blood (hemocyanin) is present; in some crabs, hemocyanin is so effective that it supports surprisingly high activity levels (some crabs can sustain chasing prey, hinting at an optimized circulatory delivery). Additionally, crustaceans have an ostial heart that can adjust rate based on oxygen demand (neural control of heart rate akin to vertebrate regulation), a refinement for their often active or variable lifestyle (e.g., burrowed vs. swimming). | (H) Typically separate sexes with internal fertilization or close coupling (sperm transfer) and egg brooding, inherited from some earlier crustaceans. (I) Brood care: female decapods carry fertilized eggs on their swimmerets (pleopods) until hatching, fanning water over them – this is a significant behavioral and morphological innovation (the pleopods have setae to hold eggs). The eggs have more yolk and often hard protective coverings compared to those of simpler crustaceans, improving embryonic survival. Larval stages (nauplius, zoea, megalopa, etc.) are specialized for planktonic life, indicating that reproduction was tuned to disperse young widely. The innovation of a protected, multi-stage life cycle (with metamorphoses between larval stages) allowed these crustaceans to exploit various niches from deep water to shoreline during development. | (H) Antennal or green glands (renal glands) at head filter blood and excrete ammonia, like ancestral crustaceans. (I) Ion regulation: many decapods live in estuaries – they evolved efficient osmoregulatory function in their excretory organs and gills, tolerating varying salinity. Some early crabs could even store water in their gill chambers and venture on land, excreting wastes intermittently when back in water (proto-terrestrial adaptation). While still primarily ammonotelic (ammonia-excreting through gills), the ability to produce some urea or uric acid in land-crabs evolved later. However, a key innovation in these crustaceans is the reabsorption of salts in the excretory gland, allowing them to conserve salts when in freshwater and to excrete excess when in the sea – a flexibility crucial for their diverse habitats.
R23: 15. Ferns | (H) No nervous system, only chemical signaling as in plants. (I) Response to light and touch: many ferns (like sensitive fern) can move their fronds slightly in response to stimuli, via changes in cell turgor – a very primitive “irritability” function. They also coordinate spore release with humidity changes (synchronized via physical-hormonal cues). While they lack nerves, the ability of the annulus mechanism on sporangia to catapult spores when dry is a controlled response to environment, an almost mechanical “decision” encoded in their tissues – an innovative adaptation for spore dispersal timing. | (H) Photosynthetic autotroph with uptake of water/minerals via roots and rhizomes (inherited vascular plant nutrition mode). (I) Large fronds (megaphylls) – ferns were among the first plants with true large leaves, increasing photosynthetic area dramatically. These fronds also enabled ferns to tap into new nutrient sources: as they die and decay, they form rich humus benefiting the plant’s own roots (a self-fertilization loop in forest floors). Some ferns evolved mutualisms (e.g., nests for ant colonies that provide nutrients via wastes), showing versatility in nutrient acquisition strategies. Additionally, ferns often grow in low-light understorey; they innovated extremely efficient low-light photosynthesis and sunfleck usage – an adaptation of their “digestive” energy-gathering strategy to the dappled forest environment. | (H) Stomata on leaves for gas exchange, as in other vascular plants. (I) Ferns often inhabit moist, shaded habitats and can also absorb water and exchange gases through their fronds and even gametophyte bodies directly. Some ferns (epiphytes) get moisture from air – their respiratory exchange partly occurs over exposed root structures or scales that trap water. A unique feature: the independence of the tiny gametophyte (prothallus) – it performs gas exchange directly with the environment as a thin film-like structure. The fern life cycle innovated by separating a free-living photosynthetic gametophyte that can handle its own respiration apart from the sporophyte, a strategy that gives ferns two shots at occupying suitable microhabitats (one as a spore that grows into a tiny plant, then as the fern itself). | (H) Xylem/phloem vascular system for internal transport, inherited from earlier tracheophytes. (I) Many ferns have a siphonostele (a tube-like arrangement of vascular tissue with a pith) which allows them to have multiple leaf gaps – letting many fronds connect to the vascular cylinder without weakening transport. This is a step up from simpler protosteles of earlier plants, enabling robust circulation to numerous large fronds. Tree ferns evolved trunk-like stems with a mantle of roots that also conduct water, an innovative augmentation of the circulatory system to reach heights of several meters without true wood. In essence, ferns diversified stele types and added hydraulic redundancy (roots and rhizomes complementing the main stem), ensuring effective nutrient/water flow in their often large, feathery bodies. | (H) Spore reproduction with water-dependent fertilization (sperm swim to egg), same as their primitive plant ancestors. (I) Dominant sporophyte stage: unlike bryophytes, ferns innovated by making the spore-producing plant the large, dominant generation, with the gametophyte small and transient. This allowed spores to be elevated and dispersed by wind more effectively from the large fronds. Some ferns became heterosporous (producing microspores and megaspores, e.g., aquatic ferns like Salvinia), an innovation foreshadowing seed plants by specializing spores for male and female – ensuring cross-fertilization and provisioning the female gametophyte with more resources. These reproductive tweaks increased genetic diversity and survival of offspring, pushing the boundaries of the primitive spore system. | (H) No dedicated excretory organ; plants sequester or repurpose wastes. (I) Silica deposition: many ferns deposit silica in their tissues (which deters herbivores but also sequesters a mineral waste). Some ferns concentrate arsenic or metals in their fronds, effectively removing it from their metabolism and shedding it when fronds die. Additionally, ferns in nutrient-poor environments evolve to be very efficient in nutrient cycling (their shed fronds rapidly decompose, returning nutrients near their roots). The shedding of older fronds can be seen as an excretory process, as it rids the fern of accumulated metabolic byproducts. Also, many ferns produce allelopathic chemicals (affecting neighboring plants) – these could be considered waste products put to use as defense; once excreted into the soil, they inhibit competitors, thus turning excretion into an advantage.
R24: 16. Amphibians | (H) Brain and nervous system inherited from early tetrapods (similar layout to fish but with additions for land). (I) Auditory processing: amphibians evolved an inner ear sensitive to airborne sound (via the tympanic membrane and stapes); their brain has an auditory center to interpret these sounds – a new sensory modality requiring neural adaptation. The optic tectum is still large (for vision), but now vision had to work in air and through a movable eyelid, so neural circuits for eye lubrication (blink reflex) and accommodation in air improved. Spinal cord circuits adapted to limb locomotion – amphibians can perform stepping, leaping (in anurans), or burrowing (in caecilians), each requiring special neural pattern generators (e.g., frogs have neural circuits for synchronous hindlimb thrusts when jumping). | (H) Carnivorous diet with a simple stomach and intestines (short in adult frogs since meat diet, longer in tadpoles if herbivorous). (I) Projectile tongue in many (frogs especially): a front-attached tongue flips out rapidly to capture insects, an innovation requiring muscular and adhesive specializations, effectively externalizing part of digestion (grabbing prey). Amphibians also evolved vomiting reflex (some can vomit to purge toxins, which fish generally cannot). Tadpoles (larval amphibians) often have a different digestive system – long coiled gut for digesting algae – which then metamorphoses into a shorter gut; this radical change in one lifetime is a unique innovation, allowing amphibians to exploit two different diets in two life stages. | (H) Lungs present (from fish ancestry) and cutaneous respiration. (I) Cutaneous gas exchange became so important that some small amphibians have no lungs at all and breathe entirely through skin. Amphibian skin has a rich capillary network to facilitate this​pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. To ventilate lungs, amphibians perfected buccal pumping (swallowing air), since they lack diaphragms. They also can use gills during the larval stage (salamander larvae have external gills). The flexibility to respire through gills (larva), lungs, and skin, switching as needed, is a hallmark of amphibians. Many can absorb oxygen from water through skin while diving and then switch to lungs on land – a highly versatile respiratory system. | (H) Three-chambered heart (two atria, one ventricle) from early tetrapod design. (I) Amphibians fine-tuned this with a spiral valve in the conus arteriosus of the heart to help separate oxygenated and deoxygenated blood, improving efficiency. They also developed cutaneous circulation – special blood vessels (e.g., branches of the pulmocutaneous artery) direct blood to the skin for gas exchange, then back to the heart. This is an innovation integrating skin breathing with the circulatory loop. Lymphatic hearts (auxiliary pumps for lymph fluid) also evolved in amphibians to
R25: 16. Amphibians | (H) Tetrapod brain (forebrain for smell, midbrain for vision, hindbrain for balance) from fish ancestors. (I) Auditory sense: developed a tympanic membrane (eardrum) and a single ear ossicle (stapes) to hear airborne sounds, with brain circuits to process them – a new neural pathway for hearing on land. The spinal cord had to coordinate limbs for walking/jumping, an upgrade from the simple undulatory swimming patterns of fish. Additionally, amphibians exhibit neuroendocrine control of metamorphosis (brain-pituitary-thyroid axis) which orchestrates their drastic transition from water to land form, a complex integration not seen in earlier vertebrates. | (H) Carnivorous diet in adults with simple gut; larval stages often herbivorous with longer gut. (I) Projectile tongue in many (frogs) – a front-attached, sticky tongue flips out to catch insects, a novel feeding mechanism requiring specialized muscles and adhesive saliva. Salivary glands emerged to help swallow on land. Amphibian larvae (tadpoles) have unique keratin beaks and long guts for algae grazing, then during metamorphosis the digestive system remodels to a short carnivorous form – an unparalleled dietary transition that maximizes resource use in two environments. | (H) Functional lungs and skin used for gas exchange (gills in larval stage) inherited from lobe-finned fish. (I) Cutaneous respiration: skin richly vascularized to absorb O₂ and release CO​pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov】, allowing breathing through skin especially during diving or hibernation. Adults retained a buccal pump method to ventilate lungs (swallowing air) since they lack a diaphragm. Many larvae have external gills, and some adults (salamanders like Necturus) keep gills – an innovation of developmental plasticity. These multiple respiratory options let amphibians exploit water and air, though binding them to moist habitats. | (H) Partially divided circulation from fish (two atria, one ventricle heart) sending blood to both lungs and body. (I) Refined double circulation: oxygenated and deoxygenated blood streams stay somewhat separate within the single ventricle via a spiral valve, improving efficiency. Amphibians can shunt blood away from lungs when underwater. They also evolved lymphatic hearts (tiny auxiliary pumps) to move lymph fluid, critical because their leaky capillaries (for skin breathing) require extra help returning fluid to the bloodstream. While not as efficient as amniote circulation, these tweaks support their dual life – providing sufficient oxygen whether submerged or on land. | (H) External fertilization in most (e.g., frogs): ancestral condition of laying jelly-coated eggs in water. (I) Amplexus behavior – male amphibian clasps the female to synchronously release sperm as eggs are laid, ensuring higher fertilization success (a behavioral innovation). Eggs are usually laid in water or very moist environments and hatch into aquatic larvae (tadpoles), a continuation of the ancestral cycle. However, amphibians diversified strategies: some salamanders evolved internal fertilization via spermatophores, and a few frogs/caecilians lay eggs on land or retain them internally, reflecting the first steps towards freeing reproduction from water (e.g., foam nests, brood pouches). Metamorphosis (egg → gilled larva → lunged adult) is a signature amphibian innovation allowing exploitation of two niches in one lifetime. | (H) Kidneys similar to fish (mesonephric) that excrete ammonia in water. (I) Ureotelism: adult amphibians excrete primarily urea (less toxic) when on land, conserving water – a shift from ammonia excretion. They possess a urinary bladder that stores dilute urine and can reabsorb water from it, an adaptation for dry periods. Amphibian skin also allows ammonia diffusion when in water, and some tree frogs can reabsorb water through a specialized pelvic patch of skin, minimizing urine output. These flexible excretory tactics are key for living in alternating aquatic and terrestrial settings, bridging the gap until the more water-conserving amniote kidney evolved.
R26: Species | Nervous System | Digestive System | Respiratory System | Circulatory System | Reproductive System | Excretory System
R27: 17. Amniotes (first) | (H) Tetrapod brain with similar regions to amphibians. (I) Expanded cerebrum for more complex behavior and sensory processing on land (e.g., improved olfaction and vision integration). The loss of lateral line systems freed up neural resources for other senses. Enhanced motor control for more agile locomotion appears (as early amniotes were more active runners than amphibians). Overall, the nervous system became better at terrestrial life – faster reflexes, better balance (thanks to a longer neck providing more sensory input about head position), and more intricate social/mating behaviors driven by a bigger forebrain. | (H) Carnivorous or insectivorous diet (simple gut) like many amphibians. (I) Salivary glands well-developed – amniotes moisten food and begin digestion in the mouth, a new feature since swallowing dry food required lubrication.
