A muscular tongue and stronger jaws allowed amniotes to bite off pieces of food (some early amniotes had small peg-like teeth suited to insects). The longer neck enabled them to reach food and manipulate it better. Also, in some early amniotes, neck muscles helped swallow (since they couldn’t use water to wash food down), an innovation for fully terrestrial feeding. | (H) Lungs as primary respiratory organ (no gills in adults), inherited from tetrapod ancestors. (I) Costal (rib) breathing: amniotes used muscles between the ribs (intercostals) to expand the chest and draw in air, rather than throat pumpin】. This innovation allowed a longer neck and larger lungs, since air could be pulled through a long trachea. The throat was freed from breathing duty and could be used for feeding/vocalizing. The lungs themselves became more compartmentalized (increasing surface area with inner folds) to support higher metabolism. Some amniotes (later synapsids) even added a diaphragm-like muscle for even more efficient ventilation – but the key first step was rib-powered inhalation, markedly improving respiratory capacity on land. | (H) Partial separation of circuits as in amphibians. (I) High-pressure, dual-circuit circulation: amniote hearts evolved a septum that was more developed, especially in active species, to separate oxygenated and deoxygenated blood. Early amniotes likely had a 3-chambered heart with an improved septum in the ventricle (and some vascular shunts). This delivered higher blood pressure to the body, crucial for animals with higher activity and raised posture. Blood was thus more efficiently oxygenated from lungs and delivered to tissues. Over time, this would lead to the fully four-chambered heart (in later archosaurs and mammals), but even the earliest sauropsids and synapsids showed circulatory refinement beyond amphibians. | (H) Still laid eggs in water or very damp areas (ancestral condition). (I) Amniotic egg: the defining innovation – eggs enclosed in a shell with internal membranes, allowing them to be laid on land without drying ou】. The amnion membrane forms a fluid-filled sac around the embryo (providing a “pond” for development】, the chorion facilitates gas exchange through the shel】, and the allantois stores nitrogenous waste and aids respiratio】. Early amniote eggs likely had leathery shells (as in modern reptiles) and required humid environments, but they freed the lineage from returning to water for reproduction. Internal fertilization became obligatory (sperm must fertilize the egg before the shell forms), leading to the evolution of intromittent organs (penis) or cloacal apposition for mating – another departure from amphibians. | (H) Kidneys similar to amphibian’s but improved concentration ability. (I) Water-conserving excretion: early amniotes shifted toward uric acid excretion (in sauropsids】 or concentrated urea (in synapsids), to package nitrogen waste in a less toxic, less water-dependent form within the eggshell (embryos cannot eliminate ammonia easily in a closed egg). The embryonic allantois stores waste safely away from the developing embry】. In adults, amniote kidneys evolved longer tubules and (in synapsids) beginnings of loops to concentrate urine. Many early reptiles also had salt glands to excrete excess ions from diet, an innovation for life in arid or marine environments. Overall, amniotes could retain water far better than amphibians – a critical factor in fully colonizing land.
R28: 18. Earliest Sauropsids | (H) Basal amniote brain plan. (I) Vision-centric brain: early sauropsids (reptile line) likely had very keen vision (large optic tecta), as modern reptiles and birds are visual hunters. Color vision was probably well-developed (many sauropsids have four cone types). The forebrain (pallium) in sauropsids started to develop a different layout than synapsids, with the first signs of the dorsal ventricular ridge – an area that in birds/reptiles handles visual and spatial processing. These neural tweaks suggest sauropsids were adapting to daytime, visually guided activity, in contrast to synapsid’s nocturnal tendency. | (H) Carnivorous/insectivorous diet. (I) Some early sauropsids (like Captorhinus in the Permian) evolved multiple rows of teeth on the jaws, possibly to grind plant material – an early experiment in herbivory. In general, sauropsids maintained a simple gut for meat, but those dabbling in plants likely had longer intestines or fermentation chambers. Sauropsids also evolved keratinous horny beaks or pads in some lineages (turtles, later) – the beginnings seen in herbivorous captorhinids with thickened jaw bones and roughened bone that suggest a beak. Salivary glands in sauropsids sometimes included venom glands (by Late Permian some reptiles were venomous) – a novel digestive aid for subduing prey. | (H) Costal lung ventilation (rib movement) as in ancestral amniotes. (I) Faveolar lungs: sauropsids tend to have lungs with a spongy interior of faveoli (pocket-like chambers) rather than alveoli. Early sauropsids likely developed more subdivided lungs to increase surface area. They also had relatively narrow, elongated ribcages, indicating efficient airflow. Some may have evolved accessory air sacs or unidirectional airflow earlier than birds/crocs, but evidence is scant for the very first sauropsids. We do see in later sauropsids (archosaurs) the seeds of one-way airflow. But initially, the key respiratory edge was simply a dry, keratinized airway and the ability to take deeper breaths, enabling higher activity in arid environments than amphibians could manage. | (H) Improved three-chambered heart from base amniotes. (I) Selective shunting: many sauropsids can direct blood flow dynamically (e.g., away from lungs when diving). Early sauropsids had the beginnings of this ability, via partial septa and arterial bypasses. Their blood pressure was higher than amphibians, enabling a more active life and higher stance. While the earliest sauropsids didn’t yet have a full four-chamber heart, the groundwork was laid. Over the Permian, some lineages (ancestors of crocodilians) improved the septum. But even the “anapsid” grade sauropsids had coronary arteries feeding the heart muscle (a sign of higher metabolism) and a division of the ventricle during contraction, indicating a step toward endotherm-level circulation (realized much later in birds). | (H) Amniotic egg with internal fertilization, as per the clade. (I) Hard-shelled eggs: early sauropsids probably still had soft, leathery shells, but the move toward calcium carbonate stiff shells (like in dinosaurs and modern birds) is a sauropsid trend. A harder shell provides better protection and allows parents to bury or even brood eggs. Sauropsids also pioneered temperature-dependent sex determination (seen in modern turtles and many reptiles) – this may have been present in the earliest sauropsids, using incubation temperature to influence sex ratios (a unique reproductive twist not found in synapsids). Parental care in earliest sauropsids was minimal (likely just choosing good sites for eggs), but the stage was set for later reptiles to guard nests (as some Permian pareiasaurs might have). | (H) Uric acid–based excretion (uricotelism) typical of reptiles, to conserve water. (I) Bladder for water reabsorption: most sauropsids evolved a urinary bladder that can reclaim water from urine, allowing them to live in dry conditions. Also, early sauropsids had relatively small glomeruli in kidneys – an adaptation to produce less initial filtrate and thus less water loss. The solid uric acid waste could be expelled as a semi-solid paste along with feces, an efficient strategy. Some early sauropsids in coastal areas likely evolved salt glands (e.g., in the nose or near eyes) to excrete excess salt from a carnivorous diet, an innovation that let them drink saline water or eat salty prey without dehydrating.
R29: 19. Earliest Synapsids | (H) Similar basic brain to other amniotes. (I) Olfactory emphasis: synapsids (mammal lineage) placed greater importance on smell; their olfactory bulbs enlarged, indicating these early synapsids (like Archaeothyris) were possibly nocturnal hunters using scent. The synapsid brain began developing a different cortex (pallium) structure, which would later become the mammalian neocortex – at this stage, just slight increases in brain folding and size, particularly in areas for smell and perhaps hearing. The single temporal fenestra in skull allowed a larger jaw muscle, but also potentially provided space for expanded jaw muscle proprioceptors and nerves – improving neural feedback for precise chewing, a prelude to later complex mastication. | (H) Insectivorous/carnivorous diet with modestly complex gut. (I) Differentiated teeth (heterodonty): even the earliest synapsids showed beginnings of distinct tooth types – notably large canines for piercing prey, and smaller rear teeth for cutting (not yet true molars, but headed that way). This is an innovation for more efficient food processing. Jaw mechanics also shifted: the synapsid jaw became more muscular and began to chew, not just snap. To support higher feeding rates (if endothermy was creeping in), some later synapsids evolved a secondary palate to eat and breathe simultaneously – though earliest ones likely had only a partial palate. Another digestive innovation down the line was milk (though earliest synapsids didn’t have that, the groundwork of glandular skin was being laid). | (H) Costal ventilation of lungs, inherited from amniote ancestor. (I) Alveolar lung trend: synapsid lungs developed millions of tiny alveoli (in later forms) for high surface area; in early synapsids, we infer an expansion of the bronchial tree and perhaps a primitive diaphragm muscle beginning to form to enhance tidal volume. The sprawling gait of early synapsids still impeded breathing while running (carrier’s constraint), so as an innovation some evolved a more upright posture in later Permian to avoid that – early synapsids likely relied on short sprints and then deep breaths. Nasal turbinates (bony scrolls) for warming and humidifying air – essential for endothermic breathing – might have had their rudimentary start in late synapsids, but earliest evidence is later. Nonetheless, the synapsid line was on the path to high-performance lungs, possibly including better separation of food and air pathways with a partial palate allowing breathing during feeding. | (H) Three-chambered heart (partial ventricular septum) from base amniotes. (I) Synapsids likely increased the degree of septation in the ventricle earlier than sauropsids, as a prelude to the four-chambered mammalian heart. Evidence: living mammals and their ancestors developed a complete septum; some late Permian synapsids (therapsids) show possible higher activity levels implying improved circulation. Early synapsids also had a higher systemic blood pressure to drive a possibly higher metabolism (some were active predators). They might have had a sinus node and AV node specialization for better heart control (inference from mammalian trait). The single systemic aortic arch (left arch in mammals) vs right in birds is a subtle circulatory divergence that began in these lineages – earliest synapsids would have two systemic arches like other amniotes, but the groundwork for streamlining to one was set in their anatomy. | (H) Amniotic egg with internal fertilization. (I) While earliest synapsids laid eggs (like reptiles), the synapsid line would eventually lead to live birth and lactation. By analogy with later pelycosaurs/therapsids, some parental care might have been present – e.g., guarding eggs or nest. The big synapsid reproductive innovation came later with the origin of mammary glands (possibly from sweat or scent glands) to feed young; earliest synapsids didn’t have milk but may have had glandular skin that could keep eggs moist or offer some nourishment to hatchlings (hypothesized intermediate steps). Additionally, synapsids likely had genetically determined sex (like mammals today), a different mode from many sauropsids’ temperature-dependent sex determination – this genetic sex determination may itself be an innovation that stabilizes sex ratios across environments. | (H) Urea as main nitrogen waste (ancestral amniote condition leaning synapsid). (I) Synapsids trended towards highly efficient kidneys with Loop of Henle (appearing in mammals) to concentrate urine. Early synapsids likely produced more urea than uric acid and had modest concentrating ability. They didn’t yet have the advanced mammalian nephron, but they may have begun retaining more water and excreting more urea in solution – a strategy that fits a perhaps more nocturnal lifestyle (where they could afford to drink water when available and didn’t need to store solid urates in eggs because some synapsids may have given live birth eventually). Also, synapsid skin lacked a thick waterproof scale coat, so they may have had to conserve water via internal means more – possibly an ancestral innovation of more active renal regulation (hormonal control via ADH-like systems to concentrate urine when needed).
R30: 20. Turtles (basal) | (H) Reptilian-grade brain (moderate size relative to body, emphasis on vision and smell). (I) Reorganized nerve pathways for neck retraction: turtles evolved a specialized cervical nerve plexus and musculature to retract the head into the shell, an action requiring precise coordination of neck vertebrae and muscles – a new neural control feature among amniotes. Their brain is still like other sauropsids, but the motor map for limbs must account for moving legs in and out of the shell (timing with breathing since shell expansion is limited). Turtles also show long-term memory and navigational ability (e.g., sea turtle migration) – indicating their neurons can support complex spatial maps, an evolutionary refinement perhaps in tandem with their longevity and slow life history. | (H) Standard reptilian digestion (stomach, intestines, etc.). (I) Beak instead of teeth: turtles lost teeth and developed a keratinous beak for cropping food. This required a strong bite force delivered by jaw muscles and a rigid skull. Herbivorous turtles have serrated beaks and elongated guts with fermentation chambers to break down plant matter – symbiotic gut bacteria help digest cellulose, an innovation allowing giant land tortoises to thrive on fibrous diets. Some aquatic turtles have specialized papillae in their throat to extract oxygen from water (buccopharyngeal respiration), technically a respiratory feature but tied to the digestive tract’s morphology. | (H) Lung breathing with no gills, like other amniotes. (I) Muscular sling breathing: because a turtle’s ribs are fused into the shell (cannot expand), turtles evolved sheets of muscle (attached to organs and shell) to pump air in and ou】. They contract certain muscles to push viscera upward, expelling air, and others to pull viscera down, drawing air in. Some turtles can also perform cloacal respiration (drawing water in and out of their cloaca where specialized sacs absorb O₂) – an innovation for prolonged underwater stays. Their lungs are large and attached to the carapace, ventilated by this unique apparatus. This system is unlike any other reptile’s and showcases an inventive solution to the constraint of a rigid shell. | (H) Typical reptile three-chambered heart (incomplete septum) with dual circuits. (I) Turtles can actively shunt blood between circuits – for example, diverting blood away from lungs when underwater for long periods. While not a full four-chamber heart, they have a functionally five-chambered heart (with a divided atria and three ventricular sub-compartments) that can shift blood flows. This is an innovation that maximizes efficiency under their variable conditions (a trait shared with many reptiles, but turtles perfected it for diving). Also, turtles have very low metabolic rates and can tolerate low oxygen (even some anaerobic metabolism during hibernation), so their circulatory system is adapted to supply tissues in low-O₂ scenarios – e.g., high buffering capacity in blood against lactic acid. | (H) Oviparous with amniotic eggs (like other reptiles). (I) Hard-shelled eggs and TSD: most turtles lay eggs with rigid calcium-carbonate shells, burying them in nests. They exhibit Temperature-Dependent Sex Determination (hatchling sex based on incubation temperature), an ancient sauropsid reproductive mode retained. Turtles do not exhibit parental care beyond nesting, which is inherited, but their long maturation time and multiple nesting attempts per season are a life-history innovation for survival in uncertain environments. Internally, female turtles can store sperm for long periods (delayed fertilization), allowing one mating to fertilize several clutches – a reproductive strategy that maximizes use of scarce mates. | (H) Uric acid–oriented excretion via kidneys and cloaca, as in other reptiles. (I) Bladder water storage: turtles, especially desert tortoises, have an oversized urinary bladder that stores water and can reabsorb it in drought conditions – essentially a canteen. They accumulate solid urates in the bladder which can be expelled when water is available. Aquatic turtles excrete more ammonia and urea (taking advantage of water to dilute waste), whereas land turtles excrete mostly uric acid paste. Some freshwater turtles can also excrete urea through specialized cloacal or pharyngeal tissues while underwater, effectively using water to wash out wastes without using their kidneys – a quirk that shows the versatility of their excretory routes. And turtle kidneys are efficient at reabsorbing salts, which helps them live in saline or brackish environments without dehydration.
R31: Species | Nervous System | Digestive System | Respiratory System | Circulatory System | Reproductive System | Excretory System
R32: 1. Earliest Cephalopods | (H) Basic molluscan nerve ring and paired ganglia controlling body functions. (I) Enlarged brain and giant nerve fibers for fast signal conduction, coordinating agile swimming and tentacle movement; image-forming eyes for predation (advanced among molluscs). | (H) Complete gut with mouth, esophagus, and radula from molluscan ancestors for scraping food. (I) Powerful chitinous beak and jaws to tear prey (predatory adaptation); a crop and stomach with digestive caecum for extra cellular digestion. Possibly an ink sac evolved for predator evasion (unique to cephalopods). | (H) Pair of gills in mantle cavity like other aquatic molluscs for gas exchange. (I) Muscular mantle pumping water over gills, enabling active respiration and jet propulsion. Developed siphuncle in shell for buoyancy control, freeing mantle for strong jet-driven breathing/outflow. | (H) Dorsal heart and blood vessels from common molluscan design. (I) Only mollusk lineage with a closed circulatory system (blood fully contained in vessels) and multiple hearts (two gill hearts plus one systemic) to support high activity​en.wikipedia.org. High-pressure blood flow improved oxygen delivery for predation. | (H) Separate sexes with external fertilization ancestral to molluscs (spawning eggs and sperm into water). (I) Evolution of internal fertilization via spermatophores (packages of sperm) to ensure fertilization in open water; larger yolky eggs laid in protective gelatinous clutches, reducing drift and predation. | (H) Nephridia (“kidneys”) filtering blood and releasing ammonia into mantle cavity water (typical aquatic waste removal). (I) Enhanced excretion to handle high-protein diet: efficient ammonia diffusion at gills. No major new organs, but likely higher renal output to eliminate metabolic wastes from active lifestyle.
R33: 2. Euthycarcinoids | (H) Arthropod ventral nerve cord with segmented ganglia and simple brain (control of appendages and senses). (I) Well-developed compound eyes and antennae for both aquatic and terrestrial sensing (facilitating amphibious foraging); improved coordination for walking on land in short bouts (integrating leg movements and balance). | (H) Straight alimentary canal with foregut (lined in chitin) and hindgut as in primitive arthropods. (I) Mandibulate mouthparts (jaws derived from appendages) for biting/chewing microbial mats or detritus; digestive ceca to aid in breaking down tough plant detritus, supporting early land herbivory/omnivory. | (H) Aquatic gills on appendages or along body (like crustacean gills) for underwater breathing. (I) Ability to breathe air briefly: possibly retained water around gills under a closeable cover or kept gills moist, enabling gas exchange in air (analogous to modern isopods). First arthropods to make short terrestrial forays. | (H) Dorsal tubular heart and open hemolymph circulation (like ancestral arthropods) with hemocyanin to carry oxygen. (I) Perhaps slightly higher hemocyanin levels or heart rate to supply oxygen during excursions on land (supporting muscle activity out of water), but overall no closed vessels (still an open system). | (H) External fertilization in water (eggs and sperm released in moist environments, as ancestral aquatic arthropods). (I) Transition toward land reproduction: eggs laid in damp mud or water’s edge to stay moist; tougher egg coatings to resist desiccation compared to fully aquatic eggs, foreshadowing terrestrial life cycle needs. | (H) Coxal glands (primitive excretory organs at leg bases) filtering hemolymph and excreting ammonia, like ancestral arthropods. (I) Partial shift to uric acid or less toxic waste when on land to avoid ammonia buildup (akin to later terrestrial arthropods). Still largely ammonia when in water, but some capacity for water retention during land travel.
R34: 3. Jawless Fish | (H) Dorsal hollow nerve cord and three-part brain from chordate ancestors (forebrain, midbrain, hindbrain). (I) Enclosed brain within a cartilaginous skull for protection; developed sense organs feeding into brain (image-forming paired eyes, inner ear balancing with 2 semicircular canals, olfactory bulbs) for active navigation and survival. | (H) Filter-feeding pharynx with gill slits from proto-chordates. (I) Muscular pharynx pump to draw in water/food, using gills now exclusively for respiration and mouth for feeding. Appearance of a true liver and pancreatic tissue (digestive glands) for improved nutrient processing in the vertebrate lineage. | (H) Multiple gill slits along the throat for gas exchange (as in ancestral chordates). (I) Gills repurposed solely for respiration (no longer for feeding) with gill filaments and rich blood supply; a muscular gill pouch system (in lampreys) or gill pouches pumping water, greatly improving oxygen uptake for a more active lifestyle than filter feeders. | (H) Basic circulation with ventral heart tube pumping blood forward (proto-heart). (I) True two-chambered heart (atrium and ventricle) evolving to push blood to gills then body; blood cells with hemoglobin appear, vastly increasing oxygen transport. This closed-loop cardiovascular innovation supports higher activity than in non-vertebrate chordates. | (H) External spawning in water – eggs and sperm released into the environment, as with amphibian-like reproduction inherited from ancestors. (I) More yolk in eggs to nourish developing larvae (e.g., lamprey eggs develop into filter-feeding larvae), and a clear metamorphosis from larval stage to adult (an extended developmental strategy unique to vertebrates). | (H) Diffusion of ammonia across gills and skin (sufficient for small ancestors). (I) Primitive kidneys (pronephros/mesonephros) with segmented nephrons that filter blood and remove waste into the cloaca. These vertebrate kidneys allow regulation of salt and water, an advance over the simple diffusion and flame cells of invertebrate precursors.
R35: 4. Bony Fish | (H) Vertebrate CNS with brain regions (cerebrum, cerebellum, etc.) similar to earlier fish. (I) Enlarged optic tectum for acute vision, and cerebellum for precise motor control of fins. Enhanced integration of sensory input (sight, lateral line, smell) supports complex behaviors like schooling and predator evasion in diverse aquatic niches. | (H) Complete digestive tract with stomach, intestine, liver, pancreas as in basal jawed fish. (I) Moveable jaws with calcified teeth for grabbing and chewing prey (first time jaws used for true biting). Many bony fish evolved a protrusible jaw for suction feeding (allowing them to slurp prey). Also, pyloric caeca (blind sacs) off the intestine increase nutrient absorption. | (H) Gill-based respiration with gill arches and filaments. (I) Bony operculum covering the gills, which pumps water over gill surfaces, allowing breathing even when stationary (major efficiency gain over sharks). Some developed a swim bladder that doubles as a lung in low-oxygen water, an air-filled organ homologous to lungs, allowing gulping air to survive oxygen-poor environments. | (H) Single-loop circulation (heart → gills → body) from ancestral fish. (I) Stronger two-chambered heart with an S-shaped ventricle for more force; addition of elastic bulbus arteriosus to smooth blood flow. Erythrocytes with hemoglobin are standard. Some bony fish later evolved accessory hearts in tail for venous return (e.g., in eels – a minor circulatory innovation). | (H) External fertilization by ancestral pattern (spawning): females release eggs and males shed sperm over them in water. (I) Complex reproductive strategies: many bony fish produce thousands of eggs with minimal yolk (broadcast spawning), while others evolved demersal sticky eggs or even mouth-brooding and nest guarding (parental care as a new behavior). Some lineages even developed internal fertilization and live birth (e.g., guppies), showing early viviparity. | (H) Kidneys and gills jointly manage waste (ammonia diffuses out at gills). (I) Advanced osmoregulation: marine bony fish drink seawater and excrete excess salt through gills; freshwater bony fish produce very dilute urine to expel water. Nitrogen waste is mostly ammonia (highly toxic but easily flushed in water) – an efficient strategy for aquatic life, with urea production in a few cases when needed (e.g., lungfish during drought).
R36: 5. Scorpions | (H) Arthropod nervous system (brain plus ventral nerve cord with segmental ganglia) inherited from chelicerate ancestors. (I) Fused ganglia forming a larger nerve mass in the cephalothorax for better integration of sensory input from multiple eyes and touch receptors. Specialized circuits control the pedipalps (pincers) and tail stinger with precise aim and timing during hunting/defense. | (H) Arthropod gut with foregut (esophagus, sucking stomach) and hindgut. (I) External digestion: scorpions secrete digestive enzymes onto prey, liquefying tissues to slurp up (pre-oral digestion adaptation). The muscular pharynx acts as a pump to suck in prey fluid. A large hepatopancreas (digestive gland) produces enzymes to break down the meal and also stores nutrients, an innovation supporting their feast-and-fast lifestyle. | (H) Book gills in aquatic ancestors (like horseshoe crabs). (I) Book lungs – internal stacked lamellae in abdominal sacs for air breathing, evolved from book gills to function in air. Four pairs of book lungs connect to outside via slit-like spiracles on the underside, enabling scorpions to live in oxygen-poor burrows. This was a key terrestrial adaptation, allowing gas exchange while conserving water. | (H) Open circulatory system with tubular heart and hemolymph, as in all arthropods. (I) Long dorsal heart with multiple ostia running through the abdomen, pushing hemolymph into sinuses including fine vessels in book lungs for gas exchange. Hemocyanin pigment carries oxygen. The system is efficient enough for a predatory but low-metabolic lifestyle, and scorpions can survive low oxygen by slowing circulation – an ancestral trait leveraged for terrestrial life. | (H) Internal fertilization via spermatophore (male deposits sperm packet on ground, then positions female to take it up) – present in early arachnids. (I) Viviparity (live birth): embryos develop inside the female, nourished by a “uterine milk” secretion. Young are born alive and crawl onto the mother’s back. This innovation (rare among invertebrates) gives the young a higher survival rate. Extended maternal care (carrying young until first molt) is also a new behavioral strategy improving offspring success. | (H) Malpighian tubules (excretory organs emptying into hindgut) from arachnid ancestors, removing wastes from hemolymph. (I) Very efficient water conservation: scorpions convert nitrogenous waste to insoluble compounds like guanine and uric acid, excreting a dry powdery feces with minimal water loss. This adaptation, along with a waxy cuticle, allows survival in deserts. They also have a rectal gland to reabsorb water, maximizing retention.
R37: Species | Nervous System | Digestive System | Respiratory System | Circulatory System | Reproductive System | Excretory System
R38: 17. Amniotes (first) | (H) Tetrapod brain with similar regions to amphibians. (I) Expanded cerebrum for more complex behavior and sensory processing on land (e.g., improved olfaction and vision integration). The loss of lateral line systems freed up neural resources for other senses. Enhanced motor control for more agile locomotion appears (as early amniotes were more active runners than amphibians). Overall, the nervous system became better at terrestrial life – faster reflexes, better balance (thanks to a longer neck providing more sensory input about head position), and more intricate social/mating behaviors driven by a bigger forebrain. | (H) Carnivorous or insectivorous diet (simple gut) like many amphibians. (I) Salivary glands well-developed – amniotes moisten food and begin digestion in the mouth, a new feature since swallowing dry food required lubrication. A muscular tongue and stronger jaws allowed amniotes to bite off pieces of food (some early amniotes had small peg-like teeth suited to insects). The longer neck enabled them to reach food and manipulate it better. Also, in some early amniotes, neck muscles helped swallow (since they couldn’t use water to wash food down), an innovation for fully terrestrial feeding. | (H) Lungs as primary respiratory organ (no gills in adults), inherited from tetrapod ancestors. (I) Costal (rib) breathing: amniotes used muscles between the ribs (intercostals) to expand the chest and draw in air, rather than throat pumpin】. This innovation allowed a longer neck and larger lungs, since air could be pulled through a long trachea. The throat was freed from breathing duty and could be used for feeding/vocalizing. The lungs themselves became more compartmentalized (increasing surface area with inner folds) to support higher metabolism. Some amniotes (later synapsids) even added a diaphragm-like muscle for even more efficient ventilation – but the key first step was rib-powered inhalation, markedly improving respiratory capacity on land. | (H) Partial separation of circuits as in amphibians. (I) High-pressure, dual-circuit circulation: amniote hearts evolved a septum that was more developed, especially in active species, to separate oxygenated and deoxygenated blood. Early amniotes likely had a 3-chambered heart with an improved septum in the ventricle (and some vascular shunts). This delivered higher blood pressure to the body, crucial for animals with higher activity and raised posture. Blood was thus more efficiently oxygenated from lungs and delivered to tissues. Over time, this would lead to the fully four-chambered heart (in later archosaurs and mammals), but even the earliest sauropsids and synapsids showed circulatory refinement beyond amphibians. | (H) Still laid eggs in water or very damp areas (ancestral condition). (I) Amniotic egg: the defining innovation – eggs enclosed in a shell with internal membranes, allowing them to be laid on land without drying ou】. The amnion membrane forms a fluid-filled sac around the embryo (providing a “pond” for development】, the chorion facilitates gas exchange through the shel】, and the allantois stores nitrogenous waste and aids respiratio】. Early amniote eggs likely had leathery shells (as in modern reptiles) and required humid environments, but they freed the lineage from returning to water for reproduction. Internal fertilization became obligatory (sperm must fertilize the egg before the shell forms), leading to the evolution of intromittent organs (penis) or cloacal apposition for mating – another departure from amphibians. | (H) Kidneys similar to amphibian’s but improved concentration ability. (I) Water-conserving excretion: early amniotes shifted toward uric acid excretion (in sauropsids】 or concentrated urea (in synapsids), to package nitrogen waste in a less toxic, less water-dependent form within the eggshell (embryos cannot eliminate ammonia easily in a closed egg). The embryonic allantois stores waste safely away from the developing embry】. In adults, amniote kidneys evolved longer tubules and (in synapsids) beginnings of loops to concentrate urine. Many early reptiles also had salt glands to excrete excess ions from diet, an innovation for life in arid or marine environments. Overall, amniotes could retain water far better than amphibians – a critical factor in fully colonizing land.
R39: 18. Earliest Sauropsids | (H) Basal amniote brain plan. (I) Vision-centric brain: early sauropsids (reptile line) likely had very keen vision (large optic tecta), as modern reptiles and birds are visual hunters. Color vision was probably well-developed (many sauropsids have four cone types). The forebrain (pallium) in sauropsids started to develop a different layout than synapsids, with the first signs of the dorsal ventricular ridge – an area that in birds/reptiles handles visual and spatial processing. These neural tweaks suggest sauropsids were adapting to daytime, visually guided activity, in contrast to synapsid’s nocturnal tendency. | (H) Carnivorous/insectivorous diet. (I) Some early sauropsids (like Captorhinus in the Permian) evolved multiple rows of teeth on the jaws, possibly to grind plant material – an early experiment in herbivory. In general, sauropsids maintained a simple gut for meat, but those dabbling in plants likely had longer intestines or fermentation chambers. Sauropsids also evolved keratinous horny beaks or pads in some lineages (turtles, later) – the beginnings seen in herbivorous captorhinids with thickened jaw bones and roughened bone that suggest a beak. Salivary glands in sauropsids sometimes included venom glands (by Late Permian some reptiles were venomous) – a novel digestive aid for subduing prey. | (H) Costal lung ventilation (rib movement) as in ancestral amniotes. (I) Faveolar lungs: sauropsids tend to have lungs with a spongy interior of faveoli (pocket-like chambers) rather than alveoli. Early sauropsids likely developed more subdivided lungs to increase surface area. They also had relatively narrow, elongated ribcages, indicating efficient airflow. Some may have evolved accessory air sacs or unidirectional airflow earlier than birds/crocs, but evidence is scant for the very first sauropsids. We do see in later sauropsids (archosaurs) the seeds of one-way airflow. But initially, the key respiratory edge was simply a dry, keratinized airway and the ability to take deeper breaths, enabling higher activity in arid environments than amphibians could manage. | (H) Improved three-chambered heart from base amniotes. (I) Selective shunting: many sauropsids can direct blood flow dynamically (e.g., away from lungs when diving). Early sauropsids had the beginnings of this ability, via partial septa and arterial bypasses. Their blood pressure was higher than amphibians, enabling a more active life and higher stance. While the earliest sauropsids didn’t yet have a full four-chamber heart, the groundwork was laid. Over the Permian, some lineages (ancestors of crocodilians) improved the septum. But even the “anapsid” grade sauropsids had coronary arteries feeding the heart muscle (a sign of higher metabolism) and a division of the ventricle during contraction, indicating a step toward endotherm-level circulation (realized much later in birds). | (H) Amniotic egg with internal fertilization, as per the clade. (I) Hard-shelled eggs: early sauropsids probably still had soft, leathery shells, but the move toward calcium carbonate stiff shells (like in dinosaurs and modern birds) is a sauropsid trend. A harder shell provides better protection and allows parents to bury or even brood eggs. Sauropsids also pioneered temperature-dependent sex determination (seen in modern turtles and many reptiles) – this may have been present in the earliest sauropsids, using incubation temperature to influence sex ratios (a unique reproductive twist not found in synapsids). Parental care in earliest sauropsids was minimal (likely just choosing good sites for eggs), but the stage was set for later reptiles to guard nests (as some Permian pareiasaurs might have). | (H) Uric acid–based excretion (uricotelism) typical of reptiles, to conserve water. (I) Bladder for water reabsorption: most sauropsids evolved a urinary bladder that can reclaim water from urine, allowing them to live in dry conditions. Also, early sauropsids had relatively small glomeruli in kidneys – an adaptation to produce less initial filtrate and thus less water loss. The solid uric acid waste could be expelled as a semi-solid paste along with feces, an efficient strategy. Some early sauropsids in coastal areas likely evolved salt glands (e.g., in the nose or near eyes) to excrete excess salt from a carnivorous diet, an innovation that let them drink saline water or eat salty prey without dehydrating.
R40: 19. Earliest Synapsids | (H) Similar basic brain to other amniotes. (I) Olfactory emphasis: synapsids (mammal lineage) placed greater importance on smell; their olfactory bulbs enlarged, indicating these early synapsids (like Archaeothyris) were possibly nocturnal hunters using scent. The synapsid brain began developing a different cortex (pallium) structure, which would later become the mammalian neocortex – at this stage, just slight increases in brain folding and size, particularly in areas for smell and perhaps hearing. The single temporal fenestra in skull allowed a larger jaw muscle, but also potentially provided space for expanded jaw muscle proprioceptors and nerves – improving neural feedback for precise chewing, a prelude to later complex mastication. | (H) Insectivorous/carnivorous diet with modestly complex gut. (I) Differentiated teeth (heterodonty): even the earliest synapsids showed beginnings of distinct tooth types – notably large canines for piercing prey, and smaller rear teeth for cutting (not yet true molars, but headed that way). This is an innovation for more efficient food processing. Jaw mechanics also shifted: the synapsid jaw became more muscular and began to chew, not just snap. To support higher feeding rates (if endothermy was creeping in), some later synapsids evolved a secondary palate to eat and breathe simultaneously – though earliest ones likely had only a partial palate. Another digestive innovation down the line was milk (though earliest synapsids didn’t have that, the groundwork of glandular skin was being laid). | (H) Costal ventilation of lungs, inherited from amniote ancestor. (I) Alveolar lung trend: synapsid lungs developed millions of tiny alveoli (in later forms) for high surface area; in early synapsids, we infer an expansion of the bronchial tree and perhaps a primitive diaphragm muscle beginning to form to enhance tidal volume. The sprawling gait of early synapsids still impeded breathing while running (carrier’s constraint), so as an innovation some evolved a more upright posture in later Permian to avoid that – early synapsids likely relied on short sprints and then deep breaths. Nasal turbinates (bony scrolls) for warming and humidifying air – essential for endothermic breathing – might have had their rudimentary start in late synapsids, but earliest evidence is later. Nonetheless, the synapsid line was on the path to high-performance lungs, possibly including better separation of food and air pathways with a partial palate allowing breathing during feeding. | (H) Three-chambered heart (partial ventricular septum) from base amniotes. (I) Synapsids likely increased the degree of septation in the ventricle earlier than sauropsids, as a prelude to the four-chambered mammalian heart. Evidence: living mammals and their ancestors developed a complete septum; some late Permian synapsids (therapsids) show possible higher activity levels implying improved circulation. Early synapsids also had a higher systemic blood pressure to drive a possibly higher metabolism (some were active predators). They might have had a sinus node and AV node specialization for better heart control (inference from mammalian trait). The single systemic aortic arch (left arch in mammals) vs right in birds is a subtle circulatory divergence that began in these lineages – earliest synapsids would have two systemic arches like other amniotes, but the groundwork for streamlining to one was set in their anatomy. | (H) Amniotic egg with internal fertilization. (I) While earliest synapsids laid eggs (like reptiles), the synapsid line would eventually lead to live birth and lactation. By analogy with later pelycosaurs/therapsids, some parental care might have been present – e.g., guarding eggs or nest. The big synapsid reproductive innovation came later with the origin of mammary glands (possibly from sweat or scent glands) to feed young; earliest synapsids didn’t have milk but may have had glandular skin that could keep eggs moist or offer some nourishment to hatchlings (hypothesized intermediate steps). Additionally, synapsids likely had genetically determined sex (like mammals today), a different mode from many sauropsids’ temperature-dependent sex determination – this genetic sex determination may itself be an innovation that stabilizes sex ratios across environments. | (H) Urea as main nitrogen waste (ancestral amniote condition leaning synapsid). (I) Synapsids trended towards highly efficient kidneys with Loop of Henle (appearing in mammals) to concentrate urine. Early synapsids likely produced more urea than uric acid and had modest concentrating ability. They didn’t yet have the advanced mammalian nephron, but they may have begun retaining more water and excreting more urea in solution – a strategy that fits a perhaps more nocturnal lifestyle (where they could afford to drink water when available and didn’t need to store solid urates in eggs because some synapsids may have given live birth eventually). Also, synapsid skin lacked a thick waterproof scale coat, so they may have had to conserve water via internal means more – possibly an ancestral innovation of more active renal regulation (hormonal control via ADH-like systems to concentrate urine when needed).
R41: 20. Turtles (basal) | (H) Reptilian-grade brain (moderate size relative to body, emphasis on vision and smell). (I) Reorganized nerve pathways for neck retraction: turtles evolved a specialized cervical nerve plexus and musculature to retract the head into the shell, an action requiring precise coordination of neck vertebrae and muscles – a new neural control feature among amniotes. Their brain is still like other sauropsids, but the motor map for limbs must account for moving legs in and out of the shell (timing with breathing since shell expansion is limited). Turtles also show long-term memory and navigational ability (e.g., sea turtle migration) – indicating their neurons can support complex spatial maps, an evolutionary refinement perhaps in tandem with their longevity and slow life history. | (H) Standard reptilian digestion (stomach, intestines, etc.). (I) Beak instead of teeth: turtles lost teeth and developed a keratinous beak for cropping food. This required a strong bite force delivered by jaw muscles and a rigid skull. Herbivorous turtles have serrated beaks and elongated guts with fermentation chambers to break down plant matter – symbiotic gut bacteria help digest cellulose, an innovation allowing giant land tortoises to thrive on fibrous diets. Some aquatic turtles have specialized papillae in their throat to extract oxygen from water (buccopharyngeal respiration), technically a respiratory feature but tied to the digestive tract’s morphology. | (H) Lung breathing with no gills, like other amniotes. (I) Muscular sling breathing: because a turtle’s ribs are fused into the shell (cannot expand), turtles evolved sheets of muscle (attached to organs and shell) to pump air in and ou】. They contract certain muscles to push viscera upward, expelling air, and others to pull viscera down, drawing air in. Some turtles can also perform cloacal respiration (drawing water in and out of their cloaca where specialized sacs absorb O₂) – an innovation for prolonged underwater stays. Their lungs are large and attached to the carapace, ventilated by this unique apparatus. This system is unlike any other reptile’s and showcases an inventive solution to the constraint of a rigid shell. | (H) Typical reptile three-chambered heart (incomplete septum) with dual circuits. (I) Turtles can actively shunt blood between circuits – for example, diverting blood away from lungs when underwater for long periods. While not a full four-chamber heart, they have a functionally five-chambered heart (with a divided atria and three ventricular sub-compartments) that can shift blood flows. This is an innovation that maximizes efficiency under their variable conditions (a trait shared with many reptiles, but turtles perfected it for diving). Also, turtles have very low metabolic rates and can tolerate low oxygen (even some anaerobic metabolism during hibernation), so their circulatory system is adapted to supply tissues in low-O₂ scenarios – e.g., high buffering capacity in blood against lactic acid. | (H) Oviparous with amniotic eggs (like other reptiles). (I) Hard-shelled eggs and TSD: most turtles lay eggs with rigid calcium-carbonate shells, burying them in nests. They exhibit Temperature-Dependent Sex Determination (hatchling sex based on incubation temperature), an ancient sauropsid reproductive mode retained. Turtles do not exhibit parental care beyond nesting, which is inherited, but their long maturation time and multiple nesting attempts per season are a life-history innovation for survival in uncertain environments. Internally, female turtles can store sperm for long periods (delayed fertilization), allowing one mating to fertilize several clutches – a reproductive strategy that maximizes use of scarce mates. | (H) Uric acid–oriented excretion via kidneys and cloaca, as in other reptiles. (I) Bladder water storage: turtles, especially desert tortoises, have an oversized urinary bladder that stores water and can reabsorb it in drought conditions – essentially a canteen. They accumulate solid urates in the bladder which can be expelled when water is available. Aquatic turtles excrete more ammonia and urea (taking advantage of water to dilute waste), whereas land turtles excrete mostly uric acid paste. Some freshwater turtles can also excrete urea through specialized cloacal or pharyngeal tissues while underwater, effectively using water to wash out wastes without using their kidneys – a quirk that shows the versatility of their excretory routes. And turtle kidneys are efficient at reabsorbing salts, which helps them live in saline or brackish environments without dehydration.
R42: Species | Nervous System | Digestive System | Respiratory System | Circulatory System | Reproductive System | Excretory System |  | Endocrine System | Immune System | Integumentary System | Skeletal System | Muscular System | Sensory System

R45: Crocodiles (Archosaur reptiles) | • Cerebrum and limbic system moderately developed – enables complex social behavior like parental care (innovation among reptiles). ⏎ • Strong instinctual patterns (e.g. guarding nests, vocal communication with young – innovation). ⏎ • Brain still small relative to body, but efficient; capable of learning simple routines (inherited). | • Very acidic stomach (pH ~1–2) – one of the strongest of any animal, dissolving bones (innovation for digesting whole prey)​facebook.com. ⏎ • Gizzard-like pylorus with gastroliths (stones) to grind food • Semi-erect posture – can lift body for faster gait (minor innovation among reptiles). | • Massive jaw muscles for one of the strongest bites in animal kingdom (innovation)​nationalgeographic.com. ⏎ • Tail muscles (laterally flattened tail) provide powerful swimming thrust (innovation for aquatic locomotion). ⏎ • Diaphragmaticus muscle (unique to crocs) to pump lungs and adjust buoyancy (innovation)​phys.org. | • Excellent night vision (large pupils, tapetum lucidum reflecting layer – innovation) for nocturnal hunting. ⏎ • Sensitive hearing – mothers hear hatchling calls from inside eggs (innovation). ⏎ • ISOs sensory pits on snout detect slightest ripples, allowing strikes in total darkness​nationalgeographic.com​nationalgeographic.com (innovation).
R46: Dinosaurs (non-avian, Mesozoic) | • Increasing brain size in some (e.g. small theropods like Troodon had large brains for their size – innovation), but many large dinos had small brains (inherited limitation). ⏎ • Complex behaviors: herding in hadrosaurs, nesting colonies, and brooding (fossil evidence) – indicates advanced brain functions for social behavior (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Balance and coordination refined in bipedal forms via expanded cerebellum (innovation). | • Diverse diets: herbivores developed gastroliths in stomach to grind tough plant material (innovation), and some (hadrosaurs, ceratopsians) evolved dental batteries – tightly packed teeth that self-sharpen for grinding (innovation). ⏎ • Carnivores had serrated teeth to slice flesh (inherited from reptiles, optimized in theropods). ⏎ • No chewing in most – food swallowed in chunks (inherited), but some had cheek-like structures to retain food while chewing (possible innovation in some ornithischians). | • Avian-style air sac system in many saurischians (theropods and sauropods) – hollow bones evidence suggests they had flow-through lungs with air sacs for continuous one-way airflow (innovation)​journals.plos.org.​journals.plos.org ⏎ • High oxygen demand: likely four-phase breathing like birds (innovation), enabling active lifestyles. Others (ornithischians) had less air sac development but still efficient lungs (inherited archosaur trait). ⏎ • Some gigantothermy: large dinos could sustain stable body temps due to size, reducing need for rapid breathing (inherited strategy of ectotherms supplemented by mass homeothermy). | • Four-chambered heart (inferred for all archosaurs, including dinos – likely innovation for high metabolism)​fountainmagazine.com. ⏎ • High blood pressure in tall sauropods to pump blood to heads (innovation: very large hearts and perhaps accessory pumps). ⏎ • Haversian bone structure in fast-growing dinos suggests high metabolic rate supported by vigorous circulation (innovation toward endothermy). | • Egg-laying with hard-shelled eggs (inherited from archosaur ancestors). ⏎ • Elaborate nesting: fossils of brooding (e.g. oviraptorids sitting on nests) show incubation behavior (innovation) – adults regulated egg temperature like birds. ⏎ • Some young altricial (requiring care) – evidence of juveniles in nests fed by parents (innovation), vs others precocial (inherited reptilian style). | • Uricotelic like other reptiles: excreted uric acid paste via kidneys and cloaca (inherited). ⏎ • Salt glands in some (e.g. marine or desert dinos) to excrete excess salt (possible innovation by convergence with birds). ⏎ • Large herbivores had immense gut systems – likely copious urine/feces processed, with water reabsorption in hindgut (inherited reptile trait). |  | • Possible endothermy in some (small theropods) – implying thyroid-driven high metabolism (innovation). ⏎ • Growth hormones: evidence of fast juvenile growth in bone rings suggests intense endocrine regulation (innovation allowing some species to reach adult size quickly). ⏎ • Reproductive hormones cyclical – synchronized mass nesting seasons (inferred, like modern birds). | • Robust immune defenses needed in dense populations – likely good wound healing and innate immunity (inferred). ⏎ • Bone marrow in dinosaurs produced immune cells as in birds (inherited). ⏎ • Gut microbiome likely contributed to immunity in herbivores (inherited concept). | • Feathers or proto-feathers present in many theropods (and even some ornithischians) – initially for insulation or display (innovation), later co-opted for flight in avian lineage.​sciencedaily.com ⏎ • Scales in other dinos (inherited reptilian trait) for body covering; some had both scales and feather-like filaments. ⏎ • Colorful display structures (frills, crests, possibly colored feathers) – used for species recognition and mating displays (innovation in integument for social signaling). | • Erect limb posture – legs positioned under the body (innovation vs sprawling gait), allowing efficient locomotion and gigantic body sizes. ⏎ • Light but strong bones: many bones hollow with internal struts (especially in saurischians) for strength and lightness (innovation, related to air sacs)​journals.plos.org. ⏎ • Specialized joints: extra hip vertebrae support (ornithischian “bird-hip” structure) and flexible necks in sauropods (elongated cervical vertebrae with air cavities – innovation). | • Massive locomotor muscles in hindlimbs of bipedal dinos for running (e.g. thick caudofemoralis muscle in tail powering legs – inherited archosaur trait, enlarged). ⏎ • Shoulder and breast muscles enlarged in some theropods for predation (holding prey) and in others (early fliers like Archaeopteryx) for flapping (incipient flight muscles – innovation). ⏎ • Gigantic herbivores had pillar-like limb muscles emphasizing support over speed (inherited strategy scaled up). | • Sharp color vision – likely tetrachromatic like birds (innovation carried from archosaur lineage) enabling identification of mates and ripe fruits. ⏎ • Acute hearing in some (cochlear lengths in fossils suggest certain theropods heard well, possibly in bird-like frequency range – innovation) and others had poorer hearing (inherited variability). ⏎ • Strong smell: large olfactory bulbs in Tyrannosaurus suggest excellent olfaction for tracking prey (innovation in predators).
R47: Earliest Mammals (Triassic small mammals) | • Expanded cerebral cortex relative to reptiles – improved learning and sensory processing (innovation). ⏎ • Excellent olfaction and hearing centers: nocturnal lifestyle drove a larger olfactory bulb and auditory cortex​en.wikipedia.org (innovation). ⏎ • Still small brain overall, but brain-to-body ratio increased compared to dinosaurs (innovation trend). | • Heterodont dentition – differentiated teeth (incisors, canines, cheek teeth) for varied diet (innovation vs uniform reptile teeth). They could nip, tear, and chew somewhat, enhancing food processing.​en.wikipedia.org ⏎ • Lactation appears: infants drank mothers’ milk (major innovation), although early mammals had no nipples – milk secreted onto fur (monotreme-like)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Likely insectivorous or omnivorous – simple stomach, short gut (inherited from insectivorous ancestors). | • Lungs with diaphragm starting to form – early mammals evolved a muscular diaphragm to pump lungs efficiently (innovation). ⏎ • Turbinate bones in nose to retain moisture and heat during breathing (innovation aiding endothermy). ⏎ • High breathing rates to support warm-blooded metabolism (innovation compared to reptiles). | • Four-chambered heart fully separating circuits (innovation inherited from earlier synapsids, fully utilized for endothermy). ⏎ • High blood pressure and rapid circulation to sustain higher metabolic rate (innovation). ⏎ • Blood with biconcave red blood cells (no nucleus) to carry more oxygen – likely present (innovation aligning with endothermy). | • Viviparity emerges: some early mammals may have birthed live young (proto-therians), but earliest ones (like Morganucodon) likely still laid eggs or had very tiny young (transitional). ⏎ • Milk production to nourish young – mammary glands with nutrient-rich milk (innovation enabling altricial young to survive). ⏎ • Epipubic bones present (as in monotremes/marsupials) restricting pregnancy length; earliest placentals later lost them to allow longer gestation​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. | • Urea excretion replaces uric acid – early mammals likely shifted to less toxic urea, needing more water but compatible with their nocturnal, moisture-rich environments (innovation vs reptiles)​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • Loops of Henle in kidney evolving to concentrate urine moderately (early innovation toward efficient kidneys). ⏎ • Separate anal and urethral openings (ureters to bladder) apart from reproductive tract, though monotreme-like cloaca still possible in earliest forms (transition stage). |  | • Endothermy (warm-bloodedness) – ability to generate internal heat via high metabolism (major innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. Likely fully evolved by late Triassic (~233 Mya)​scientificamerican.com. ⏎ • Hair follicle glands (sebaceous, apocrine) present, secreting oils for fur and possibly pheromones (innovation). ⏎ • Thyroid & adrenal glands more developed to regulate higher metabolic rate (innovation). | • Improved innate immunity – fur reduces ectoparasites, and higher body temp (≈30–35°C in early mammals) inhibits some infections (innovation). ⏎ • Adaptive immunity advanced: bone marrow and spleen producing diverse lymphocytes (inherited from synapsids, refined). ⏎ • Maternal antibodies possibly transferred via milk, granting pups passive immunity (innovation). | • Fur (hair) for insulation – a new integumentary feature indicating endothermy (major innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Whiskers (vibrissae) on snout – tactile sensors in the fur, evidence of which seen in small Triassic mammals (innovation to navigate in dark)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Mammary hairs – milk secreted onto patches of fur (in monotreme-like fashion)​en.wikipedia.org, early form of mammary gland (innovation). | • Jaw joint restructured: dentary-squamosal joint formed, while quadrate and articular bones repurposed into middle ear ossicles (malleus, incus) – a hallmark mammalian innovation improving hearing​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Three middle ear bones fully detached from jaw, increasing hearing sensitivity (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Seven neck vertebrae (inherited constraint of mammals) and flexible backbone for agility (innovation: more dorso-ventral flexion for running). | • Diaphragm muscle developed, separating chest and abdomen – greatly improves breathing efficiency (innovation). ⏎ • Differentiated facial muscles – allowed suckling (lips forming seal to nurse, an innovation in feeding young). ⏎ • Postural muscles adapted for sustained activity – early mammals could sustain exercise to forage at night (innovation, ties to endothermy). | • Highly sensitive hearing – three-ossicle middle ear and outer ear pinna (if present) gave acute hearing of high frequencies (innovation aiding nocturnal life). ⏎ • Strong sense of smell – large olfactory bulb and turbinates indicate powerful olfaction for finding food and mates in the dark​en.wikipedia.org (innovation). ⏎ • Vision likely rod-dominated (nocturnal bottleneck): good night vision but poor color (inherited constraint); later mammals re-evolved color in daytime niches.
R48: Lizards & more Reptiles (Squamates) | • Mid-sized brains – larger than amphibians but smaller than mammals; capable of learning simple tasks (inherited reptile brain). ⏎ • Vomeronasal organ well-developed – tongue flicking delivers scent particles, integrated in brain for acute chemosensing (innovation). ⏎ • Reflex-driven prey capture (rapid visual processing for hunting insects, inherited). | • Mostly carnivorous/insectivorous – simple tubular gut (inherited) with strong stomach acid for whole prey digestion (inherited). ⏎ • Some herbivorous lizards evolved longer guts and hindgut fermentation chambers (ceca) to break down plant cellulose (innovation). ⏎ • Kinetic skulls in many lizards (movable jaw joints) to swallow large prey (innovation). | • Lung ventilation by ribs (like other reptiles, inherited). Small lizards may also use throat pumping to assist breathing when running (innovation). ⏎ • Some developed air sacs in lungs (e.g. monitors) for more efficient gas exchange (minor innovation). ⏎ • Cutaneous respiration negligible – dry skin (inherited constraint, rely fully on lungs). | • Three-chambered heart with partial septum in some (e.g. monitors) – moderate separation of blood (inherited baseline). ⏎ • Lymphatic heart present: accessory pumps move lymph (innovation in some lizards). ⏎ • Relatively lower blood pressure than mammals (ectothermic metabolic needs, inherited). | • Hemipenes in males – paired intromittent organs (innovation of squamates) for internal fertilization. ⏎ • Oviparity common: lay flexible or rigid-shelled eggs buried in soil (inherited reptile trait); some lizards and many snakes later evolved viviparity (live birth) as an innovation in harsh climates. ⏎ • Minimal parental care – most abandon eggs (inherited), though a few guard nests (minor innovation). | • Uric acid excretion via kidneys and cloaca (inherited reptile trait) – thick, whitish paste in droppings. ⏎ • Salt glands in marine iguanas and desert lizards excrete excess salt (innovation for osmoregulation). ⏎ • Cloaca expels feces and urine together (inherited). |  | • Ecdysis (molting) regulated by thyroid hormones – periodic shedding of skin (innovation in growth). ⏎ • Temperature-dependent metabolism – endocrine control (thyroid) adjusts metabolic enzymes with ambient temperature (inherited ectothermy). ⏎ • Reproductive cycles timed by hormones and often linked to seasonal rainfall (inherited strategy). | • Strong innate immunity – scales prevent infection entry (inherited physical barrier). ⏎ • Wound healing is efficient but slower than in mammals (ectothermic constraint). ⏎ • Some lizards can autotomize tails to escape predators, with minimal bleeding and infection – indicates effective clotting and healing (innovation). | • Keratin scales overlapping to protect and reduce water loss (inherited reptile skin). ⏎ • Color-changing in chameleons via chromatophores under neural/endocrine control (innovation for camouflage and thermoregulation). ⏎ • Regenerative tails in many – integument regrows with cartilage rod and scales after tail drop (innovation in tissue regeneration). | • Flexible skull and jaw (especially in snakes, a subset of squamates) – multiple joints and elastic ligaments allow large prey swallowing (innovation). ⏎ • Epiphyseal plates in bones for determinate growth (stop growing at adulthood, unlike many reptiles – some squamates approach this, minor innovation). ⏎ • Feet with toepads in geckos (innovation: Van der Waals setae for adhesion) enabling climbing. | • Segmented limb muscles enabling quick bursts of speed to catch prey (inherited). ⏎ • Autotomy muscles in tail – pre-formed break points and muscle constrictors to detach tail and later regrow (innovation). ⏎ • Thoracic muscles also aid in standing display (e.g. push-ups in lizards), combining locomotion and signaling (inherited, repurposed behavior). | • Vision often excellent – many diurnal lizards have color vision (4 cones) and UV detection (inherited from reptilian ancestors). ⏎ • Forked tongue + vomeronasal organ in snakes and some lizards for acute smell/taste of air (innovation) – can detect prey trails.​en.wikipedia.org ⏎ • Infrared heat-sensing pits evolved in boas, pythons, and pit vipers to detect warm-blooded prey (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org.
R49: Monotremes (Platypus, Echidna) | • Mix of reptilian and mammalian traits: brain relatively small, no corpus callosum connecting hemispheres (inherited primitive trait)​en.wikipedia.org, but highly developed sensory regions (e.g. tactile bill in platypus – innovation). ⏎ • Electroreception processing in platypus brain – unique sensory neurons map in cortex (innovation). ⏎ • Capable of REM and non-REM sleep, indicating mammalian neural organization (inherited). | • Edentulous (toothless) as adults – platypus has grinding plates, echidna has none; food (invertebrates) ground between tongue and palate (inherited primitive trait)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Keratinous pads/spines in mouth to grind food (innovation replacing teeth). ⏎ • Cloaca present – single opening for digestive and urinary wastes (inherited reptile-like feature)​en.wikipedia.org. | • Lungs and diaphragm like other mammals (inherited mammal trait), so monotremes do use a diaphragm for breathing (though body temperature is lower). ⏎ • Lower metabolic rate means slower breathing rate; can also enter torpor (echidnas hibernate) – endocrine regulated slowing of respiration (innovation for energy saving). ⏎ • Nostrils at snout tip (platypus) or on face (echidna) – can close underwater (adaptation for diving in platypus). | • Four-chambered heart (like all mammals, inherited). ⏎ • Low body temperature (~32°C), so heart rate and blood pressure are lower than in placentals (innovation – secondarily lower metabolism)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Large azygous vein in platypus, aiding venous return from their sprawled posture (adaptation). | • Oviparous mammals – females lay small, leathery eggs (inherited reptilian trait). Eggs hatch after short incubation, and tiny young nurse on milk (innovation: mammalian lactation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • No nipples – milk “sweated” from mammary gland openings on belly fur patches, licked by young​en.wikipedia.org (primitive mammal trait). ⏎ • Cloaca (“monotreme” means one opening) for reproductive tract (inherited)​en.wikipedia.org; males have penis but only used for semen transfer, retracted otherwise. | • Urea excretion (like other mammals, inherited) – kidneys produce urea, excreted via cloaca with feces (urine and feces mix in cloaca). ⏎ • No urinary bladder in platypus (urine stored in cloaca/rectum area briefly), echidna has a rudimentary bladder; overall still cloacal (reptilian-like) system (inherited). ⏎ • Electrolyte regulation: kidneys less efficient than placentals, but sufficient given their lower metabolism (inherited). |  | • Lower basal metabolism – body temp ~32°C; thyroid activity is lower than in placentals (innovation or retention of primitive condition). ⏎ • Venom gland in male platypus (crural gland on hind leg) produces peptide venom (seasonal, used in mating fights – unique endocrine/exocrine innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Milk production controlled by prolactin as in other mammals (inherited) – monotreme milk contains antimicrobial proteins unique to them (innovation for protecting young). | • Innate immunity functioning at lower body temp – monotreme immune cells operate a bit slower but effectively. ⏎ • Platypus venom (from male spur) is made of defensin-like proteins​en.wikipedia.org, evolved from immune molecules – interesting link between immune peptides and venom (innovation). ⏎ • Long-lived for their size (echidnas ~50 years) – suggests effective immune surveillance against cancers/infections (speculative). | • Fur for insulation (inherited mammal trait) – dense fur on platypus (waterproof underfur), spines (modified hairs) on echidna for defense (innovation). ⏎ • Spur on hind ankle (males) connected to venom gland – unique integumentary weapon (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Mammary patch – no nipple, milk oozes through skin; hairy patch helps spread milk for young​en.wikipedia.org (primitive mammary structure). | • Shoulder girdle retains ancestral bones (interclavicle, coracoids) lost in other mammals – more reptile-like skeletal layout (inherited)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Epipubic bones present (like marsupials) to support abdominal muscles or egg-carrying (inherited primitive trait). ⏎ • Legs held somewhat sprawled (especially in platypus front legs) – gives reptilian gait in water and land (inherited trait). | • Platypus bill muscles highly refined for electrosensory guided motion – bill moves in scanning pattern underwater (innovation in feeding muscles). ⏎ • Echidna forelimbs power digging – strong claws and burrowing muscles (innovation for fossorial life). ⏎ • Spur muscles in male platypus can inject venom during breeding season (muscular control of venom delivery – innovation). | • Electroreception in platypus: ~40,000 electroreceptors in bill skin detect prey’s electric signals – only mammal with this sense (major sensory innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Echidna snout has electroreceptors too (fewer) and a keen sense of smell for finding ants (inherited/modified). ⏎ • Poor vision in monotremes – small eyes, rely more on other senses (inherited primitive trait).
R50: Marsupials (Kangaroos, Koalas, etc.) | • Moderately sized brains – smaller than placental mammals of similar body size (especially reduced cerebral cortex folding, inherited constraint). ⏎ • No corpus callosum connecting brain halves (like monotremes)​en.wikipedia.org – neural fibers cross via anterior commissure instead (primitive trait). ⏎ • Strong olfaction and hearing – nocturnal ancestry maintained keen smell and mobile ears (inherited trait). | • Varied diets: some are herbivores (kangaroos, koalas) with large chambered stomachs or elongated cecum for fermentation (innovation similar to ruminants), others carnivorous (Tasmanian devil) with short gut (inherited mammal trait). ⏎ • Dentition often specialized: e.g. kangaroos have high-crowned molars for grinding tough grass (innovation), opossums have 50 teeth for omnivory (inherited generalized trait). ⏎ • Lactation highly tuned: milk composition changes as joey develops in pouch (innovation – early milk high in simple sugars, later milk richer in fats/protein). | • Lungs and diaphragm like other mammals (inherited); breathing rates linked to hopping in kangaroos (they synchronize breathing with hops to save energy – innovation). ⏎ • Lower metabolic rate on average than placentals, so slightly lower lung ventilation needs (inherited trait). ⏎ • Nasal turbinates present for water conservation (inherited). | • Four-chambered heart (inherited mammalian). ⏎ • Many marsupials have lower metabolic output, thus slightly larger red blood cells and lower blood pressure than placentals (adaptation to lower O₂ needs). ⏎ • Double circulation fully developed (inherited). | • Short gestation – young born extremely underdeveloped, essentially embryo-like (marsupial hallmark innovation). ⏎ • Pouch (marsupium) in most: newborns crawl to nipple in pouch, latch on and continue development (innovation – external womb). ⏎ • Bifurcated reproductive tract: females have two uteruses and two vaginas; males often have bifid (forked) penis – unique marsupial anatomy (innovation), linked to giving birth to very small young. | • Urea excretion (inherited mammal trait) – kidneys concentrate urine moderately. ⏎ • Many marsupials conserve water well (desert species produce very concentrated urine – innovation). ⏎ • Cloaca in marsupials: not truly separate openings – single opening for excretion and reproduction (partial retention of primitive trait, though some have a slight separation internally). |  | • Gestation mainly external means progesterone supports early pregnancy briefly, then lactation hormones (prolactin, oxytocin) dominate for pouch life – highly specialized endocrine shift (innovation). ⏎ • Embryonic diapause in some (kangaroos): can pause development of a new embryo until pouch young vacates, via hormonal control (innovation). ⏎ • Adrenal and thyroid glands functional but marsupials often have slightly lower body temps and metabolism than placentals (endocrine level inherited trait). | • Immune transfer via milk – since young spend long in pouch with immature immune systems, mothers’ milk provides antibodies (innovation akin to placentals’ placental transfer) to protect young. ⏎ • Low body temperature (~35°C) in many marsupials may reduce fever response but also limits some pathogens (inherited trait). ⏎ • Pouch is a clean micro-environment; some marsupials lick it to add antimicrobial enzymes (behavioral/immune aid). | • Fur for insulation (inherited). Some have pouch with sweat glands to keep it moist for newborns (innovation). ⏎ • Pouch internally has specialized skin that swells to form a tight seal around nursing young (innovation). ⏎ • Scent glands in some (e.g. possums) for marking territory and attracting mates (inherited from early mammals). | • Epipubic bones present (like monotremes) – support the pouch and abdominal wall (inherited primitive feature)​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. ⏎ • Dentary bone in jaw and three ear bones (inherited mammal skeleton). ⏎ • Many marsupials have extra molar count (e.g. 3 premolars, 4 molars per side) different from placentals – a skeletal dental formula difference (inherited from early metatherians). | • Strong forelimbs in newborns – even at birth, joeys have relatively developed arms to climb to pouch (innovation: precocious forelimb muscles). ⏎ • Adult marsupials show varied muscle specializations: kangaroos’ large hindlimb muscles and elastic tendons for hopping (innovation for energy-efficient locomotion). ⏎ • Tail muscles in some (kangaroo tail acts as a fifth limb when moving slowly, providing push – innovation). | • Highly developed smell – nocturnal marsupials rely on olfaction for finding food and recognizing offspring (inherited). ⏎ • Good hearing – many have large pinnae ears to detect predators (innovation in some, e.g. bilby). ⏎ • Vision varies: generally good night vision; some (wallabies) have color vision but less acute than placentals (inherited constraint).
R51: Pollinating Insects (e.g. early Bees) | • Brain with mushroom bodies enlarged for learning and memory – bees can learn flower colors and navigate using sun compass (innovation among insects). ⏎ • Social behavior in some (honeybees) indicates complex neural communication (dance language interpretation – innovation). ⏎ • Ventral nerve cord with segmental ganglia controls flight and reflexes (inherited arthropod trait). | • Modified mouthparts – long proboscis/tongue to sip nectar (innovation for pollination). ⏎ • Honey stomach (crop) in bees to carry nectar without digesting, and pollen baskets on legs to transport pollen (innovation). ⏎ • Digestive division: foregut (crop), midgut (enzyme digestion), hindgut (water reabsorption) – fairly standard insect design (inherited). | • Tracheal system – a network of internal air tubes (tracheae) delivering O₂ directly to tissues (inherited insect innovation)​britannica.com. ⏎ • Spiracles (valved openings) on body regulate air intake and water loss (inherited). ⏎ • Flight muscle pumping: active insects like bees ventilate tracheae by contracting flight muscles, moving air in and out (innovation to support high metabolism). | • Open circulatory system – hemolymph bathes organs, pumped by a tubular heart (inherited)​britannica.com. ⏎ • Dorsal heart with ostia drives hemolymph circulation; not used for oxygen transport (tracheae handle O₂)​britannica.com​britannica.com. ⏎ • Hemolymph transports nutrients, hormones, waste; contains immune cells (inherited)​britannica.com. | • Oviparity – lay eggs (inherited from insects). Many pollinators (bees, butterflies) lay eggs on or near host plants. ⏎ • Holometabolous metamorphosis – complete life cycle (egg, larva, pupa, adult) (innovation) allowing larvae and adults to occupy different niches​britannica.com. ⏎ • Haplodiploidy in bees/wasps: fertilized eggs become females, unfertilized become males (innovation influencing social structure). | • Malpighian tubules in hindgut filter wastes from hemolymph, excreting uric acid into the intestine​britannica.com (inherited insect excretory system). ⏎ • Uric acid – nitrogenous waste excreted as solid or semisolid, conserving water (inherited adaptation to land). ⏎ • Minimal water loss in frass (feces) – efficient for tiny bodies (inherited). |  | • Juvenile hormone & ecdysone regulate metamorphosis – coordinate larva to pupa to adult transformation (innovation in life cycle control). ⏎ • Queen pheromones in social insects act like hormones at colony level – e.g. queen bee pheromone prevents worker ovaries from developing (innovation in reproductive regulation). ⏎ • Diurnal rhythm: hormones and neural clocks synchronize for daytime foraging (inherited trait). | • Robust innate immunity – hemocytes in hemolymph engulf pathogens (inherited)​britannica.com. ⏎ • Antimicrobial peptides (e.g. defensins) produced in response to infection (inherited insect immune strategy). ⏎ • Social immunity in honeybees (innovation): behaviors like grooming nestmates and propolis use to reduce microbes in hive. | • Chitinous exoskeleton – lightweight yet protective cuticle (inherited arthropod trait). ⏎ • Waxy cuticle minimizes water loss (inherited adaptation to land). ⏎ • Setae (hairs) on bee bodies are branched and electrostatic – trap pollen efficiently (innovation aiding pollination). | • Exoskeleton provides attachment for powerful flight muscles (inherited). ⏎ • Wing structure – two pairs of membranous wings in most (bees, butterflies), often coupled or scaled; rigid veins support them (inherited insect design, refined for flight). ⏎ • Flexibly segmented abdomen allows aculeate insects (bees) to sting via ovipositor modification (innovation: stinger + venom apparatus). | • Indirect flight muscles in bees: power flight by deforming thorax rapidly (up to 200 Hz wing beats – major innovation enabling hovering)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Leg muscles specialized for pollen gathering – bees have corbicula (pollen basket) on hind legs, with muscles to pack pollen (innovation). ⏎ • Proboscis muscles in butterflies/moths to unfurl and furl the long proboscis for nectar feeding (innovation). | • Compound eyes with numerous facets – provide wide-angle UV vision to see flower patterns (innovation: UV reflection guides pollinators). ⏎ • Color vision extends into UV range (bees see ultraviolet nectar guides on petals – innovation) while red may be poorly seen (inherited limitation). ⏎ • Antennae densely packed with chemoreceptors – exquisite smell to detect floral scents and pheromones (innovation for finding flowers and communication).
R52: Birds (Aves, avian dinosaurs) | • Enlarged brains relative to body – especially cerebellum for flight coordination and optic tectum for vision (innovation for flight). ⏎ • High learning ability in crows, parrots etc., and complex social behavior (tool use, vocal learning) – advanced cognitive capacity (innovation beyond typical reptiles). ⏎ • Efficient thermoregulation control – hypothalamus well-developed to maintain high body temp (innovation). | • No teeth – keratinous beak instead (innovation to lighten head for flight).
