Some primates (gibbons) have throat air sacs to amplify calls (innovation in respiratory anatomy for communication). ⏎ • Many can thermoregulate well – e.g. humans sweat for cooling (primates’ integument interacting with respiration for evaporative cooling – innovation). | • 4-chambered heart (inherited) with typical high endurance capacity in many (e.g. migratory bats, long-distance primate foragers). ⏎ • Rodents: small size, very fast heart rates; can sustain high blood flow for their size (inherited trait scaled up). ⏎ • Primates: some (apes) have higher blood pressure to maintain brain flow upright (adaptation). Some hibernators in this clade (dwarf lemurs) store fat and drop heart rate in torpor (innovation in primates). | • Placental viviparity (inherited). Primates generally have longer gestation and fewer offspring (1 at a time) with extended upbringing (innovation – K-selection). Rodents opposite: short gestation, large litters, rapid maturity (innovation – r-selection). ⏎ • Social breeding: some primates have complex mating systems (harems, pair bonds, etc., regulated by social cues and hormones – innovation), some rodents (mole rats) even eusocial with a single breeding queen (extreme innovation). | • Urea excretion (inherited) – efficient kidneys; e.g. desert rodents (kangaroo rat) produce highly concentrated urine (innovation: some of the longest Henle’s loops of any mammal). ⏎ • Primates: moderate concentrating ability; apes/humans actually less adept at concentrating urine than desert mammals (inherited trait in lush environment). ⏎ • Defecation frequency: e.g. rabbits produce cecotropes (special nutrient-rich droppings) and re-ingest them (excretory/ingestive adaptation). |  | • Primates: complex endocrine cycles – e.g. menstrual cycle in Old World monkeys/apes (innovation, endometrium sheds), concealed ovulation in humans (innovation in mating strategy). High cortisol responses to social stress (inherited mammal trait). ⏎ • Rodents: often induced ovulation (ovulate after mating stimulus – innovation for efficiency). They can breed prolifically; hormones allow postpartum estrus (mate right after giving birth – innovation maximizing reproduction). | • Primates: generally strong immune systems; long-lived ones (humans) have slower thymus involution (somewhat longer immune youth). Some (chimpanzees) can get human diseases – similar immunity (inherited). ⏎ • Rodents: immune tolerance is high – can live in large colonies with disease exposure. Lab mice (in this clade) have been key to understanding adaptive immunity; their immune system is prototypical mammal (inherited). Some rodents evolved cancer resistance (naked mole rat with unique hyaluronan, though that is a different order in Euarchontoglires – innovation). | • Primates: most have nails instead of claws (innovation aiding touch and manipulation), sensitive pads with fingerprints (innovation). Dense hair except in humans (who have sparse hair, sweat glands increased – unique hominin integument innovation). ⏎ • Rodents: fur often specialized (e.g. whiskers, stripes for camouflage in chipmunks). Some have dermal armor bits (hair strengthened into quills in porcupines – innovation like hedgehogs via convergence). ⏎ • Many adapt pelage color seasonally (e.g. snowshoe hare white in winter – innovation for camouflage). | • Primates: flexible skeleton, mobile shoulder (especially apes – innovation: capable of brachiation), grasping hands/feet with opposable thumbs or big toes (innovation). Upright walking evolved in hominins (bipedal pelvis, spine S-curve – major innovation). ⏎ • Rodents: generally small and light skeleton, some with gliding membranes (flying squirrels – extended rib/limb skin, innovation convergent with bats). Lagomorphs (rabbits) have extra peg teeth and strong hind legs for jumping (inherited and refined). | • Primates: well-developed facial muscles for expression (innovation for social communication), fine finger muscles for dexterity (inherited but refined in apes). Apes also have strong arms for climbing/hanging (inherited adaptation). ⏎ • Rodents: powerful jaw muscles for gnawing (massive masseter muscle relative to size – innovation), and in some (kangaroo rats) huge hindlimb muscles for jumping (innovation). ⏎ • Primates (some) and rodents can sustain long activities (arboreal leaping in lemurs, burrowing in gophers) – muscles tuned for endurance or bursts accordingly (diverse, inherited). | • Primates: trichromatic color vision in Old World monkeys and apes (innovation from ancestral dichromacy) – better fruit/leaf discrimination. Depth perception from forward eyes (inherited from early primates). Highly social species evolved keen perception of expressions (cognitive-sensory link). ⏎ • Rodents: often poor vision but excellent smell and hearing (inherited nocturnal trait). Many are ultrasonic communicators (mice pups call ultrasonically, bats in sister clade use ultrasound, innovation in communication). Whisker touch is extremely refined – they can navigate mazes by touch (innovation in mechanosensation).
R89: Species | Nervous System | Digestive System | Respiratory System | Circulatory System | Reproductive System | Excretory System |  | Endocrine System | Immune System | Integumentary System | Skeletal System | Muscular System | Sensory System
R90: Reptiles – Sauropsids (2nd split) | • Brain similar to amphibian ancestors but with improved sensory integration for life on land (inherited) – larger optic lobes for vision and proprioceptive stretch receptors in limbs​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • Still ectothermic (cold-blooded), relying on behavioral thermoregulation (inherited)​www2.gwu.edu. | • Terrestrial feeding begins – strong jaws and simple cone teeth (inherited from amphibians) to grasp prey (inherited), but no complex chewing yet. ⏎ • Salivary glands present to lubricate food (inherited). ⏎ • Cloaca present for waste exit (inherited from early tetrapods). | • Lungs with expandable rib cage for ventilation (innovation vs amphibian buccal pumping)​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • No diaphragm, but intercostal muscles expand the chest to draw air (innovation). ⏎ • Internal nostrils (choanae) allow breathing with mouth closed (inherited). | • Partially divided ventricle in heart reducing blood mixing (innovation)​www2.gwu.edu – a step toward efficient separation of oxygenated and deoxygenated blood. ⏎ • Double circulation established (pulmonary vs systemic) (inherited from early amniotes). | • Amniotic egg with shell and membranes (chorion, amnion, allantois) – allows reproduction on land (major innovation)​www2.gwu.edu​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • Allantois membrane stores embryonic waste as uric acid​www2.gwu.edu, and provides respiratory surface​www2.gwu.edu (innovation). ⏎ • Internal fertilization via penis/cloaca (inherited from early amniotes). | • Uric acid excretion – kidneys produce concentrated uric acid paste, minimizing water loss (innovation for dry land)​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • A common cloaca for urine and feces (inherited from amphibians), but separate ducts for urinary and reproductive tracts (innovation)​www2.gwu.edu. |  | • Basic endocrine glands (thyroid, adrenal, etc.) present (inherited). ⏎ • Corticosterone drives seasonal breeding cycles (inherited). ⏎ • Ectothermy means hormones mainly regulate growth and reproduction rather than internal metabolism (inherited trait). | • Innate immune system similar to amphibians (inherited). ⏎ • Scales as barrier reduce pathogen entry (innovation: keratinized skin)​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • Thymus and spleen present for lymphocyte production (inherited). | • Thick keratinized scales covering skin to prevent dehydration (innovation)​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • Claws on toes for traction and digging (inherited from early amniotes). ⏎ • Few skin glands, reducing water loss (innovation). | • Fully ossified skeleton with stronger limbs for land locomotion (inherited, refined). ⏎ • One occipital condyle (skull-neck joint) allowing head mobility (innovation vs amphibians’ two)​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • Expanded ribcage to support lungs (innovation). | • Limb musculature strengthened for walking on land (inherited but improved). ⏎ • Costal (rib) muscles power breathing by expanding thorax (innovation). ⏎ • Epaxial muscles (along spine) support posture out of water (inherited). | • Vision optimized for air – well-developed eyes with movable eyelids and color vision (inherited, improved). ⏎ • Olfactory sense enhanced with a vomeronasal organ for smelling on land (innovation). ⏎ • Hearing via stapes bone – detects vibrations, but still limited (inherited from amphibians).
R91: First Octopus (early cephalopod) | • Complex brain with ~500 million neurons (a huge innovation among invertebrates)​en.wikipedia.org – capable of learning and problem-solving. ⏎ • Decentralized nerves: two-thirds of neurons in arms, giving arms autonomous control​en.wikipedia.org (innovation). ⏎ • Cartilaginous brain capsule protects brain (innovation). | • Beak and radula to tear prey (innovation among mollusks)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Crop and caecum in gut for storage and digestion​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Venomous saliva (in some octopuses) from salivary glands to paralyze prey (innovation). ⏎ • No chewing – swallows chunks and uses strong enzymes to digest (inherited trait). | • Gills for aquatic respiration (inherited from mollusk ancestors) – water drawn into mantle cavity and expelled through siphon​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Muscular mantle pumps water over gills (innovation: active ventilation). ⏎ • Some cutaneous respiration – skin absorbs O₂ (up to ~40% at rest)​en.wikipedia.org. | • Closed circulatory system – blood always in vessels (rare in inverts, innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Three hearts (two branchial hearts for gills, one systemic heart) pump blood​en.wikipedia.org (innovation for high activity). ⏎ • Copper-based hemocyanin makes blood blue and efficient in cold, low-O₂ water​en.wikipedia.org. | • External fertilization: male uses a specialized arm (hectocotylus) to transfer spermatophores to female (innovation in mating behavior). ⏎ • Female lays eggs and broods them, then dies (semelparity) – one reproductive cycle (inherited trait in cephalopods). ⏎ • No larval stage – direct developing hatchlings (innovation among many mollusks). | • Nephridia (kidneys) filter blood and excrete ammonia into mantle cavity (inherited from mollusks)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Ammonia waste – toxic but diluted in water (inherited strategy, as water is abundant). ⏎ • Ink sac (excretory/defensive): releases ink cloud to expel wastes and evade predators (innovation). |  | • Optic gland in brain controls reproductive hormone release and aging (if removed, female lives longer – shows endocrine control of lifespan). ⏎ • Neurosecretory cells regulate growth, reproduction, molting of skin chromatophores (innovation in hormonal control for color change). | • Innate immune cells (hemocytes) in hemolymph phagocytose bacteria (inherited in invertebrates). ⏎ • Antimicrobial peptides in skin and mucus to prevent infection (innovation). ⏎ • Wound healing is rapid – arms can even regenerate without infection (strong innate immunity). | • Soft body with no shell – relies on skin that can rapidly change color and texture via chromatophores, iridophores, and papillae (innovation for camouflage)​en.wikipedia.org​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Ink sac under skin for defense, ejects melanin ink (innovation). ⏎ • Flexible skin allows squeezing through tight spaces. | • No external or internal skeleton – supported by muscular hydrostat and cartilage in head (inherited loss of shell). ⏎ • Eight arms with rigid beak as only hard part (innovation: beak serves as “teeth”). ⏎ • Brain encased in cartilage cranium (innovation protecting nervous system). | • Eight muscular arms with suction cups for powerful grasp and manipulation (innovation – unique muscle structure). ⏎ • Mantle muscle for jet propulsion: rapid contraction expels water for escape (innovation). ⏎ • Funnel/siphon muscles to direct jet and steer (innovation in locomotion control). | • Camera-type eyes very similar to vertebrate eyes (convergent innovation) – acute vision and focus​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Polarized light vision – can detect polarized light patterns (innovation). ⏎ • Chemoreceptors on suckers to “taste” by touch (innovation) for exploring food.
R92: Crocodiles (Archosaur reptiles) | • Cerebrum and limbic system moderately developed – enables complex social behavior like parental care (innovation among reptiles). ⏎ • Strong instinctual patterns (e.g. guarding nests, vocal communication with young – innovation). ⏎ • Brain still small relative to body, but efficient; capable of learning simple routines (inherited). | • Very acidic stomach (pH ~1–2) – one of the strongest of any animal, dissolving bones (innovation for digesting whole prey)​facebook.com. ⏎ • Gizzard-like pylorus with gastroliths (stones) to grind food (innovation, similar to birds). ⏎ • Secondary palate in mouth allows breathing while eating (innovation shared with mammals) – can hold prey in jaws and still breathe. | • Powerful lungs with unidirectional airflow (archosaur trait) – some flow-through respiration, though less efficient than birds (inherited archosaur innovation). ⏎ • Hepatic piston: a diaphragmaticus muscle pulls liver to act as a diaphragm, aiding breathing (unique innovation)​phys.org. ⏎ • Can stay submerged ~1–2 hours by slowing metabolism and heart rate (innovation for diving). | • Four-chambered heart (like birds/mammals) – fully separates oxygenated and deoxygenated blood (innovation among reptiles)​fountainmagazine.com. ⏎ • Foramen of Panizza – a special valve connecting aortas that shunts blood when diving, aiding oxygen conservation (unique innovation)​fountainmagazine.com. ⏎ • High blood pressure to perfuse large bodies (inherited archosaur trait). | • Oviparous: lay hard-shelled eggs in nests (inherited archosaur trait). ⏎ • Temperature-dependent sex determination – nest temperature decides offspring sex (innovation). ⏎ • Parental care: mothers guard nests and assist hatching, even carrying young to water (innovation in reptiles). | • Uric acid excretion via kidneys and cloaca (inherited reptile trait). ⏎ • Salt glands on tongue excrete excess salt (innovation for semi-aquatic life). ⏎ • Cloaca also used for water reabsorption when on land (inherited). |  | • Corticosterone mediates long-term stress response (inherited). ⏎ • Thyroid gland relatively low activity – supports slower metabolism/growth (inherited from reptiles). ⏎ • Melatonin cycle influences seasonal behaviors like breeding (inherited). | • Robust immune system – blood has potent antibacterial properties (crocodile blood shows broad-spectrum antimicrobial peptides – innovation). ⏎ • Thick skin and armored scutes reduce infection risk from injuries (innovation). ⏎ • Slow immune response at low temperatures (ectothermic constraint, inherited). | • Armored skin with bony osteoderms under horny scales (innovation – protection and maybe mineral storage). ⏎ • “ISOs” (Integumentary Sense Organs) on scales, especially jaws – tiny pits that detect water vibrations and touch, more sensitive than human fingertips (innovation)​nationalgeographic.com​nationalgeographic.com. ⏎ • Nictitating membrane protects eyes underwater (inherited). | • Robust skull and jaw with secondary palate (innovation for feeding/breathing). ⏎ • Short, strong limbs with sprawling gait (inherited from early reptiles). ⏎ • Semi-erect posture – can lift body for faster gait (minor innovation among reptiles). | • Massive jaw muscles for one of the strongest bites in animal kingdom (innovation)​nationalgeographic.com. ⏎ • Tail muscles (laterally flattened tail) provide powerful swimming thrust (innovation for aquatic locomotion). ⏎ • Diaphragmaticus muscle (unique to crocs) to pump lungs and adjust buoyancy (innovation)​phys.org. | • Excellent night vision (large pupils, tapetum lucidum reflecting layer – innovation) for nocturnal hunting. ⏎ • Sensitive hearing – mothers hear hatchling calls from inside eggs (innovation). ⏎ • ISOs sensory pits on snout detect slightest ripples, allowing strikes in total darkness​nationalgeographic.com​nationalgeographic.com (innovation).
R93: Dinosaurs (non-avian, Mesozoic) | • Increasing brain size in some (e.g. small theropods like Troodon had large brains for their size – innovation), but many large dinos had small brains (inherited limitation). ⏎ • Complex behaviors: herding in hadrosaurs, nesting colonies, and brooding (fossil evidence) – indicates advanced brain functions for social behavior (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Balance and coordination refined in bipedal forms via expanded cerebellum (innovation). | • Diverse diets: herbivores developed gastroliths in stomach to grind tough plant material (innovation), and some (hadrosaurs, ceratopsians) evolved dental batteries – tightly packed teeth that self-sharpen for grinding (innovation). ⏎ • Carnivores had serrated teeth to slice flesh (inherited from reptiles, optimized in theropods). ⏎ • No chewing in most – food swallowed in chunks (inherited), but some had cheek-like structures to retain food while chewing (possible innovation in some ornithischians). | • Avian-style air sac system in many saurischians (theropods and sauropods) – hollow bones evidence suggests they had flow-through lungs with air sacs for continuous one-way airflow (innovation)​journals.plos.org.​journals.plos.org ⏎ • High oxygen demand: likely four-phase breathing like birds (innovation), enabling active lifestyles. Others (ornithischians) had less air sac development but still efficient lungs (inherited archosaur trait). ⏎ • Some gigantothermy: large dinos could sustain stable body temps due to size, reducing need for rapid breathing (inherited strategy of ectotherms supplemented by mass homeothermy). | • Four-chambered heart (inferred for all archosaurs, including dinos – likely innovation for high metabolism)​fountainmagazine.com. ⏎ • High blood pressure in tall sauropods to pump blood to heads (innovation: very large hearts and perhaps accessory pumps). ⏎ • Haversian bone structure in fast-growing dinos suggests high metabolic rate supported by vigorous circulation (innovation toward endothermy). | • Egg-laying with hard-shelled eggs (inherited from archosaur ancestors). ⏎ • Elaborate nesting: fossils of brooding (e.g. oviraptorids sitting on nests) show incubation behavior (innovation) – adults regulated egg temperature like birds. ⏎ • Some young altricial (requiring care) – evidence of juveniles in nests fed by parents (innovation), vs others precocial (inherited reptilian style). | • Uricotelic like other reptiles: excreted uric acid paste via kidneys and cloaca (inherited). ⏎ • Salt glands in some (e.g. marine or desert dinos) to excrete excess salt (possible innovation by convergence with birds). ⏎ • Large herbivores had immense gut systems – likely copious urine/feces processed, with water reabsorption in hindgut (inherited reptile trait). |  | • Possible endothermy in some (small theropods) – implying thyroid-driven high metabolism (innovation). ⏎ • Growth hormones: evidence of fast juvenile growth in bone rings suggests intense endocrine regulation (innovation allowing some species to reach adult size quickly). ⏎ • Reproductive hormones cyclical – synchronized mass nesting seasons (inferred, like modern birds). | • Robust immune defenses needed in dense populations – likely good wound healing and innate immunity (inferred). ⏎ • Bone marrow in dinosaurs produced immune cells as in birds (inherited). ⏎ • Gut microbiome likely contributed to immunity in herbivores (inherited concept). | • Feathers or proto-feathers present in many theropods (and even some ornithischians) – initially for insulation or display (innovation), later co-opted for flight in avian lineage.​sciencedaily.com ⏎ • Scales in other dinos (inherited reptilian trait) for body covering; some had both scales and feather-like filaments. ⏎ • Colorful display structures (frills, crests, possibly colored feathers) – used for species recognition and mating displays (innovation in integument for social signaling). | • Erect limb posture – legs positioned under the body (innovation vs sprawling gait), allowing efficient locomotion and gigantic body sizes. ⏎ • Light but strong bones: many bones hollow with internal struts (especially in saurischians) for strength and lightness (innovation, related to air sacs)​journals.plos.org. ⏎ • Specialized joints: extra hip vertebrae support (ornithischian “bird-hip” structure) and flexible necks in sauropods (elongated cervical vertebrae with air cavities – innovation). | • Massive locomotor muscles in hindlimbs of bipedal dinos for running (e.g. thick caudofemoralis muscle in tail powering legs – inherited archosaur trait, enlarged). ⏎ • Shoulder and breast muscles enlarged in some theropods for predation (holding prey) and in others (early fliers like Archaeopteryx) for flapping (incipient flight muscles – innovation). ⏎ • Gigantic herbivores had pillar-like limb muscles emphasizing support over speed (inherited strategy scaled up). | • Sharp color vision – likely tetrachromatic like birds (innovation carried from archosaur lineage) enabling identification of mates and ripe fruits. ⏎ • Acute hearing in some (cochlear lengths in fossils suggest certain theropods heard well, possibly in bird-like frequency range – innovation) and others had poorer hearing (inherited variability). ⏎ • Strong smell: large olfactory bulbs in Tyrannosaurus suggest excellent olfaction for tracking prey (innovation in predators).
R94: Earliest Mammals (Triassic small mammals) | • Expanded cerebral cortex relative to reptiles – improved learning and sensory processing (innovation). ⏎ • Excellent olfaction and hearing centers: nocturnal lifestyle drove a larger olfactory bulb and auditory cortex​en.wikipedia.org (innovation). ⏎ • Still small brain overall, but brain-to-body ratio increased compared to dinosaurs (innovation trend). | • Heterodont dentition – differentiated teeth (incisors, canines, cheek teeth) for varied diet (innovation vs uniform reptile teeth). They could nip, tear, and chew somewhat, enhancing food processing.​en.wikipedia.org ⏎ • Lactation appears: infants drank mothers’ milk (major innovation), although early mammals had no nipples – milk secreted onto fur (monotreme-like)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Likely insectivorous or omnivorous – simple stomach, short gut (inherited from insectivorous ancestors). | • Lungs with diaphragm starting to form – early mammals evolved a muscular diaphragm to pump lungs efficiently (innovation). ⏎ • Turbinate bones in nose to retain moisture and heat during breathing (innovation aiding endothermy). ⏎ • High breathing rates to support warm-blooded metabolism (innovation compared to reptiles). | • Four-chambered heart fully separating circuits (innovation inherited from earlier synapsids, fully utilized for endothermy). ⏎ • High blood pressure and rapid circulation to sustain higher metabolic rate (innovation). ⏎ • Blood with biconcave red blood cells (no nucleus) to carry more oxygen – likely present (innovation aligning with endothermy). | • Viviparity emerges: some early mammals may have birthed live young (proto-therians), but earliest ones (like Morganucodon) likely still laid eggs or had very tiny young (transitional). ⏎ • Milk production to nourish young – mammary glands with nutrient-rich milk (innovation enabling altricial young to survive). ⏎ • Epipubic bones present (as in monotremes/marsupials) restricting pregnancy length; earliest placentals later lost them to allow longer gestation​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. | • Urea excretion replaces uric acid – early mammals likely shifted to less toxic urea, needing more water but compatible with their nocturnal, moisture-rich environments (innovation vs reptiles)​www2.gwu.edu. ⏎ • Loops of Henle in kidney evolving to concentrate urine moderately (early innovation toward efficient kidneys). ⏎ • Separate anal and urethral openings (ureters to bladder) apart from reproductive tract, though monotreme-like cloaca still possible in earliest forms (transition stage). |  | • Endothermy (warm-bloodedness) – ability to generate internal heat via high metabolism (major innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. Likely fully evolved by late Triassic (~233 Mya)​scientificamerican.com. ⏎ • Hair follicle glands (sebaceous, apocrine) present, secreting oils for fur and possibly pheromones (innovation). ⏎ • Thyroid & adrenal glands more developed to regulate higher metabolic rate (innovation). | • Improved innate immunity – fur reduces ectoparasites, and higher body temp (≈30–35°C in early mammals) inhibits some infections (innovation). ⏎ • Adaptive immunity advanced: bone marrow and spleen producing diverse lymphocytes (inherited from synapsids, refined). ⏎ • Maternal antibodies possibly transferred via milk, granting pups passive immunity (innovation). | • Fur (hair) for insulation – a new integumentary feature indicating endothermy (major innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Whiskers (vibrissae) on snout – tactile sensors in the fur, evidence of which seen in small Triassic mammals (innovation to navigate in dark)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Mammary hairs – milk secreted onto patches of fur (in monotreme-like fashion)​en.wikipedia.org, early form of mammary gland (innovation). | • Jaw joint restructured: dentary-squamosal joint formed, while quadrate and articular bones repurposed into middle ear ossicles (malleus, incus) – a hallmark mammalian innovation improving hearing​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Three middle ear bones fully detached from jaw, increasing hearing sensitivity (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Seven neck vertebrae (inherited constraint of mammals) and flexible backbone for agility (innovation: more dorso-ventral flexion for running). | • Diaphragm muscle developed, separating chest and abdomen – greatly improves breathing efficiency (innovation). ⏎ • Differentiated facial muscles – allowed suckling (lips forming seal to nurse, an innovation in feeding young). ⏎ • Postural muscles adapted for sustained activity – early mammals could sustain exercise to forage at night (innovation, ties to endothermy). | • Highly sensitive hearing – three-ossicle middle ear and outer ear pinna (if present) gave acute hearing of high frequencies (innovation aiding nocturnal life). ⏎ • Strong sense of smell – large olfactory bulb and turbinates indicate powerful olfaction for finding food and mates in the dark​en.wikipedia.org (innovation). ⏎ • Vision likely rod-dominated (nocturnal bottleneck): good night vision but poor color (inherited constraint); later mammals re-evolved color in daytime niches.
R95: Lizards & more Reptiles (Squamates) | • Mid-sized brains – larger than amphibians but smaller than mammals; capable of learning simple tasks (inherited reptile brain). ⏎ • Vomeronasal organ well-developed – tongue flicking delivers scent particles, integrated in brain for acute chemosensing (innovation). ⏎ • Reflex-driven prey capture (rapid visual processing for hunting insects, inherited). | • Mostly carnivorous/insectivorous – simple tubular gut (inherited) with strong stomach acid for whole prey digestion (inherited). ⏎ • Some herbivorous lizards evolved longer guts and hindgut fermentation chambers (ceca) to break down plant cellulose (innovation). ⏎ • Kinetic skulls in many lizards (movable jaw joints) to swallow large prey (innovation). | • Lung ventilation by ribs (like other reptiles, inherited). Small lizards may also use throat pumping to assist breathing when running (innovation). ⏎ • Some developed air sacs in lungs (e.g. monitors) for more efficient gas exchange (minor innovation). ⏎ • Cutaneous respiration negligible – dry skin (inherited constraint, rely fully on lungs). | • Three-chambered heart with partial septum in some (e.g. monitors) – moderate separation of blood (inherited baseline). ⏎ • Lymphatic heart present: accessory pumps move lymph (innovation in some lizards). ⏎ • Relatively lower blood pressure than mammals (ectothermic metabolic needs, inherited). | • Hemipenes in males – paired intromittent organs (innovation of squamates) for internal fertilization. ⏎ • Oviparity common: lay flexible or rigid-shelled eggs buried in soil (inherited reptile trait); some lizards and many snakes later evolved viviparity (live birth) as an innovation in harsh climates. ⏎ • Minimal parental care – most abandon eggs (inherited), though a few guard nests (minor innovation). | • Uric acid excretion via kidneys and cloaca (inherited reptile trait) – thick, whitish paste in droppings. ⏎ • Salt glands in marine iguanas and desert lizards excrete excess salt (innovation for osmoregulation). ⏎ • Cloaca expels feces and urine together (inherited). |  | • Ecdysis (molting) regulated by thyroid hormones – periodic shedding of skin (innovation in growth). ⏎ • Temperature-dependent metabolism – endocrine control (thyroid) adjusts metabolic enzymes with ambient temperature (inherited ectothermy). ⏎ • Reproductive cycles timed by hormones and often linked to seasonal rainfall (inherited strategy). | • Strong innate immunity – scales prevent infection entry (inherited physical barrier). ⏎ • Wound healing is efficient but slower than in mammals (ectothermic constraint). ⏎ • Some lizards can autotomize tails to escape predators, with minimal bleeding and infection – indicates effective clotting and healing (innovation). | • Keratin scales overlapping to protect and reduce water loss (inherited reptile skin). ⏎ • Color-changing in chameleons via chromatophores under neural/endocrine control (innovation for camouflage and thermoregulation). ⏎ • Regenerative tails in many – integument regrows with cartilage rod and scales after tail drop (innovation in tissue regeneration). | • Flexible skull and jaw (especially in snakes, a subset of squamates) – multiple joints and elastic ligaments allow large prey swallowing (innovation). ⏎ • Epiphyseal plates in bones for determinate growth (stop growing at adulthood, unlike many reptiles – some squamates approach this, minor innovation). ⏎ • Feet with toepads in geckos (innovation: Van der Waals setae for adhesion) enabling climbing. | • Segmented limb muscles enabling quick bursts of speed to catch prey (inherited). ⏎ • Autotomy muscles in tail – pre-formed break points and muscle constrictors to detach tail and later regrow (innovation). ⏎ • Thoracic muscles also aid in standing display (e.g. push-ups in lizards), combining locomotion and signaling (inherited, repurposed behavior). | • Vision often excellent – many diurnal lizards have color vision (4 cones) and UV detection (inherited from reptilian ancestors). ⏎ • Forked tongue + vomeronasal organ in snakes and some lizards for acute smell/taste of air (innovation) – can detect prey trails.​en.wikipedia.org ⏎ • Infrared heat-sensing pits evolved in boas, pythons, and pit vipers to detect warm-blooded prey (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org.
R96: Monotremes (Platypus, Echidna) | • Mix of reptilian and mammalian traits: brain relatively small, no corpus callosum connecting hemispheres (inherited primitive trait)​en.wikipedia.org, but highly developed sensory regions (e.g. tactile bill in platypus – innovation). ⏎ • Electroreception processing in platypus brain – unique sensory neurons map in cortex (innovation). ⏎ • Capable of REM and non-REM sleep, indicating mammalian neural organization (inherited). | • Edentulous (toothless) as adults – platypus has grinding plates, echidna has none; food (invertebrates) ground between tongue and palate (inherited primitive trait)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Keratinous pads/spines in mouth to grind food (innovation replacing teeth). ⏎ • Cloaca present – single opening for digestive and urinary wastes (inherited reptile-like feature)​en.wikipedia.org. | • Lungs and diaphragm like other mammals (inherited mammal trait), so monotremes do use a diaphragm for breathing (though body temperature is lower). ⏎ • Lower metabolic rate means slower breathing rate; can also enter torpor (echidnas hibernate) – endocrine regulated slowing of respiration (innovation for energy saving). ⏎ • Nostrils at snout tip (platypus) or on face (echidna) – can close underwater (adaptation for diving in platypus). | • Four-chambered heart (like all mammals, inherited). ⏎ • Low body temperature (~32°C), so heart rate and blood pressure are lower than in placentals (innovation – secondarily lower metabolism)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Large azygous vein in platypus, aiding venous return from their sprawled posture (adaptation). | • Oviparous mammals – females lay small, leathery eggs (inherited reptilian trait). Eggs hatch after short incubation, and tiny young nurse on milk (innovation: mammalian lactation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • No nipples – milk “sweated” from mammary gland openings on belly fur patches, licked by young​en.wikipedia.org (primitive mammal trait). ⏎ • Cloaca (“monotreme” means one opening) for reproductive tract (inherited)​en.wikipedia.org; males have penis but only used for semen transfer, retracted otherwise. | • Urea excretion (like other mammals, inherited) – kidneys produce urea, excreted via cloaca with feces (urine and feces mix in cloaca). ⏎ • No urinary bladder in platypus (urine stored in cloaca/rectum area briefly), echidna has a rudimentary bladder; overall still cloacal (reptilian-like) system (inherited). ⏎ • Electrolyte regulation: kidneys less efficient than placentals, but sufficient given their lower metabolism (inherited). |  | • Lower basal metabolism – body temp ~32°C; thyroid activity is lower than in placentals (innovation or retention of primitive condition). ⏎ • Venom gland in male platypus (crural gland on hind leg) produces peptide venom (seasonal, used in mating fights – unique endocrine/exocrine innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Milk production controlled by prolactin as in other mammals (inherited) – monotreme milk contains antimicrobial proteins unique to them (innovation for protecting young). | • Innate immunity functioning at lower body temp – monotreme immune cells operate a bit slower but effectively. ⏎ • Platypus venom (from male spur) is made of defensin-like proteins​en.wikipedia.org, evolved from immune molecules – interesting link between immune peptides and venom (innovation). ⏎ • Long-lived for their size (echidnas ~50 years) – suggests effective immune surveillance against cancers/infections (speculative). | • Fur for insulation (inherited mammal trait) – dense fur on platypus (waterproof underfur), spines (modified hairs) on echidna for defense (innovation). ⏎ • Spur on hind ankle (males) connected to venom gland – unique integumentary weapon (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Mammary patch – no nipple, milk oozes through skin; hairy patch helps spread milk for young​en.wikipedia.org (primitive mammary structure). | • Shoulder girdle retains ancestral bones (interclavicle, coracoids) lost in other mammals – more reptile-like skeletal layout (inherited)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Epipubic bones present (like marsupials) to support abdominal muscles or egg-carrying (inherited primitive trait). ⏎ • Legs held somewhat sprawled (especially in platypus front legs) – gives reptilian gait in water and land (inherited trait). | • Platypus bill muscles highly refined for electrosensory guided motion – bill moves in scanning pattern underwater (innovation in feeding muscles). ⏎ • Echidna forelimbs power digging – strong claws and burrowing muscles (innovation for fossorial life). ⏎ • Spur muscles in male platypus can inject venom during breeding season (muscular control of venom delivery – innovation). | • Electroreception in platypus: ~40,000 electroreceptors in bill skin detect prey’s electric signals – only mammal with this sense (major sensory innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Echidna snout has electroreceptors too (fewer) and a keen sense of smell for finding ants (inherited/modified). ⏎ • Poor vision in monotremes – small eyes, rely more on other senses (inherited primitive trait).
R97: Marsupials (Kangaroos, Koalas, etc.) | • Moderately sized brains – smaller than placental mammals of similar body size (especially reduced cerebral cortex folding, inherited constraint). ⏎ • No corpus callosum connecting brain halves (like monotremes)​en.wikipedia.org – neural fibers cross via anterior commissure instead (primitive trait). ⏎ • Strong olfaction and hearing – nocturnal ancestry maintained keen smell and mobile ears (inherited trait). | • Varied diets: some are herbivores (kangaroos, koalas) with large chambered stomachs or elongated cecum for fermentation (innovation similar to ruminants), others carnivorous (Tasmanian devil) with short gut (inherited mammal trait). ⏎ • Dentition often specialized: e.g. kangaroos have high-crowned molars for grinding tough grass (innovation), opossums have 50 teeth for omnivory (inherited generalized trait). ⏎ • Lactation highly tuned: milk composition changes as joey develops in pouch (innovation – early milk high in simple sugars, later milk richer in fats/protein). | • Lungs and diaphragm like other mammals (inherited); breathing rates linked to hopping in kangaroos (they synchronize breathing with hops to save energy – innovation). ⏎ • Lower metabolic rate on average than placentals, so slightly lower lung ventilation needs (inherited trait). ⏎ • Nasal turbinates present for water conservation (inherited). | • Four-chambered heart (inherited mammalian). ⏎ • Many marsupials have lower metabolic output, thus slightly larger red blood cells and lower blood pressure than placentals (adaptation to lower O₂ needs). ⏎ • Double circulation fully developed (inherited). | • Short gestation – young born extremely underdeveloped, essentially embryo-like (marsupial hallmark innovation). ⏎ • Pouch (marsupium) in most: newborns crawl to nipple in pouch, latch on and continue development (innovation – external womb). ⏎ • Bifurcated reproductive tract: females have two uteruses and two vaginas; males often have bifid (forked) penis – unique marsupial anatomy (innovation), linked to giving birth to very small young. | • Urea excretion (inherited mammal trait) – kidneys concentrate urine moderately. ⏎ • Many marsupials conserve water well (desert species produce very concentrated urine – innovation). ⏎ • Cloaca in marsupials: not truly separate openings – single opening for excretion and reproduction (partial retention of primitive trait, though some have a slight separation internally). |  | • Gestation mainly external means progesterone supports early pregnancy briefly, then lactation hormones (prolactin, oxytocin) dominate for pouch life – highly specialized endocrine shift (innovation). ⏎ • Embryonic diapause in some (kangaroos): can pause development of a new embryo until pouch young vacates, via hormonal control (innovation). ⏎ • Adrenal and thyroid glands functional but marsupials often have slightly lower body temps and metabolism than placentals (endocrine level inherited trait). | • Immune transfer via milk – since young spend long in pouch with immature immune systems, mothers’ milk provides antibodies (innovation akin to placentals’ placental transfer) to protect young. ⏎ • Low body temperature (~35°C) in many marsupials may reduce fever response but also limits some pathogens (inherited trait). ⏎ • Pouch is a clean micro-environment; some marsupials lick it to add antimicrobial enzymes (behavioral/immune aid). | • Fur for insulation (inherited). Some have pouch with sweat glands to keep it moist for newborns (innovation). ⏎ • Pouch internally has specialized skin that swells to form a tight seal around nursing young (innovation). ⏎ • Scent glands in some (e.g. possums) for marking territory and attracting mates (inherited from early mammals). | • Epipubic bones present (like monotremes) – support the pouch and abdominal wall (inherited primitive feature)​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. ⏎ • Dentary bone in jaw and three ear bones (inherited mammal skeleton). ⏎ • Many marsupials have extra molar count (e.g. 3 premolars, 4 molars per side) different from placentals – a skeletal dental formula difference (inherited from early metatherians). | • Strong forelimbs in newborns – even at birth, joeys have relatively developed arms to climb to pouch (innovation: precocious forelimb muscles). ⏎ • Adult marsupials show varied muscle specializations: kangaroos’ large hindlimb muscles and elastic tendons for hopping (innovation for energy-efficient locomotion). ⏎ • Tail muscles in some (kangaroo tail acts as a fifth limb when moving slowly, providing push – innovation). | • Highly developed smell – nocturnal marsupials rely on olfaction for finding food and recognizing offspring (inherited). ⏎ • Good hearing – many have large pinnae ears to detect predators (innovation in some, e.g. bilby). ⏎ • Vision varies: generally good night vision; some (wallabies) have color vision but less acute than placentals (inherited constraint).
R98: Pollinating Insects (e.g. early Bees) | • Brain with mushroom bodies enlarged for learning and memory – bees can learn flower colors and navigate using sun compass (innovation among insects). ⏎ • Social behavior in some (honeybees) indicates complex neural communication (dance language interpretation – innovation). ⏎ • Ventral nerve cord with segmental ganglia controls flight and reflexes (inherited arthropod trait). | • Modified mouthparts – long proboscis/tongue to sip nectar (innovation for pollination). ⏎ • Honey stomach (crop) in bees to carry nectar without digesting, and pollen baskets on legs to transport pollen (innovation). ⏎ • Digestive division: foregut (crop), midgut (enzyme digestion), hindgut (water reabsorption) – fairly standard insect design (inherited). | • Tracheal system – a network of internal air tubes (tracheae) delivering O₂ directly to tissues (inherited insect innovation)​britannica.com. ⏎ • Spiracles (valved openings) on body regulate air intake and water loss (inherited). ⏎ • Flight muscle pumping: active insects like bees ventilate tracheae by contracting flight muscles, moving air in and out (innovation to support high metabolism). | • Open circulatory system – hemolymph bathes organs, pumped by a tubular heart (inherited)​britannica.com. ⏎ • Dorsal heart with ostia drives hemolymph circulation; not used for oxygen transport (tracheae handle O₂)​britannica.com​britannica.com. ⏎ • Hemolymph transports nutrients, hormones, waste; contains immune cells (inherited)​britannica.com. | • Oviparity – lay eggs (inherited from insects). Many pollinators (bees, butterflies) lay eggs on or near host plants. ⏎ • Holometabolous metamorphosis – complete life cycle (egg, larva, pupa, adult) (innovation) allowing larvae and adults to occupy different niches​britannica.com. ⏎ • Haplodiploidy in bees/wasps: fertilized eggs become females, unfertilized become males (innovation influencing social structure). | • Malpighian tubules in hindgut filter wastes from hemolymph, excreting uric acid into the intestine​britannica.com (inherited insect excretory system). ⏎ • Uric acid – nitrogenous waste excreted as solid or semisolid, conserving water (inherited adaptation to land). ⏎ • Minimal water loss in frass (feces) – efficient for tiny bodies (inherited). |  | • Juvenile hormone & ecdysone regulate metamorphosis – coordinate larva to pupa to adult transformation (innovation in life cycle control). ⏎ • Queen pheromones in social insects act like hormones at colony level – e.g. queen bee pheromone prevents worker ovaries from developing (innovation in reproductive regulation). ⏎ • Diurnal rhythm: hormones and neural clocks synchronize for daytime foraging (inherited trait). | • Robust innate immunity – hemocytes in hemolymph engulf pathogens (inherited)​britannica.com. ⏎ • Antimicrobial peptides (e.g. defensins) produced in response to infection (inherited insect immune strategy). ⏎ • Social immunity in honeybees (innovation): behaviors like grooming nestmates and propolis use to reduce microbes in hive. | • Chitinous exoskeleton – lightweight yet protective cuticle (inherited arthropod trait). ⏎ • Waxy cuticle minimizes water loss (inherited adaptation to land). ⏎ • Setae (hairs) on bee bodies are branched and electrostatic – trap pollen efficiently (innovation aiding pollination). | • Exoskeleton provides attachment for powerful flight muscles (inherited). ⏎ • Wing structure – two pairs of membranous wings in most (bees, butterflies), often coupled or scaled; rigid veins support them (inherited insect design, refined for flight). ⏎ • Flexibly segmented abdomen allows aculeate insects (bees) to sting via ovipositor modification (innovation: stinger + venom apparatus). | • Indirect flight muscles in bees: power flight by deforming thorax rapidly (up to 200 Hz wing beats – major innovation enabling hovering)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Leg muscles specialized for pollen gathering – bees have corbicula (pollen basket) on hind legs, with muscles to pack pollen (innovation). ⏎ • Proboscis muscles in butterflies/moths to unfurl and furl the long proboscis for nectar feeding (innovation). | • Compound eyes with numerous facets – provide wide-angle UV vision to see flower patterns (innovation: UV reflection guides pollinators). ⏎ • Color vision extends into UV range (bees see ultraviolet nectar guides on petals – innovation) while red may be poorly seen (inherited limitation). ⏎ • Antennae densely packed with chemoreceptors – exquisite smell to detect floral scents and pheromones (innovation for finding flowers and communication).
R99: Birds (Aves, avian dinosaurs) | • Enlarged brains relative to body – especially cerebellum for flight coordination and optic tectum for vision (innovation for flight). ⏎ • High learning ability in crows, parrots etc., and complex social behavior (tool use, vocal learning) – advanced cognitive capacity (innovation beyond typical reptiles). ⏎ • Efficient thermoregulation control – hypothalamus well-developed to maintain high body temp (innovation). | • No teeth – keratinous beak instead (innovation to lighten head for flight). Birds swallow food whole or in chunks; gizzard (muscular stomach) with grit grinds food (innovation replacing chewing). ⏎ • Crop in many birds – a storage pouch in esophagus to soften food or feed young via regurgitation (innovation). ⏎ • Rapid digestion – high metabolic rate necessitates quick processing; short intestines in carnivores, longer with ceca in herbivores for fermenting plant matter (inherited but optimized). | • Flow-through lungs with air sacs – unique unidirectional airflow through parabronchi for continuous gas exchange (major innovation)​britannica.com. ⏎ • Air sacs (9+ sacs) act as bellows: fresh air passes through lungs on both inhale and exhale​britannica.com, giving very efficient O₂ uptake (innovation)​britannica.com​britannica.com. ⏎ • Cross-current blood flow in lung capillaries maximizes O₂ extraction (innovation). | • Four-chambered heart, large relative to body, supporting high-pressure circulation (inherited from archosaurs, refined). ⏎ • Resting heart rates high, and can beat extremely fast in small birds (e.g. hummingbird ~1200 bpm) – powerful cardiac muscle (innovation for endothermy). ⏎ • Nucleated red blood cells (inherited reptilian trait, birds did not evolve mammalian anucleate RBCs) – slightly less efficient O₂ carry but compensated by rapid flow and high blood O₂ capacity. | • Oviparous – all birds lay hard calcified eggs (inherited from dinosaurs). Internal fertilization via cloaca "kiss". ⏎ • Extensive parental care – most incubate eggs using body heat (feathers) and feed hatchlings (innovation at high level among vertebrates). ⏎ • Single ovary in females (most species) to reduce weight (innovation for flight); reproductive organs shrink in off-season to save weight (innovation). | • Uric acid excretion (inherited reptile trait) – white paste-like urine mixed with feces in cloaca, eliminated as semisolid droppings (efficient water conservation). ⏎ • No urinary bladder (most birds) – minimizes weight; uric acid is expelled frequently (innovation for flight). ⏎ • Salt glands in marine birds (e.g. gulls) excrete salt from nostrils (innovation for osmoregulation in oceanic life). |  | • Endothermy at ~40°C – very high body temperature; requires active thyroid regulation of metabolism (innovation). ⏎ • Seasonal breeding hormones (gonadotropins) fluctuate strongly – linked to day length via pineal gland (innovation, extreme seasonality). ⏎ • Stress hormones (corticosterone) mobilize energy quickly for flight or long migrations (inherited but critical in birds). | • Vigorous immune system – high body temp and rapid metabolism give birds strong fever responses and quick wound healing (innovation relative to reptiles). ⏎ • Bursa of Fabricius (unique organ in young birds) produces B-cells for adaptive immunity (innovation in immune organ). ⏎ • Preen gland oil has antimicrobial properties – bird maintains feather health and reduces parasites (behavioral/chemical immune aid). | • Feathers – modified scales made of β-keratin (major innovation). Down feathers insulate; contour and flight feathers enable aerodynamics​www2.gwu.edu. Feathers also provide waterproofing and coloration for display/camouflage (multi-function innovation). ⏎ • Skin thin and lightweight, with few glands (inherited from dinosaurs); main gland is uropygial gland at tail for preen oil (inherited from early birds). ⏎ • Beaks and claws made of keratin – tools for feeding and defense (innovation replacing teeth). | • Light, hollow bones with internal struts (pneumatized from air sacs) – strong yet lightweight (innovation for flight). ⏎ • Fused skeleton in many parts: e.g. furcula (wishbone) acts as spring, keeled sternum for muscle attachment, fused tail vertebrae (pygostyle) for tail fan support (innovations for flight stability). ⏎ • Modified hand bones – wing has fused digits to support primaries; legs scaled and sturdy for landing (inherited from theropods, modified for perching). | • Enormous pectoral muscles (up to 20% of body mass in strong fliers) powering downstroke (innovation). ⏎ • Supracoracoideus muscle – unique pulley arrangement to lift wings on upstroke (innovation enabling rapid wing beats). ⏎ • Red aerobic muscle fibers in flight muscles for endurance; leg muscles vary (raptors have powerful grasping talons, perching birds have automatic flexor locking tendons – innovation for perching without effort). | • Vision extremely acute – many have 4-color (tetrachromatic) vision, some with UV sensitivity; raptors have visual acuity 2–3× human’s (innovation) for spotting prey​britannica.com. ⏎ • Inner ear with superb balance organs – crucial for flight equilibrium (innovation). ⏎ • Magnetoreception in migratory birds: ability to sense Earth’s magnetic field for navigation (innovation – magnetite crystals or quantum mechanisms in retina assist).
R100: Earliest Placental Mammals (Eutherians) | • Expanded neocortex and corpus callosum present – placentals evolved a nerve fiber bridge between hemispheres (innovation absent in marsupials)​en.wikipedia.org, allowing faster interhemispheric communication. ⏎ • Generally small, shrew-like animals with agile behavior and improving learning (inherited from small mammal ancestors). ⏎ • Nocturnal sensory bias still – strong olfaction and hearing; vision likely dichromatic and less acute (inherited). | • Generalized insectivore/omnivore diet – earliest placentals (e.g. Eomaia) likely ate insects and fruits; tribosphenic molars (three-cusped) allowed both slicing and crushing​en.wikipedia.org (innovation for dietary flexibility). ⏎ • Short digestive tract suited for nutrient-dense foods (inherited). ⏎ • Suckle young in utero via placenta (no lactation until birth, but placenta partly replaces prolonged milk-only phase – innovation freeing mother somewhat). | • High metabolic lungs – similar structure to other mammals, but with smaller body size, rapid breathing to sustain endothermy (inherited mammal trait). ⏎ • Diaphragm fully developed (inherited) enabling active ventilation even during running. ⏎ • Some may have had nasolabial glands to moisten air intake (speculative minor trait). | • Complete separation of circuits (inherited 4-chamber heart); likely high heart rates as small animals. ⏎ • Placenta’s blood supply: maternal-fetal blood in close proximity – placenta acted as an exchange interface for nutrients/gases (innovation: chorioallantoic placenta to nourish embryo internally). ⏎ • Umbilical cord present attaching fetus to uterine wall (innovation connecting to maternal circulation). | • Longer gestation than marsupials – fetus nourished by placenta for extended time (major innovation). Newborns therefore more developed at birth (hair, open eyes in some cases). ⏎ • Loss of epipubic bones in eutherians to allow abdominal expansion during pregnancy​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov (innovation facilitating longer gestation). ⏎ • Small, helpless young still, but less embryonic than marsupial joeys – required maternal care and lactation post-birth (inherited mammal trait). | • Advanced kidneys with longer Loops of Henle than monotremes/marsupials – better urine concentration (innovation improving water retention). ⏎ • Ureotelic (urea) excretion (inherited mammal trait), with separate urinary opening (no cloaca) in most – distinct urethra (innovation separating systems fully). ⏎ • Placenta also acted as excretory organ for fetus, removing wastes to mother’s blood (innovation of embryonic waste management). |  | • Placenta produces hormones (e.g. chorionic gonadotropin) to maintain pregnancy (innovation). ⏎ • Complex endocrine interplay between mother and fetus – fetal adrenal hormones trigger birth (innovation in birth timing). ⏎ • Higher thyroid activity to sustain higher metabolic rate typical of placentals (innovation vs lower metatherian levels). | • Maternal immune tolerance of fetus – placenta evolved mechanisms to prevent maternal immune rejection (innovation: suppression of T-cell response locally)​pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. ⏎ • Newborns with some maternal antibodies (IgG) crossing placenta in late gestation – born with passive immunity (innovation; marsupials lack this, they rely on milk). ⏎ • Adaptive immunity otherwise standard mammalian (inherited). | • Fur and vibrissae (whiskers) present (inherited). ⏎ • Fully separated anal and urogenital openings (in females, separate vagina and urethra; in males, separate anus – an innovation from marsupial/cloacal condition). ⏎ • Variations in skin glands: likely had sweat and sebaceous glands; possibly developed nipples (teats) for milk delivery (the oldest eutherian Juramaia might predate nipples, but eutherians soon had them – innovation improving feeding efficiency over milk patch). | • Simplified pelvic girdle (no epipubic bones) allowed pregnancy expansion (innovation)​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov. ⏎ • Flexible ankle joints – eutherians have an astragalus (talus) bone in ankle enabling diverse foot rotations (inherited from basal eutherians, aided rapid locomotion). ⏎ • Dental formula stabilizing: typical primitive eutherian had 3 incisors, 1 canine, 4 premolars, ~3 molars per side – slightly different from marsupials (innovation in dental evolution). | • Improved locomotor muscles – these small mammals were likely fast, scampering animals, with developed limb musculature for running and climbing (inherited). ⏎ • Facial muscles around mouth well-developed, enabling suckling on nipple (innovation beyond monotremes). ⏎ • Uterine muscles (myometrium) more complex to allow powerful contractions for live birth (innovation for parturition). | • Refined hearing – had cochleae with more coils than monotremes, improving frequency resolution (innovation). ⏎ • Olfaction still very acute – olfactory receptor repertoire expanded, useful for nocturnal life (inherited/improved). ⏎ • Touch: likely had sensitive touch via whiskers and paws (inherited), aiding nocturnal navigation and foraging.
R101: Flowering Plants (Angiosperms) | • No nervous system – instead use chemical and electrical signaling between cells (e.g. action potentials in phloem) to coordinate responses (alternate strategy). ⏎ • Plant hormones (auxins, etc.) act analogous to nerves, guiding growth and response (inherited plant strategy, more complex in angiosperms). ⏎ • Rapid electrical signals in some (Mimosa, Venus flytrap) allow quick movement without nerves (innovation in plants). | • Autotrophic (photosynthetic) – chloroplasts convert sunlight to sugars, so no digestive organs (inherited from ancestors). ⏎ • Roots absorb water/minerals; symbiotic fungi (mycorrhizae) assist nutrient uptake (innovation enhancing “digestion” of soil nutrients). ⏎ • Carnivory in a few angiosperms (e.g. sundew, pitcher plants) – evolved specialized leaves to trap insects and digest them with enzymes (innovation beyond typical autotrophy). | • Gas exchange via stomata on leaves – open to take in CO₂, release O₂, with guard cells regulating to reduce water loss (inherited from earlier vascular plants). ⏎ • No lungs: each cell respires individually; diffusion of gases through intercellular air spaces (inherited). ⏎ • Some aquatic angiosperms have snorkel-like structures or aerenchyma (air-filled tissues) to get O₂ to submerged parts (innovation for aquatic life). | • Vascular system (xylem & phloem) acts as circulatory analog: xylem vessels (an angiosperm innovation) conduct water and minerals upward​en.wikipedia.org, phloem sieve tubes distribute sugars from leaves (inherited from earlier vascular plants). ⏎ • Heartless circulation: driven by transpiration pull (water evaporation from leaves) and osmotic pressure – an efficient passive system (inherited plant strategy). ⏎ • Vessel elements in xylem (wider, perforated cells) allow faster flow than tracheids​en.wikipedia.org (innovation unique to flowering plants). | • Flowers – highly specialized reproductive organs (major innovation): contain stamens (produce pollen) and carpels (ovary containing ovules)​en.wikipedia.org. Enable directed pollination by attracting animals (innovation vs wind-only in gymnosperms). ⏎ • Double fertilization – two sperm from pollen: one fertilizes egg (forming embryo), another fuses with central cell to form triploid endosperm (nutritive tissue) (unique innovation)​www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Fruit – mature ovary enclosing seeds, aids dispersal by attracting animals or other mechanisms (innovation: protects seeds and enhances spread)​en.wikipedia.org. | • No discrete excretory organs – plants convert toxic ammonia to harmless amino acids or incorporate excess minerals into tissues (inherited plant strategy). ⏎ • Gas waste: O₂ from photosynthesis simply diffuses out via stomata (inherited). ⏎ • Leaf drop (abscission) to shed accumulated waste or heavy metals stored in leaves (innovation as seasonal “excretion”). |  | • Hormonal control: e.g. auxin regulates growth towards light, ethylene gas triggers fruit ripening, abscisic acid signals stomata to close under drought (sophisticated hormone system – innovation in diversity of signals). ⏎ • Flowering controlled by florigen and photoperiod – endocrine-like signaling to initiate reproductive development (innovation aligning reproduction with seasons). ⏎ • Secondary metabolites (e.g. alkaloids) production is hormonally modulated – some deter herbivores (chemical defense as extended endocrine function). | • Innate immunity: every cell can produce antimicrobial compounds (inherited strategy). Pathogen recognition receptors trigger responses like cell wall strengthening and phytoalexin production (inherited). ⏎ • Systemic acquired resistance – infection in one part induces whole-plant defensive readiness via hormone (salicylic acid) signaling (innovation akin to immune memory). ⏎ • Structural defenses: thorns, bark, lignin deter pathogens and herbivores (inherited physical defense). | • Dermal tissue: epidermis with waxy cuticle covering leaves and stems to prevent water loss (inherited land plant trait). ⏎ • Woody angiosperms form bark (cork cambium produces protective outer layer) – protects against injury and infection (innovation refined from gymnosperm bark). ⏎ • Trichomes (hair-like outgrowths) on leaves/stems – provide shade, reduce herbivory (inherited/innovation in different species). | • Cell walls of cellulose and lignin provide rigid support (inherited from earlier vascular plants). Lignified fibers allow tall growth and woody structure (innovation in some lineages). ⏎ • Vascular bundles with xylem and phloem run through stem and roots, often arranged in rings – structural support and transport combined (inherited). ⏎ • Flowers/fruits add new structures: e.g. ovary walls that become fruit tissues – a novel structural feature (innovation) to aid seed dispersal. | • No muscles, but motor cells in some organs allow movement: e.g. cells in pulvini (leaf joints) change turgor to fold leaves (as in sensitive plant) – hydraulic “muscle” (innovation). ⏎ • Vine growth: tendrils coil via differential cell growth rates when touching support (innovation enabling movement toward support). ⏎ • Rapid growth movements – e.g. Venus flytrap snaps by quick turgor changes and cell wall elasticity – acts like a muscle for trapping prey (innovation). | • Photoreception: leaves have photoreceptor proteins (phytochromes, cryptochromes) sensing light spectrum and direction, driving growth towards light (inherited ability). ⏎ • Gravireception: statoliths (starch granules) in root cells settle with gravity, telling roots to grow downward (inherited). ⏎ • Chemical senses: roots grow towards nutrient gradients; flowers sense temperature to open/close – all without nerves, but through cellular signaling (inherited plant sensory strategy).
R102: Tortoises (Land Turtles) | • Slow metabolism brain – small brain, adequate for routine behaviors (inherited reptile trait). Can learn simple spatial tasks over long lifespan. ⏎ • Longevity with minimal cognitive decline – suggests effective neuronal maintenance (perhaps an immune/neuroendocrine adaptation). ⏎ • Relies heavily on innate behaviors (e.g. homing to specific nesting sites, inherited). | • Herbivorous diet in giant tortoises – high-fiber diet processed in a large hindgut; they have an enlarged colon with symbiotic bacteria to ferment grass and leaves (innovation for herbivory). Smaller tortoises may be omnivorous. ⏎ • Slow digestion – can go long periods between meals, extracting maximum nutrients and water (inherited strategy with cold-blooded efficiency). ⏎ • Tongue and beak: no teeth, sharp horny beak to cut vegetation (inherited from turtle ancestors). | • Rigid shell prevents rib expansion, so tortoises use a unique muscular sling to breathe (innovation): muscles attached to the shell contract to force air in and out of lungs​sciencedaily.com​sciencedaily.com. ⏎ • Peripheral respiration: some gas exchange through moist cloacal tissue when water is scarce (a form of accessory breathing, minor innovation). ⏎ • Large lung capacity – helps tortoise float when crossing water (inherited turtle trait, secondary use). | • 3-chambered heart (two atria, one ventricle) like other turtles – but with partial separation and ability to shunt blood during dives or when withdrawn in shell (inherited). ⏎ • Low resting heart rate and blood pressure, reflecting slow metabolism (inherited). ⏎ • Can tolerate anoxia for periods (e.g. when holding breath in shell), by heart shunting and high lactic acid tolerance (innovation). | • Oviparous: female tortoises dig nests and lay eggs in soil (inherited reptile mode). No parental care after laying (inherited). ⏎ • Temperature-dependent sex: eggs incubated at higher temps often yield females (common in turtles, inherited trait). ⏎ • Slow maturation – tortoises take many years to reach sexual maturity, but can reproduce for decades (inherited trait linked to longevity). | • Uric acid excretion (inherited from reptiles) – pasty urate excreted via cloaca to conserve water. ⏎ • Bladder large – can store water and dilute wastes; tortoises often reabsorb water from urine in bladder during drought (innovation for arid habitat survival). ⏎ • Salt glands absent; rely on kidney and bladder; desert tortoises precipitate excess salts as solids in bladder (innovation). |  | • Slow thyroid activity suits long life and slow growth (inherited lower metabolism). ⏎ • Seasonal breeders: hormones triggered by environmental cues (rainfall for desert tortoises) – coordinate mating when conditions are right (inherited strategy). ⏎ • Stress response low – seem to have low cortisol spikes, possibly contributing to longevity (speculative endocrine adaptation). | • Strong innate immunity – heavy shell reduces injury risk, but if wounded they heal slowly yet effectively (inherited reptilian immune system). ⏎ • Long life immune maintenance: tortoises show sustained immunity even in old age, implying effective cellular repair (possible telomerase activity – speculative adaptation). ⏎ • Shell as immune shield – osteoderms in shell marrow still produce immune cells (inherited function repurposed). | • Bony shell (carapace on top, plastron below) – fused ribs and vertebrae form a domed armor (innovation unique to turtles) that protects against predators​sciencedaily.com. ⏎ • Keratin scutes cover shell bones, providing a tough shield and reducing water loss (inherited turtle trait). ⏎ • Skin on head/limbs is thick and scaly (inherited). | • Shell fusion: ribs, vertebrae, and shoulder girdle are fused into shell, sacrificing trunk flexibility for protection (inherited turtle trait). ⏎ • Elephantine legs in tortoises – column-like with strong bones to bear heavy shell (innovation for terrestrial weight support). ⏎ • Beak (rhamphotheca) instead of teeth – skull adapted to support horny beak for cropping plants (inherited from early turtles). | • Limb muscles arranged for graviportal locomotion – tortoises walk slowly with columnar legs under body, using triceps and shoulder muscles to lift shell weight (innovation vs sprawled gait). ⏎ • Neck muscles very flexible and strong to retract head into shell (inherited turtle feature). ⏎ • Jaw muscles moderately strong for grinding tough vegetation with beak (inherited). | • Color vision and good eyesight – tortoises see plants and distinguish ripe fruits (inherited from diurnal reptile ancestors). ⏎ • Decent sense of smell to locate food and detect other tortoises’ scent (inherited). ⏎ • Hearing is dull – mainly low-frequency vibrations; tortoises rely more on vision and smell (inherited constraint of turtles).
R103: Snakes (Serpentes) | • Streamlined nervous system – elongated body means spinal cord with many repeated segments, but brain relatively small (inherited from lizards). ⏎ • Specialized prey response circuits – fast strike and swallow reflexes coordinated by brainstem and spinal patterns (innovation for limbless predation). ⏎ • Some have heat-sensing trigeminal nerve integration (pit vipers) allowing infrared “vision” in the optic tectum (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. | • Carnivorous diet – highly adapted. Skull kinesis allows swallowing huge prey whole; jaws separate at symphysis (innovation). Prey is digested slowly over days with powerful enzymes and acids (inherited from carnivorous reptiles, intensified). ⏎ • Some have venom glands (modified salivary glands) delivering toxins via fangs to immobilize and pre-digest prey (major innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Simple linear gut – stretchable stomach, short intestine; can handle large prey pieces (inherited but extreme). | • One functional lung – right lung developed, left lung vestigial or absent in most species (innovation: body elongation trade-off)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Posterior part of lung often a saccular air sac without much blood supply, used for buoyancy in water snakes or as a bellows reserve (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Glottis can protrude out of mouth to breathe while swallowing large prey (innovation). | • 3-chambered reptile heart with ability to shunt blood when diving or during long prey swallowing (inherited from lizards). ⏎ • Elongated blood vessels to serve long body – organs staggered in placement to fit (innovation in circulatory layout). ⏎ • Generally low blood pressure and intermittent heart activity after big meals (snakes can lower heart rate while inactive, then raise after feeding – flexible cardio response, inherited ectotherm trait). | • Internal fertilization with hemipenes (paired penises) in males – stored inverted in tail, used one at a time (inherited squamate trait). ⏎ • Mostly oviparous – lay leathery eggs hidden in secure spots (inherited), but many colubrids and vipers are ovoviviparous/viviparous – retaining eggs internally until live birth (innovation for cooler climates). ⏎ • No parental care in most (inherited); a few like pythons coil around eggs to incubate (innovation of maternal care by shivering thermogenesis). | • Uricotelic – excrete uric acid as semisolid white paste (inherited reptile trait), via cloaca. ⏎ • No bladder in most snakes – ureters empty directly into cloaca; uric acid is voided periodically (innovation consistent with lightweight, narrow body – no bulky bladder). ⏎ • Cloacal scent glands – at tail base excrete foul-smelling musk when threatened (chemical defense, modified excretory product). |  | • Ecdysis regulated by thyroid hormones – snakes shed entire skin in one piece when growth or renewal needed (inherited reptile process, dramatic in snakes). ⏎ • Venom production in venomous snakes is under endocrine control (venom glands are modified serous glands influenced by hormonal cycles – innovation for predation). ⏎ • Temperature-dependent sex is rare in snakes (most have genotypic sex determination, inherited change from many reptiles), so sex hormones follow fixed genetic sex. | • Robust innate immunity – despite infrequent feeding, snakes can handle septic prey; blood contains strong antimicrobial peptides (some snake blood inhibits bacteria, possibly evolved due to eating carrion or high bacterial loads – innovation). ⏎ • Venom also contains enzymes to reduce prey’s immune defense (innovation for prey subjugation, not self-immunity but offensive immunity). ⏎ • Snakes lack lymph nodes (common in reptiles)​en.wikipedia.org but have diffuse lymphatic tissue; they combat infection with circulating immune cells (inherited). | • Scaled skin – overlapping scales allow flexibility and protect snake as it moves in rough terrain (inherited). ⏎ • Shedding skin renews and can remove parasites (innovation in frequency – some snakes shed multiple times a year as they grow). ⏎ • Some snakes have cryptic coloration or bold patterns for camouflage or warning (e.g. mimicry of venomous species), an integumentary adaptation (innovation in many lineages). | • Skull highly kinetic – many bones loosely articulated by ligaments (jaws, palate) to engulf large prey (innovation)​en.wikipedia.org​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Hundreds of vertebrae/ribs – vertebral column extended, each vertebra with extra articulation (zygosphenes) in some to strengthen spine (innovation for flexibility + support). ⏎ • No limbs – pectoral and pelvic girdles lost or vestigial (inherited serpentine trait); vestigial pelvic spurs remain in pythons/boas (inherited from lizard ancestors). | • Axial muscles (segmented along body) used for locomotion – snakes have multiple modes: lateral undulation, rectilinear crawling, concertina, sidewinding (innovation in muscle coordination). ⏎ • Jaw muscles relatively modest for bite force, but inertial feeding – muscles “walk” jaws over prey (innovation in prey handling). ⏎ • Constriction in boas/pythons: extremely strong trunk muscles wrap and squeeze prey, stopping circulation (innovation as a predation strategy). | • Forked tongue + Jacobson’s organ gives acute smell/taste – snake constantly “tastes” air for prey or mates (innovation refining chemoreception). ⏎ • Infrared heat pits in pit vipers, some boas/pythons – detect warm prey in darkness by imaging infrared radiation (major sensory innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Eyes: many snakes have fixed clear scale (brille) instead of eyelid (inherited); vision ranges from excellent (tree snakes) to poor (burrowers). Most see motion well but some color vision lost (inherited constraint of nocturnal ancestors).
R104: Grass (e.g. early Grasses, Poaceae) | • No neurons – growth and responses governed by hormones and cell signaling (inherited plant strategy). ⏎ • Local electrical signals can propagate when cut or grazed, triggering defense chemistry (innovation: wounded grass emits electric potential to adjacent cells). ⏎ • Induced signaling: grazing triggers systemic chemical signals (like jasmonate) to grow new shoots (in place of neural response). | • Autotroph – photosynthesis in leaves produces sugars (inherited). ⏎ • C₄ photosynthesis in many grasses – biochemical pathway that concentrates CO₂ in bundle sheath cells, improving efficiency in high heat/light (innovation ~ later evolution in grasses)​quizlet.com. (46% of grass species are C₄​en.wikipedia.org.) ⏎ • Extensive fibrous root system efficiently takes up water and nutrients from soil surface (innovation vs taproot for quickly exploiting rainfall). | • Stomata in linear leaves open/close to regulate gas exchange (inherited). Many grasses have stomata on both leaf surfaces and can handle intense sunlight for maximal CO₂ intake (inherited, optimized in open habitats). ⏎ • Kranz anatomy in C₄ grasses – wreath-like arrangement of cells for efficient CO₂ pumping (innovation aiding respiration in hot climates). ⏎ • High silica content reduces herbivore grazing (less respiration load due to fewer leaves being eaten – indirect benefit). | • Xylem vessels and phloem in vascular bundles run through leaves and stems (inherited vascular trait). ⏎ • Efficient transpiration stream: deep or extensive roots draw water; evaporation from leaves pulls continuous water flow (inherited). ⏎ • Narrow leaves reduce transpiration demand and ensure water reaches tip (adaptation to savanna environments – innovation in morphology). | • Wind-pollinated flowers – small, non-showy florets in inflorescences (spikelets) produce large amounts of pollen (innovation for open habitats)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Double fertilization with endosperm (inherited angiosperm trait) ensures each seed has food reserve (critical for grass seeds/g rains). ⏎ • Continuous meristems: grasses grow from base (intercalary meristems) allowing rapid regrowth after grazing or mowing​en.wikipedia.org (innovation for reproductive and vegetative regeneration). | • No discrete excretory organs – wastes (like oxygen) diffuse out or are incorporated into tissues (inherited plant condition). ⏎ • Excess salts may be stored in vacuoles or old leaves which later die back (inherited strategy). ⏎ • Leaf shedding during drought can rid plant of accumulated toxins or conserve water (adaptive strategy). |  | • Gibberellins promote rapid vertical growth to outcompete neighbors (inherited hormone). ⏎ • Abscisic acid rises during drought, causing dormancy and closure of stomata (inherited water-stress response). Grasses enter dormancy (brown out) under drought, reviving when rain comes (endocrine-regulated survival strategy). ⏎ • C₄ pathway regulated by light and sugars – unique enzyme (PEP carboxylase) controlled by day/night cycle (innovation in metabolic endocrine-like regulation). | • Constitutive defenses: high silica phytoliths in leaves deter microbial breakdown and herbivory​en.wikipedia.org (physical-chemical immune defense). ⏎ • Induced chemical defenses: when grazed, grasses can increase silica or produce phenolics to reduce digestibility (innovation: dynamic defense)​frontiersin.org. ⏎ • Symbiotic endophytes (fungi living in grass) produce alkaloids deterring pests – mutualistic immune strategy (innovation). | • Thin narrow leaves with waxy cuticle to reduce water loss (inherited adaptation). ⏎ • Ligule at leaf sheath junction prevents water and dirt from entering sheath​en.wikipedia.org (innovation unique to grasses). ⏎ • Silica-hardened blades – microscopic silica bodies stiffen leaves​en.wikipedia.org, making them tough and abrasive (innovation defending against herbivores). | • Hollow stems (culms) with solid nodes – provide strength with minimal material (innovation for height with economy)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Intercalary meristems at base of leaves and internodes – enable continuous growth after parts are removed (major innovation)​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Rhizomes or stolons in many grasses for spreading laterally and perennating (innovation in skeletal structure for clonal growth). | • No muscle tissue, but rapid growth acts as movement – grasses can quickly send up new shoots (pseudo-muscular response to grazing). ⏎ • Turgor changes in pulvini at base of leaves allow leaves to orient vertically in intense sun (paraheliotropism) or fold in drought – a motor function via water pressure (innovation to avoid excess heat/water loss). ⏎ • Flexibility: fibrous stems bend in wind without breaking (inherited trait of fibrous support). | • Light sensing: grasses grow toward light; phytochrome system tells stem to elongate if shaded (inherited plant phototropism). ⏎ • Gravity sensing: ensures roots grow downward, shoots upward (inherited). ⏎ • Chemical signaling: can detect competition (e.g. root exudates from neighbors) and adjust growth (inherited). No specialized sensory organs, but whole plant responds to environment cues.
R105: Dinosaurs Extinction (K–Pg Event, 66 Mya) | • Selective survival: creatures with flexible behaviors fared better – mammals and birds could shelter/burrow and scavenge (those adaptable neural patterns survived)​en.wikipedia.org. Large non-avian dinosaurs with fixed behaviors perished. ⏎ • Post-impact, ecosystems favored small, smart generalists – mammalian brains allowed opportunistic feeding (innovation that aided survival) and rapid reproduction. | • Food chain collapse: herbivores died as plants died; survivors were detritivores or omnivores that could eat seeds, decaying matter, and small prey​en.wikipedia.org. Mammals and birds with broad diets (insects, seeds, worms) persisted (inherited omnivory advantage). ⏎ • Dormancy/hibernation: some mammals may have entered torpor to survive lean times (innovation helping ride out famine). Crocodilians survived by fasting and living off stored energy (inherited cold-blooded strategy). | • Atmospheric dust reduced sunlight – plant photosynthesis stalled​en.wikipedia.org, oxygen levels may have dipped. Small animals with lower O₂ needs (and possibly the ability to lower metabolic rate) had an edge (innovation/inherited trait). ⏎ • Birds’ and mammals’ efficient lungs (air sacs in birds, diaphragm in mammals) let them sustain activity in low-light, cooler conditions (advantage). | • Only small animals (<25 kg) survived on land​en.wikipedia.org; a small body means circulatory needs are lower and easier to maintain on scarce food (inherited advantage)​en.wikipedia.org. Larger animals couldn’t sustain their circulation without abundant food. ⏎ • Warm-blooded birds/mammals could keep organs perfused in cold “impact winter”, whereas ectothermic dinos got lethargic (advantage of endothermic circulation). | • Surviving mammals carried unborn young internally – protected from harsh outside conditions (placental reproduction conferred some safety, innovation) – vs egg-laying dinosaurs whose eggs were exposed and failed to hatch.
