Bird eggs survived only in hardy lineages (e.g. ground-dwelling birds). ⏎ • Short generation time of survivors (small mammals) meant faster evolution post-event to fill niches (inherited trait: quick breeding cycles). This led to rapid adaptive radiation after extinction. | • Global darkness and drought meant limited drinking water and altered chemistry. Mammals’ efficient kidneys and ability to concentrate urine helped them survive dehydration (inherited). ⏎ • Many amphibians and large reptiles died; mammals and birds could move to find water or hibernate to conserve it (advantage). ⏎ • Waste recycling: burrowing mammals could re-eat fecal matter (coprophagy) to extract every nutrient (some rodents do this – possible survival strategy). |  | • Profound stress: animals that could hormonally enter torpor or hibernation (lowered thyroid and adrenal activity) survived the worst period. E.g. some ancestral placentals might have hibernated​pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov (innovation enabling survival of extended cold and scarce food). ⏎ • Birds and mammals ramped up stress hormones (cortisol) to mobilize fat reserves and remain active in resource-sparse environment (inherited fight-or-flight physiology put to extreme use). | • Mass die-off of most species meant less disease transmission (paradoxically survivors faced fewer pathogens). However, survivors’ immunity was tested by environmental stress. Hardiest individuals with robust immune genes survived (selection). ⏎ • Smaller populations went through genetic bottlenecks, possibly purging some deleterious alleles – leading to generally healthy immune systems in the aftermath (evolutionary outcome). | • Insulating coverings – fur in mammals, feathers in birds – helped maintain body heat during the post-impact cold and darkness (critical innovation)​brainly.com. By contrast, scaled dinosaurs lacked such insulation (except feathered ones, and indeed only feathered ones – birds – survived). ⏎ • Burrows and nests became refuges: mammals and some birds could hide underground or in sheltered spots, using soil and environmental cover as an “external integument” against harsh climate (behavioral use of integumentary environment). | • Small body frames of survivors withstood environmental extremes better. Skeletal flexibility (e.g. mammals could burrow or squeeze into shelters) aided survival (inherited small-animal trait). ⏎ • Flight in birds allowed some to escape local disasters and find refugia (bird skeleton adapted for flight was a lifesaver – innovation). ⏎ • Non-avian dinosaur skeletons, specialized for massive size, were maladapted to new conditions of scarce resources – they perished, leaving niches that mammal skeletons diversified to fill after. | • Burrowing musculature in mammals (e.g. strong forelimbs in shrew-like animals) allowed underground survival during firestorms and impact winter (innovation for survival). ⏎ • Flight muscles in birds allowed relocation/migration to find food and better climate (inherited ability; many early bird survivors were waterfowl/shorebirds that could fly and dive for food). ⏎ • Ectothermic torpor: crocodilians and turtles survived using minimal movement, slowing metabolism and muscular activity in sheltered aquatic environments (inherited survival strategy). | • Senses guided survivors to food: mammals with keen smell/hearing found buried insects and detritus to eat (inherited sensory advantages)​en.wikipedia.org. Birds with good vision scavenged carcasses and spotted remaining plants/seeds. ⏎ • Dark-adapted vision – nocturnal mammals and birds functioned in the dim, sunless period better than diurnal reptiles (advantage of inherited nocturnality). ⏎ • Magneto/astro-navigation: irrelevant during the prolonged darkness; instead, sensory generalists (able to switch to scent and touch) thrived. Survivors tended to be those not overly specialized on a single sense or food source.
R106: Afrotheres (1st placental split, e.g. Hyrax) | • Diverse clade – early Afrotheres (like tenrecs, elephant shrews) were small and nocturnal: strong olfactory and auditory processing (inherited small mammal traits). ⏎ • Some showed heterothermy (tenrecs can enter torpor) – neurological control of body temperature flexibility (innovation allowing survival in variable climates). ⏎ • Overall brain sizes follow body size; elephants later evolved very large brains with advanced memory and social cognition (innovation in one afrothere lineage). | • Early afrotheres mostly insectivorous (tenrecs, elephant shrews) – simple gut (inherited). ⏎ • Hyraxes and elephants (later Afrotheres) became herbivorous: hyraxes have multichambered stomachs (foregut fermentation) like ruminants (innovation convergent with ungulates), elephants are hindgut fermenters with an enormous cecum (innovation for bulk feeding). ⏎ • Low metabolic rate feeders: many Afrotheres (tenrecs, golden moles) can eat less per body weight due to lower metabolism, surviving lean conditions (inherited trait). | • Standard mammalian lungs/diaphragm (inherited). ⏎ • Elephant shrews have high activity and correspondingly efficient lungs for their size (inherited mammal trait). ⏎ • Aquatic afrotheres (manatees) evolved dense lungs for buoyancy control (innovation in that subgroup), but generally Afrotheria as a whole has typical mammalian respiration. | • Internal testes (no scrotum) in many Afrotheres (elephants, hyraxes, manatees) mean they tolerate higher core temperature; their circulatory system adapted to cool blood to testes internally (innovation or retained ancestral trait)​eurekalert.org. ⏎ • Elephants have a huge heart (weighing 20+ kg) with very slow rate – specialized circulation for a large body (innovation in that lineage). ⏎ • Small afrotheres (tenrecs) often have lower blood pressure and can undergo torpor – circulatory flexibility (inherited/innovation). | • Placental, live-bearing – similar to other eutherians (inherited). ⏎ • Some afrotheres have long gestations (elephants ~22 months, manatees ~12 months – extreme innovation in pregnancy length) and produce well-developed young. ⏎ • Delayed implantation in elephant shrews (a mechanism to align birth with seasons – innovation in reproduction timing). | • Urea excretion via kidneys (inherited). ⏎ • Many Afrotheres (like desert tenrecs) have very efficient kidneys to conserve water (innovation for arid adaptation). ⏎ • Manatees can excrete excess salt via kidney adjustments (no salt gland, but very large kidneys – innovation for living in sea/brackish water). |  | • Some retain ancestral traits: e.g. elephants lack scrotum due to loss of genes for testicular descent​eurekalert.org – suggests lower reliance on cooling sperm via endocrine means, possibly slight differences in sperm development (unique hormonal milieu). ⏎ • Elephant matriarchs have long-term temporal gland secretions (musth in males, temporal gland in females) regulated by hormones – unique social endocrine signals (innovation in proboscideans). ⏎ • Heterothermy in tenrecs – ability to let body temperature drop, under endocrine control (thyroid downregulation), to save energy (innovation). | • Tenrecs and golden moles often have lower body temperatures and irregular thermoregulation, possibly reducing immune efficiency but also parasite load (they often have ectoparasites though). ⏎ • Elephants have strong immune systems (they rarely get cancer; p53 gene duplications in elephants are known – innovation enhancing DNA repair and cancer suppression). ⏎ • Afrotheres show no glaring immune deficiencies – immune systems are standard placental type (inherited). | • Varied integument: thick skin in elephants (up to 2.5 cm, with little hair – innovation for preventing water loss and heat, plus wrinkles trap moisture/mud to cool). ⏎ • Spines in tenrecs (some species) akin to hedgehogs – convergent innovation for defense, showing afrotheres can evolve diverse skin structures. ⏎ • Many Afrotheres have sparse fur (elephants, rhinos have ancestral afrothere ties) – possibly inherited from a low-metabolism ancestor or adapted to hot climates. | • Early afrotheres were small, but this clade produced some of the largest land mammals (elephants) and smallest (shrews). Skeletally: flexible spinal articulations in xenarthrans (if included; actually xenarthrans are separate clade) – ignore. ⏎ • Elephant skeleton: columnar limbs, graviportal stance (innovation for heavy weight), skull with pneumatic sinuses to reduce weight (innovation). ⏎ • Hyrax skeleton retains some primitive features: e.g. multiple cranial arteries like primitive ungulates (inherited trait) and rudimentary hoof-like nails (innovation convergent with ungulates). | • Elephants: enormous muscular trunk (over 40,000 muscles) – unique appendage for manipulation and breathing (innovation). ⏎ • Tenrecs: low body temperature means lower muscle tone; they can enter torpor with muscles relaxing (inherited/heterothermy trait). ⏎ • Elephant shrews: extremely fast runners for size – well-developed limb muscles for sprinting (innovation in this clade for evading predators). | • Many Afrotheres rely on smell and hearing: e.g. elephants have superb sense of smell (more olfactory genes than any other mammal, innovation) and infrasonic hearing for long-distance communication (innovation). ⏎ • Vibrations: elephants sense seismic vibrations through feet (innovation in sensory extension). ⏎ • Vision tends to be less critical in some (elephants have modest eyesight), whereas elephant shrews have keen vision for daytime (some variation – mostly inherited mammal senses with specializations in each lineage).
R107: Xenarthra (2nd placental split: armadillos, sloths, anteaters) | • Very low metabolic rate brains – sloths have the smallest brain-to-body ratio among mammals, reflecting energy-saving (inherited xenarthran trait). Cognitively slow but adequate for simple tasks. ⏎ • Specialized neural adaptations: anteaters have enlarged somatosensory cortex for the long tongue and snout, to precisely control licking and detect ant/termite vibrations (innovation). ⏎ • Spinal cord specializations with xenarthrous vertebrae likely give enhanced proprioception of their rigid trunk (innovation name-giving feature aiding posture). | • Anteaters: no teeth, long sticky tongue with huge salivary glands – swallow insects whole and grind in muscular stomach (innovation for myrmecophagy). Stomach has keratinous spines inside to mash ants, acting like a gizzard (innovation). ⏎ • Sloths: entirely herbivorous, multi-chambered stomach for fermenting leaves (convergent to ruminants, innovation); extremely slow digestion (can take days) to extract limited nutrients (innovation aligned with low metabolism). ⏎ • Armadillos: omnivorous, peg-like simple teeth (no enamel) for grubs and plants (inherited primitive dentition), moderately long intestines for mixed diet. | • Standard mammal lungs but used at low capacity due to low metabolic need (inherited). ⏎ • Sloths have slow breathing; can even hold breath ~minutes while sloth swims (they can close nostrils, slight aquatic ability – innovation). ⏎ • Giant anteater has elongated nostrils but normal lung structure; breathes slowly even when active (inherited low metabolism adaptation). | • Low heart rates and low blood pressure – xenarthrans ~40-60% metabolism of other mammals their size​sciencedirect.com (clade-wide inherited trait). ⏎ • Extra articulations (xenarthrous joints) in lumbar spine add rigidity, possibly aiding their ability to use the diaphragm effectively despite odd postures (speculative benefit). ⏎ • Armadillos have higher blood volume relative to body to compensate for periodic apnea when digging (innovation: can hold breath ~6 min while burrowing). | • Placental, with variable reproduction: armadillos (like nine-banded) show polyembryony – one fertilized egg can split into four identical embryos (innovation) resulting in quadruplets​en.wikipedia.org. ⏎ • Delayed implantation common (armadillos delay embryo implant for months, likely to time birth with favorable season – innovation). ⏎ • Sloths: usually single offspring after long gestation (~6 months in three-toed) and extensive maternal care (carry baby for months – inherited mammal parenting). | • Urea excretion (inherited). ⏎ • Sloths: very infrequent urination/defecation – they descend from trees about once a week to void; colon accumulates waste (behavioral adaptation to avoid predation, but requires tolerance of high waste load – sloth metabolism handles it, innovation in excretory timing). ⏎ • Armadillos: kidneys concentrate urine well; desert species can conserve water by producing dry feces and concentrated urine (innovation for arid survival). |  | • Low thyroid output – xenarthrans maintain low body temps (sloths ~30–34°C) and slow metabolism​sciencedirect.com, likely via thyroid hormone levels on lower end (inherited clade trait). ⏎ • Unique reproductive hormone patterns in armadillos: they can pause pregnancy (delayed implantation) and orchestrate identical quadruplets (suggests unusual early pregnancy signaling – innovation). ⏎ • Adrenal glands – possibly enlarged in armadillos to cope with stress of burrowing and heavy armor (they handle stress well, evident in their wide habitat tolerance – speculative). | • Robust innate immunity: armadillos have low body temp (~33°C) which makes them susceptible to certain bacteria like leprosy (Mycobacterium leprae thrives in cooler temps) – an odd consequence of their physiology​sciencedirect.com. They tolerate chronic infections well, indicating unique immune tolerance (inherited trait, albeit problematic for humans!). ⏎ • Armor (armadillo) provides physical immunity – hard to bite through (innovation for defense). ⏎ • Sloths host symbiotic algae in fur, perhaps producing chemicals that repel parasites (a possible immune aid, innovation by symbiosis). | • Tough hide or armor: armadillos have bony osteoderm plates covered by keratin scales – provides defense (major integumentary innovation) and also prevents water loss in dry burrows. ⏎ • Sloth fur: dense and grooved, holds algae that camouflage green and may supplement diet when sloths groom (unique integument ecosystem, innovation). Fur hangs upside-down oriented (grows from belly toward back) to shed rain when hanging (innovation for arboreal inverted life). ⏎ • Anteaters: thick hide on limbs to resist ant bites and a big bushy tail they use as a blanket when resting (behavioral use of integument for warmth). | • Xenarthrous vertebrae – extra interlocking joints in lower spine that give name to clade (innovation). Provide rigidity for digging (armadillos) and hanging (sloths)​sciencedirect.com. ⏎ • Armadillo shell: carapace of bony plates across shoulders, back, hips; flexible bands in middle for some movement – unique skeletal adaptation (innovation). ⏎ • Sloths: long curved claws and hook-like phalanges to hang; reduced tibia/fibula fusion for rotating ankles upside down – specialized skeletal adaptation for hanging inverted (innovation). | • Armadillos: powerful digging muscles in forelimbs – large claws and strong pectoral/shoulder musculature to burrow for food and shelter (innovation). ⏎ • Anteaters: huge tongue muscles anchored to sternum (instead of hyoid) – extend tongue up to 2 feet with sticky saliva to capture ants (major feeding muscle innovation). Also strong forearm muscles to rip open ant nests (innovation). ⏎ • Sloths: reduced muscle mass (they have ~30% of expected muscle for their size) – saves energy; muscles mostly slow-twitch endurance fibers, allowing them to hang for long periods without fatigue (innovation for energy economy). | • Armadillo senses: decent smell for finding insects underground, poor eyesight (inherited nocturnal burrower traits). They can feel ground vibrations via armored shell and limbs (inherited). ⏎ • Sloths: rely on vision less; decent smell and hearing to detect predators, but generally slow responses (inherited). ⏎ • Anteaters: no teeth so hearing and smell are critical – they can sniff out ant colonies by scent (keen olfaction, innovation), and have good hearing to hear ant movement in nests. Vision is limited (inherited small-eyed trait).
R108: Laurasiatheria (Placental superorder: ungulates, carnivores, bats, etc.) | • Large diversity – includes very high-performance brains in carnivores (e.g. dogs’ social cognition) and simpler ones in shrews. As a clade, no single neural trait, but generally advanced sensory-processing for active lifestyles (inherited mammal trait, diversified). ⏎ • Many Laurasiatherians are predators or fast movers, so reflexes and motor coordination circuits are well-developed (e.g. flight in bats requires superb neural timing – innovation in Chiroptera). ⏎ • Echolocating bats evolved specialized auditory cortex maps for sonar (innovation for high-frequency processing). | • Ranges from insectivory (shrews) to herbivory (ungulates) to omnivory (pigs) to carnivory (cats) – digestive systems diversified accordingly (superorder trait is diversity). ⏎ • Multi-chambered stomachs evolved in ruminant artiodactyls (deer, cattle) for fermenting plant cellulose (innovation) and hindgut fermentation in perissodactyls (horses) (innovation in different branch). ⏎ • Carnivorans developed shearing carnassial teeth for slicing meat (innovation in dentition) and relatively short guts (inherited for meat diet). Overall, Laurasiatheria exhibits the full spectrum of mammalian digestive adaptations. | • No universal respiratory trait – all have diaphragmatic lungs. But extremes: bats have incredibly high lung surface area and capillary density for sustained flight (innovation within clade), whales modified lungs/ribcage for diving and rapid exchange at surface (innovation in cetaceans). ⏎ • Many laurasiatheres (e.g. hoofed mammals) have large nostrils/turbinates to support high oxygen demands when running (inherited high-metabolism adaptation). ⏎ • Hibernation ability in some (bats, hedgehogs) – can drastically slow respiration in torpor (innovation for energy saving in cold). | • 4-chambered heart in all (inherited mammal). Several lineages pushed it: whales have huge hearts and bradycardia dive response (innovation), bats have very rapid heartbeats during flight (up to >1000 bpm in tiny bats) to fuel flight (innovation of rate flexibility). ⏎ • Counter-current heat exchangers in legs of ungulates (to reduce heat loss) and in whale fins (to avoid overheating) – circulatory adaptations to environment (innovations in vascular design). ⏎ • Blood: some (deep-diving whales) have extra myoglobin and blood volume for oxygen storage (innovation). | • Placental viviparity (inherited). Many laurasiatheres have short to moderate gestations with more developed young than marsupials but less so than primates (variable). ⏎ • Some evolved unique structures: e.g. placental villi deeply invasive in carnivores (zonary placenta), diffuse large placentas in horses – specialized for each group (innovations in placentation forms). ⏎ • Parental care high – most provide extended care via nursing; social systems for rearing (wolves cooperative breeding, bats crèche young, etc. – behavioral reproductive innovations in clade). | • Urea excretion via advanced kidneys in all (inherited). Many laurasiatheres show specializations: desert rodents (jerboas) concentrate urine extremely to conserve water (an example in rodent clade of Euarchontoglires though), marine laurasiatheres (whales) have reniculate kidneys with multiple small lobes to handle salt load (innovation). ⏎ • Carnivores have very acidic urine to mark territory (some produce pungent nitrogen wastes for marking – not excretory efficiency but communication). |  | • This clade includes endotherms with some of highest metabolisms – e.g. hummingbirds (if considering birds from Laurasiatheria context, but no, just mammals). Among mammals, shrews (in Eulipotyphla) have extremely high thyroid output to sustain frenetic activity (inherited from small placental ancestor, extreme in shrew). ⏎ • Bats evolved unique hibernation endocrine controls to allow shutting down metabolism for winter (innovation in chiropterans). ⏎ • Ungulates often have seasonal rut (mating season) driven by surges in testosterone (males grow antlers etc.), and photoperiod-driven breeding hormones (innovation in large herbivore life-history timing). | • Robust immune systems across clade (inherited mammal). Some specific notes: bats have an immune tolerance allowing them to carry viruses without ill effects (dampened inflammatory response – innovation) which is of keen research interest. ⏎ • Carnivores often face wounds from prey – strong wound healing and infection resistance (e.g. big cats heal fast, possibly evolved). ⏎ • Domestic dogs (a carnivore) show selection for tame immune reactions to living with humans (a very recent artificial selection, not broadly applicable to clade). Overall, laurasiatheres do not have a unifying immune quirk beyond typical mammalian immunity. | • Laurasiatheres include fur-covered mammals of all sorts (inherited fur trait), with some dramatic forms: spines in hedgehogs (modified hairs for defense, innovation), thick blubber in whales (subcutaneous fat layer as thermal integument, innovation in marine branch). ⏎ • Horns/antlers in ungulates – keratin horns (rhinos) or bony antlers shed annually (deer) – novel integumentary structures for defense and mating displays (innovation in Artiodactyla). ⏎ • Camouflage fur patterns (striped tigers, spotted deer) common – coat colors adapted to environment (inherited adaptability). | • Laurasiatheria gave rise to hoofed feet – keratin hooves and elongated limb bones in ungulates (innovation for running). ⏎ • Flight skeleton in bats – hand bones extremely elongated with skin membranes (major innovation making them the only flying mammals). ⏎ • Cetaceans (whales/dolphins) have hyper-streamlined skeletons: forelimb flippers, lost hindlimbs (vestigial pelvis), and tail flukes (no bones, supported by fibrous tissue) – huge skeletal overhaul for aquatic life (innovation). | • Extreme diversity: from cheetah’s sprint muscles (fast-twitch fibers for bursts) to whale tail muscles (powerful axial musculature for swimming). Generally, laurasiatheres evolved muscles for endurance running (cursorial ungulates have large aerobic muscle capacity – innovation), or powered flight (bats’ pectoral muscles and calcar muscles to maneuver – innovation). ⏎ • Shivering thermogenesis in bats and small insectivores to generate heat (inherited in endotherms, critical for hibernation arousal – well-developed in this clade). | • High-frequency hearing in many: bats evolved ultrasonic echolocation (massive sensory innovation) and many carnivores hear ultrasonic rodent squeaks (inherited usage). ⏎ • Olfaction: carnivores and ungulates have large olfactory epithelium – critical for hunting and social communication (inherited, refined). ⏎ • Vision: varies – many ungulates have panoramic vision (eyes on sides, great motion detection, poor depth), carnivores have forward eyes with depth perception for hunting (innovation within clade), bats often have small eyes (some use vision less, others like fruit bats have large eyes for night vision).
R109: Euarchontoglires (Primates, Rodents, etc.) | • Trend of high intelligence in primates – large cerebral cortex, complex social brains (innovation culminating in apes/humans). ⏎ • Rodents favor olfaction and whisker tactile maps – enlarged sensory cortex for vibrissae (innovation for nocturnal navigation). ⏎ • Visual specialization in primates – forward-facing eyes, color vision, and expanded visual cortex (innovation for arboreal life). Overall, versatile brains capable of learning and memory (inherited, refined in multiple lineages). | • Rodents: ever-growing incisors for gnawing (major dental innovation) and a gap (diastema) with no canines; complex chewing motions to grind plant matter (innovation). Many have enlarged cecum for hindgut fermentation of cellulose (e.g. guinea pigs – innovation). ⏎ • Primates: generalized omnivorous teeth (incisors, canines, molars) – early primates ate fruits/insects, requiring versatile digestion (inherited). Later primates (e.g. folivorous monkeys) evolved complex stomachs or gut microbiomes (howler monkeys have enlarged colon – minor innovation). | • Standard mammalian respiration, but some special cases: small tree shrews (close to primates) are high-metabolic – rapid breathing (inherited small-mammal trait). ⏎ • Some primates (e.g. humans, apes) evolved descended larynx enabling complex vocalizations at cost of choking risk (innovation for speech in humans). ⏎ • Generally, no unique respiratory organ across clade, but adaptability – e.g. some flying lemurs (colugos) have broad ribcages to support gliding membrane (skeletal, not organ difference). | • Four-chambered heart (inherited). ⏎ • Many small members (mice, lemurs) have high heart rates; some (lemurs) can slow metabolism (dwarf lemurs hibernate with low heart rate – innovation in primates). ⏎ • Some primates have relatively large brains requiring steady blood supply – evolution of Carotid arterial retia in some to buffer blood flow (innovation in brain perfusion). | • Primates: typically one baby at a time, long gestation for body size, placenta hemochorial (intimate connection) – producing very developed newborn brain (innovation: extended gestation and parental care). ⏎ • Rodents/lagomorphs: often large litters, short gestation (inherited prolific strategy), altricial young that mature quickly – rapid reproductive turnover (innovation for quick population growth). ⏎ • Dermal mating signals in primates (e.g. sexual swellings) to coordinate reproduction – secondary innovation in social breeding. | • Urea excretion (inherited). ⏎ • Many Euarchontoglires are small and need to conserve water: desert rodents have extremely efficient kidneys (long loops of Henle – innovation to concentrate urine beyond typical mammal). ⏎ • Primates (esp. in tropics) have less pressure on water, some (like howler monkeys) even have frequent defecation of moist feces due to abundant water (no special adaptation). Overall, excretory systems typical, aside from rodent extremes. |  | • Primates: complex hormones for long juvenile periods, e.g. extended parental care linked to oxytocin bonds (innovation in social hormones). Also menstrual cycles in higher primates with cyclic estrogen/progesterone (innovation – endometrial shedding in Anthropoids unlike other mammals). ⏎ • Rodents: some can enter torpor daily (hamsters) regulated by melatonin and leptin changes (innovation for energy saving). Many have induced ovulation (e.g. rabbits/lagomorphs – ovulate upon mating, endocrine adaptation). | • Rodents: strong innate immunity, but also carriers of many diseases due to close quarter living and fast reproduction (inherited, not an adaptation but notable). Some evolved tolerance to certain plant toxins in diet (via liver enzymes, part of immune/detox system – innovation). ⏎ • Primates: generally strong immune systems; some old-world primates evolved simian immunodeficiency virus tolerance (partial adaptation). The clade doesn’t have a unified immune twist aside from typical placental features and some species-specific pathogen resistances. | • Fur: Euarchontoglires mostly furry (inherited). Some specialized: e.g. vibrissae (whiskers) highly developed in rodents and tree shrews for tactile sensing (innovation in using integumentary organs as sensors). ⏎ • Nails instead of claws in primates (innovation): flattened nails on digits for better touch and manipulation; touch pads with dermatoglyphs (fingerprints) in higher primates improve grip (innovation). ⏎ • Colorful sexual skin in some primates (mandrill face, baboon rump) – integument used in display via hormonal changes (innovation in social signaling). | • Rodents/Lagomorphs: lightweight, flexible skeletons for quick movement; high reproductive output sometimes leads to skeletal calcium mobilization (female rodents can mobilize bone calcium for milk – physiological adaptation). Ever-growing incisors requiring constant gnawing (dental skeletal innovation). ⏎ • Primates: grasping hands/feet with opposable thumbs or big toes (major skeletal innovation for arboreal life), and forward-facing orbits with postorbital bar/enclosure for eye protection (innovation for better vision). ⏎ • Scandentia (tree shrews) and colugos (flying lemurs) have gliding membranes attached to limbs in colugos (skeletal modifications in colugos with extended rib cage and membranes – innovation).

patterns (e.g. guarding nests, vocal communication with young – innovation). ⏎ • Brain still small relative to body, but efficient; capable of learning simple routines (inherited). | • Very acidic R115: Dinosaurs (non-avian, Mesozoic) | • Increasing brain size in some (e.g. small theropods like Troodon had large brains for their size – innovation), but many large dinos had small brains (inherited limitation). ⏎ • Complex behaviors: herding in hadrosaurs, nesting colonies, and brooding (fossil evidence) – indicates advanced brain functions for social behavior (innovation​en.wikipedia.org】. ⏎ • Balance and coordination refined in bipedal forms via expanded cerebellum (innovation). | • Diverse diets: herbivores developed gastroliths in stomach to grind tough plant material (innovation), and some (hadrosaurs, ceratopsians) evolved dental batteries – tightly packed teeth that self-sharpen for grinding (innovation). ⏎ • Carnivores had serrated teeth to slice flesh (inherited from reptiles, optimized in theropods). ⏎ • No chewing in most – food swallowed in chunks (inherited), but some had cheek-like structures to retain food while chewing (possible innovation in some ornithischians). | • Avian-style air sac system in many saurischians (theropods and sauropods) – hollow bones evidence suggests they had flow-through lungs with air sacs for continuous one-way airflow (innovation​journals.plos.org​journals.plos.org】. ⏎ • High oxygen demand: likely four-phase breathing like birds (innovation), enabling active lifestyles. Others (ornithischians) had less air sac development but still efficient lungs (inherited archosaur trait). ⏎ • Some gigantothermy: large dinos could sustain stable body temps due to size, reducing need for rapid breathing (inherited strategy of ectotherms supplemented by mass homeothermy). | • Four-chambered heart (inferred for all archosaurs, including dinos – likely innovation for high metabolism​fountainmagazine.com】. ⏎ • High blood pressure in tall sauropods to pump blood to heads (innovation: very large hearts and perhaps accessory pumps). ⏎ • Haversian bone structure in fast-growing dinos suggests high metabolic rate supported by vigorous circulation (innovation toward endothermy). | • Egg-laying with hard-shelled eggs (inherited from archosaur ancestors). ⏎ • Elaborate nesting: fossils of brooding (e.g. oviraptorids sitting on nests) show incubation behavior (innovation) – adults regulated egg temperature like birds. ⏎ • Some young altricial (requiring care) – evidence of juveniles in nests fed by parents (innovation), vs others precocial (inherited reptilian style). | • Uricotelic like other reptiles: excreted uric acid paste via kidneys and cloaca (inherited). ⏎ • Salt glands in some (e.g. marine or desert dinos) to excrete excess salt (possible innovation by convergence with birds). ⏎ • Large herbivores had immense gut systems – likely copious urine/feces processed, with water reabsorption in hindgut (inherited reptile trait). |  | • Possible endothermy in some (small theropods) – implying thyroid-driven high metabolism (innovation). ⏎ • Growth hormones: evidence of fast juvenile growth in bone rings suggests intense endocrine regulation (innovation allowing some species to reach adult size quickly). ⏎ • Reproductive hormones cyclical – synchronized mass nesting seasons (inferred, like modern birds). | • Robust immune defenses needed in dense populations – likely good wound healing and innate immunity (inherited). ⏎ • Bone marrow in dinosaurs produced immune cells as in birds (inherited). ⏎ • Gut microbiome likely contributed to immunity in herbivores (inherited concept). | • Feathers or proto-feathers present in many theropods (and even some ornithischians) – initially for insulation or display (innovation), later co-opted for flight in avian lineag​sciencedaily.com】. Feathers also provided camouflage and mating displays. ⏎ • Scales in other dinos (inherited reptilian trait) for body covering; some had both scales and filamentous down-like coverings. ⏎ • Colorful display structures (frills, crests, possibly colored feathers) – used for species recognition and mating displays (innovation in integument for social signaling). | • Erect limb posture – legs positioned under the body (innovation vs sprawling gait), allowing efficient locomotion and gigantic body sizes. ⏎ • Light but strong bones: many bones hollow with internal struts (especially in saurischians) for strength and lightness (innovation, related to air sacs​journals.plos.org】. ⏎ • Specialized joints: extra hip vertebrae support (ornithischian “bird-hip” structure) and flexible necks in sauropods (elongated cervical vertebrae with air cavities – innovation). | • Massive locomotor muscles in hindlimbs of bipedal dinos for running (e.g. thick caudofemoralis muscle in tail powering legs – inherited archosaur trait, enlarged). ⏎ • Shoulder and breast muscles enlarged in some theropods for predation (holding prey) and in others (early fliers like Archaeopteryx) for flapping (incipient flight muscles – innovation). ⏎ • Gigantic herbivores had pillar-like limb muscles emphasizing support over speed (inherited strategy scaled up). | • Sharp color vision – likely tetrachromatic like birds (innovation carried from archosaur lineage) enabling identification of mates and ripe fruits. ⏎ • Acute hearing in some (cochlear lengths in fossils suggest certain theropods heard well, possibly in bird-like frequency range – innovation) while others had poorer hearing (inherited variability). ⏎ • Strong smell: large olfactory bulbs in Tyrannosaurus suggest excellent olfaction for tracking prey (innovation in predators).
R116: Earliest Mammals (Triassic small mammals) | • Expanded cerebral cortex relative to reptiles – improved learning and sensory processing (innovation). ⏎ • Excellent olfaction and hearing centers: nocturnal lifestyle drove a larger olfactory bulb and auditory corte​en.wikipedia.org】 (innovation). ⏎ • Still small brain overall, but brain-to-body ratio increased compared to dinosaurs (innovation trend). | • Heterodont dentition – differentiated teeth (incisors, canines, cheek teeth) for varied diet (innovation vs uniform reptile teeth). They could nip, tear, and chew somewhat, enhancing food processing​en.wikipedia.org】 ⏎ • Lactation appears: infants drank mothers’ milk (major innovation), although early mammals had no nipples – milk secreted onto fur (monotreme-like​en.wikipedia.org】. ⏎ • Likely insectivorous or omnivorous – simple stomach, short gut (inherited from insectivorous ancestors). | • Lungs with diaphragm starting to form – early mammals evolved a muscular diaphragm to pump lungs efficiently (innovation). ⏎ • Turbinate bones in nose to retain moisture and heat during breathing (innovation aiding endothermy). ⏎ • High breathing rates to support warm-blooded metabolism (innovation compared to reptiles). | • Four-chambered heart fully separating circuits (innovation inherited from earlier synapsids, fully utilized for endothermy). ⏎ • High blood pressure and rapid circulation to sustain higher metabolic rate (innovation). ⏎ • Blood with biconcave red blood cells (no nucleus) to carry more oxygen – likely present (innovation aligning with endothermy). | • Viviparity emerges: some early mammals may have birthed live young (proto-therians), but earliest ones (like Morganucodon) likely still laid eggs or had very tiny young (transitional). ⏎ • Milk production to nourish young – mammary glands with nutrient-rich milk (innovation enabling altricial young to survive). ⏎ • Epipubic bones present (as in monotremes/marsupials) restricting pregnancy length; earliest placentals later lost them to allow longer gestatio​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov​pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov】. | • Urea excretion replaces uric acid – early mammals likely shifted to less toxic urea, needing more water but compatible with their nocturnal, moisture-rich environments (innovation vs reptiles​www2.gwu.edu】. ⏎ • Loops of Henle in kidney evolving to concentrate urine moderately (early innovation toward efficient kidneys). ⏎ • Separate anal and urethral openings (ureters to bladder) apart from reproductive tract, though monotreme-like cloaca still possible in earliest forms (transition stage). |  | • Endothermy (warm-bloodedness) – ability to maintain elevated body temperature via high metabolism (major innovation​en.wikipedia.org】. Likely fully evolved by late Triassic (~233 Mya​scientificamerican.com】. ⏎ • Hair follicle glands (sebaceous, apocrine) present, secreting oils for fur and possibly pheromones (innovation). ⏎ • Thyroid & adrenal glands more developed to regulate higher metabolic rate (innovation). | • Improved innate immunity – fur reduces ectoparasites, and higher body temp (≈30–35°C in early mammals) inhibits some infections (innovation). ⏎ • Adaptive immunity advanced: bone marrow and spleen producing diverse lymphocytes (inherited from synapsids, refined). ⏎ • Maternal antibodies possibly transferred via milk, granting pups passive immunity (innovation). | • Fur (hair) for insulation – a new integumentary feature indicating endothermy (major innovation​en.wikipedia.org】. ⏎ • Whiskers (vibrissae) on snout – tactile sensors in the fur, evidence of which seen in small Triassic mammals (innovation to navigate in dark​en.wikipedia.org】. ⏎ • Mammary hairs – milk secreted onto patches of into middle ear ossicles (malleus, incus) – a hallmark mammalian innovation improving hearin​en.wikipedia.org】. ⏎ • Three middle ear bones fully detached from jaw, increasing hearing sensitivity (innovation​en.wikipedia.org】. ⏎ • Seven neck vertebrae (inherited constraint of mammals) and flexible backbone for agility (innovation: more dorso-ventral flexion for running). | • Diaphragm muscle developed, separating chest and abdomen – greatly improves breathing efficiency (innovation). ⏎ • Differentiated facial muscles – allowed suckling (lips forming seal to nurse, an innovation in feeding young). ⏎ • Postural muscles adapted for sustained activity – early mammals could sustain exercise to forage at night (innovation, ties to endothermy). | • Highly sensitive hearing – three-ossicle
