R180: Laurasiatheria (e.g. ungulates, bats, cats) | • Highly diverse brains – e.g. carnivores have advanced social and hunting circuits (pack coordination in canines – innovation), bats evolved echo-processing centers in auditory cortex (innovation), shrews have rapid reflex networks for high metabolism (inherited small-mammal trait). ⏎ • Generally capable of complex behaviors: social structures in carnivorans, long-distance navigation in bats (magnetic sensing), etc. (varied innovations). | • Extremes of diet: ruminant herbivores (cows) have multi-chambered stomachs with microbial fermentation (innovation), hindgut fermenters (horses) have enlarged cecum (innovation), carnivores have short simple guts and carnassial teeth (inherited optimized for meat). ⏎ • Bats mostly insectivorous with fast digestion for flight weight (innovation: rapid processing). Cetaceans (whales, in this clade) lack a rumen but have compartmented stomach for fish/crustaceans (adapted). | • All have diaphragm and high lung capacity (inherited mammal trait). Special cases: bats – extremely high oxygen demand; have high alveolar surface area and can ventilate rapidly during flight (innovation), whales – lungs and chest collapse safely under pressure, rapid gas exchange at surface (innovation). ⏎ • Many can thermoregulate via panting (dogs) or sweating (horses) to cool during exertion – respiratory evaporation (inherited, optimized). | • 4-chambered heart (inherited) with lineage-specific tweaks: giraffes (ungulate) have very high blood pressure and vessel valves to manage blood to head (innovation), whales have bradycardia diving reflex (heart slows, peripheral circulation cut to conserve oxygen – innovation). ⏎ • Some small laurasiatheres (bats, hedgehogs) enter torpor/hibernation, dramatically lowering heart rate (innovation for energy conservation). | • Placental viviparity (inherited) with full internal development. Ungulates often have diffuse or cotyledonary placentas, carnivores zonary placentas – all efficient nutrient transfer (variations as innovations). ⏎ • Parental care: extended in carnivores (packs raise young, innovation in social care), minimal in some hoofed animals (precocial young can run soon after birth, inherited strategy). ⏎ • Frequency of reproduction varies: rodents (in sister Euarchontoglires) are high, large laurasiatheres lower – overall, flexible strategies. | • Urea excretion via advanced kidneys (inherited). Many desert laurasiatheres (e.g. camels in ungulates) have extraordinary kidney concentrating ability and can tolerate dehydration (innovation – camels’ urine/feces extremely dry). ⏎ • Cetaceans have large, lobed kidneys to excrete salt from ingesting seawater (innovation). Bats may void nitrogen waste mid-flight to reduce weight (unique behavior). ⏎ • No cloaca – separate rectum and urethra in all (inherited placental trait). |  | • Thyroid-driven high metabolism in small insectivores (shrews have to eat constantly, high T3/T4 – inherited endothermy at extreme). ⏎ • Seasonal breeding common: many temperate bats have delayed fertilization or implantation timed with spring (innovation); ungulates go into rut (mating season) regulated by photoperiod (innovation). ⏎ • Diverse endocrine specializations: e.g. bears (carnivoran) hibernate with metabolic downregulation (hormonal control of torpor – innovation). | • Strong immune systems typical of placentals (inherited). Certain laurasiatheres show unique aspects: bats harbor many viruses with minimal illness (dampened inflammatory response – innovation), hedgehogs have quill microbiomes that may kill bacteria (innovation by symbiosis). ⏎ • Ungulates often have high parasite loads; evolved robust gut-associated lymphoid tissue to handle it (inherited adaptation). | • Fur/hair (inherited): huge variety – e.g. thick insulating fur in arctic fox (innovation for cold), virtually no hair in whales (secondarily reduced for streamlining, innovation). ⏎ • Special structures: horns in bovids (keratin covering bone, not shed, innovation), antlers in deer (entirely bone, shed annually, innovation), baleen plates in toothless whales (keratin sheets filter plankton – innovation replacing teeth). ⏎ • Sweat glands well-developed in horses (for cooling) and minimal in others (dogs rely on panting – diversity in integumentary cooling strategies). | • Great range: from light, hollow bird-like bones in bats (for flight, innovation) to heavy dense bones in whales (for buoyancy control, innovation). ⏎ • Leg modifications: running animals (horses) have elongated limb bones and fused wrist/ankle elements for stability (innovation), bats have wing membrane stretched over very long finger bones (major skeletal innovation for powered flight). ⏎ • Dentition and jaw vary: e.g. in carnivores, enlarged canines and reinforced skulls for bite force (innovation), in some whales, teeth lost entirely for baleen (skeletal/dental innovation). | • Specialized locomotor muscles: e.g. ungulates have springy leg tendons and high-endurance muscles for running (innovation), bats have modified chest and back muscles to power flight (and a tendon locking mechanism to hang inverted without effort – innovation). ⏎ • Carnivores have powerful jaw and neck muscles for subduing prey (inherited and amplified). Moles (insectivores in this clade) have oversized digging muscles in forelimbs (innovation for fossorial life). | • Keen senses in predators – e.g. wolves have excellent smell and hearing (inherited, refined), bats navigate by echolocation (ultrasonic hearing and sound production – major innovation), many ungulates have panoramic vision and good motion detection but weaker acuity (inherited prey trait). ⏎ • Magnetoreception suspected in some migratory bats (innovation) and cetaceans for long migrations. Echolocation also evolved in toothed whales (sonar via melon organ – innovation parallel to bats). ⏎ • Vibrissae (whiskers) in many (cats, shrews, etc.) for tactile sensing (inherited from early mammals, well-developed).
R181: Euarchontoglires (e.g. primates, rodents) | • Primates: large brains, especially expanded cerebral cortex for reasoning and social behavior (innovation culminating in great apes/humans). Fine motor control (tool use in some) and color vision (in higher primates) enhanc6】. ⏎ • Rodents/Lagomorphs: smaller brains but excellent olfaction and tactile processing (whisker barrels in cortex – innovation). Some (e.g. capuchin monkeys, dolphins in sister clade) exhibit high intelligence and learning (diverse innovations). | • Primates: omnivorous, generalized teeth (incisors, canines, premolars, molars); some evolved specializations – e.g. colobine monkeys have foregut fermentation for leaves (innovation), while others have simple gut but very long intestines for fruit (inherited). Coprophagy in some (rodents, rabbits re-eat feces for nutrients – innovation in excretion/digestion overlap). ⏎ • Rodents: continuously growing incisors (major innovation) for gnawing; many have cheek pouches for food carrying (innovation). Ruminant-like fermentation in some rodents (hindgut cecum) to digest cellulose (innovation). | • Standard mammalian lungs/diaphragm (inherited). ⏎ • Many small species (mice, treeshrews) have extremely high respiratory rates to fuel fast metabolism (inherited). Some primates (gibbons) have throat air sacs to amplify calls (innovation in respiratory anatomy for communication). ⏎ • Many can thermoregulate well – e.g. humans sweat for cooling (primates’ integument interacting with respiration for evaporative cooling – innovation). | • 4-chambered heart (inherited) with typical high endurance capacity in many (e.g. migratory bats, long-distance primate foragers). ⏎ • Rodents: small size, very fast heart rates; can sustain high blood flow for their size (inherited trait scaled up). ⏎ • Primates: some (apes) have higher blood pressure to maintain brain flow upright (adaptation). Some hibernators in this clade (dwarf lemurs) store fat and drop heart rate in torpor (innovation in primates). | • Placental viviparity (inherited). Primates generally have longer gestation and fewer offspring (1 at a time) with extended upbringing (innovation – K-selection). Rodents opposite: short gestation, large litters, rapid maturity (innovation – r-selection). ⏎ • Social breeding: some primates have complex mating systems (harems, pair bonds, etc., regulated by social cues and hormones – innovation), some rodents (mole rats) even eusocial with a single breeding queen (extreme innovation). | • Urea excretion (inherited) – efficient kidneys; e.g. desert rodents (kangaroo rat) produce highly concentrated urine (innovation: some of the longest Henle’s loops of any mammal). ⏎ • Primates: moderate concentrating ability; apes/humans actually less adept at concentrating urine than desert mammals (inherited trait in lush environment). ⏎ • Defecation frequency: e.g. rabbits produce cecotropes (special nutrient-rich droppings) and re-ingest them (excretory/ingestive adaptation). |  | • Primates: complex endocrine cycles – e.g. menstrual cycle in Old World monkeys/apes (innovation, endometrium sheds), concealed ovulation in humans (innovation in mating strategy). High cortisol responses to social stress (inherited mammal trait). ⏎ • Rodents: often induced ovulation (ovulate after mating stimulus – innovation for efficiency). They can breed prolifically; hormones allow postpartum estrus (mate right after giving birth – innovation maximizing reproduction). | • Primates: generally strong immune systems; long-lived ones (humans) have slower thymus involution (somewhat longer immune youth). Some (chimpanzees) can get human diseases – similar immunity (inherited). ⏎ • Rodents: immune tolerance is high – can live in large colonies with disease exposure. Lab mice (in this clade) have been key to understanding adaptive immunity; their immune system is prototypical mammal (inherited). Some rodents evolved cancer resistance (naked mole rat with unique hyaluronan, though that is a different order in Euarchontoglires – innovation). | • Primates: most have nails instead of claws (innovation aiding touch and manipulation), sensitive pads with fingerprints (innovation). Dense hair except in humans (who have sparse hair, sweat glands increased – unique hominin integument innovation). ⏎ • Rodents: fur often specialized (e.g. whiskers, stripes for camouflage in chipmunks). Some have dermal armor bits (hair strengthened into quills in porcupines – innovation like hedgehogs via convergence). ⏎ • Many adapt pelage color seasonally (e.g. snowshoe hare white in winter – innovation for camouflage). | • Primates: flexible skeleton, mobile shoulder (especially apes – innovation: capable of brachiation), grasping hands/feet with opposable thumbs or big toes (innovation). Upright walking evolved in hominins (bipedal pelvis, spine S-curve – major innovation). ⏎ • Rodents: generally small and light skeleton, some with gliding membranes (flying squirrels – extended rib/limb skin, innovation convergent with bats). Lagomorphs (rabbits) have extra peg teeth and strong hind legs for jumping (inherited and refined). | • Primates: well-developed facial muscles for expression (innovation for social communication), fine finger muscles for dexterity (inherited but refined in apes). Apes also have strong arms for climbing/hanging (inherited adaptation). ⏎ • Rodents: powerful jaw muscles for gnawing (massive masseter muscle relative to size – innovation), and in some (kangaroo rats) huge hindlimb muscles for jumping (innovation). ⏎ • Primates (some) and rodents can sustain long activities (arboreal leaping in lemurs, burrowing in gophers) – muscles tuned for endurance or bursts accordingly (diverse, inherited). | • Primates: trichromatic color vision in Old World monkeys and apes (innovation from ancestral dichromacy) – better fruit/leaf discrimination. Depth perception from forward eyes (inherited from early primates). Highly social species evolved keen perception of expressions (cognitive-sensory link). ⏎ • Rodents: often poor vision but excellent smell and hearing (inherited nocturnal trait). Many are ultrasonic communicators (mice pups call ultrasonically, bats in sister clade use ultrasound, innovation in communication). Whisker touch is extremely refined – they can navigate mazes by touch (innovation in mechanosensation).
